# Descartes, Discourse on Method and Essays (AT VI) — Commentary

References are to Adam–Tannery vol. VI by page: AT 1–485 for the French text of 1637, AT 540–720 for the Latin version of 1644 (Étienne de Courcelles, revised by Descartes; the Geometry has no Latin text in this volume). The Latin is brought in only where it changes the sense in a way that bears on the argument. Four misreadings are guarded against throughout. The Discourse is the preface to the Essays and presents a method practised in them, not a treatise of logic; the four precepts mean nothing apart from that practice. Its doubt is an instrument for finding something certain, not a sceptical position. "I think, therefore I am" is not a syllogism with a suppressed major premise. The beast-machine denies thought to animals, not life or bodily feeling.

## Discourse on the Method for Rightly Conducting One's Reason and Seeking Truth in the Sciences

(AT 1) The prefatory notice divides the discourse into six parts and lists their contents in the third person, as the work of "the Author" of an anonymous book. The sequence it gives — remarks on the sciences, the method, a morality drawn from the method, the metaphysics, the order of physical questions with the heart, medicine and the animal soul, and finally the conditions of further research — already traces the tree of philosophy that the 1647 preface to the Principles makes explicit, with metaphysics as root and medicine and morals as branches. Two phrases are exact. The method is one the author "sought," not one he received; and the proofs of God and of the soul are called the "foundations" of the metaphysics, so the cogito appears in the summary only as a means to them. The title's verb, to conduct (conduire) one's reason, sets the governing image of the whole text: reason as a traveller who needs a road, a pace and a direction. The Latin title, recte utendi ratione, loses that image, and the Latin drops the notice altogether in favour of marginal headings.

### Part One

(AT 1–2) Descartes opens with a joke that is also a thesis. Good sense is the best-shared thing in the world, since nobody wants more of it than he has. The joke's evidence is weak, but the conclusion he draws from it is serious: the power of judging well and of telling true from false, which is good sense or reason, is naturally equal in all. Differences of opinion then cannot come from unequal endowment. They come from leading our thoughts by different roads and attending to different things. Having a good mind is not enough; what matters is applying it well, and the slow walker on the straight road outruns the fast one who strays. The equality thesis moves the whole difference between minds from nature to use, and so makes a method both possible and necessary. The Latin renders good sense as bona mens seu recta ratio, the phrase that opens the Regulae ("bona mens, sive haec universalis Sapientia"), and calls it innate; it ties the Discourse's starting point explicitly to the earlier project of a single human wisdom underlying all the sciences.

(AT 2–3) Descartes denies that his own mind is better than the common run. He has often wished for quicker thought, a clearer imagination, a fuller or readier memory, and these alone perfect a mind. Reason itself, being what makes us men and sets us apart from beasts, is whole in each of us, and here he follows the scholastic rule that more and less occur only among accidents, never among the forms or natures of individuals of one species. The modesty separates two things the Regulae had already separated: the auxiliary powers of speed, imagination and memory, which vary and which method can train or supplement, and reason, which does not vary. The scholastic doctrine he invokes is borrowed with irony, since his physics will abolish substantial forms; the Latin names them as such (formas substantiales). Yet he keeps one form, the human one, and Part Five will need exactly this all-or-nothing view of reason to deny it to animals altogether.

(AT 3) He claims to have hit, from youth, on paths that led to considerations and maxims out of which he formed a method. It lets him increase his knowledge by degrees, up to the highest point his mind and short life permit. He leans toward self-distrust and finds nearly every human undertaking vain, yet the progress he has made gives him extreme satisfaction, and he dares to think that if any occupation of men "purely as men" is solidly good, it is his. Two features define the method from the start: it is cumulative, rising by degrees rather than by illumination, and it is bounded by the capacity of the individual mind. The restriction to men "purely as men" sets aside the religious calling, which he will place beyond human reason a few pages later. The claim to have chosen the best occupation anticipates the conclusion of the provisional morality (AT 27), where the choice is made again on principle.

(AT 3–4) He may be wrong: what he takes for gold and diamonds may be copper and glass, and our judgments about ourselves, like our friends' judgments in our favour, deserve suspicion. He will therefore show the roads he took and portray his life as in a painting, so that everyone can judge it, and the public verdict will become one more means of instruction. Publication is framed as an experiment that solicits correction, and this frame governs Part Six's request that objections be sent to the printer (AT 75) and later the whole apparatus of Objections and Replies. The Latin adds that he will listen "hidden behind the picture," the posture Pliny ascribes to Apelles. The addition suits an author who publishes anonymously and wants criticism without exposure.

(AT 4) His design is not to teach the method everyone must follow but to show how he tried to conduct his own reason. Whoever gives precepts sets himself above those he instructs and is to blame for the least fault. He offers the work as a history or a fable, containing some examples worth imitating and others better not followed, and hopes it will help some and harm none. The disclaimer is a strategy, not false modesty. To Mersenne (March 1637) he explained that he wrote "Discourse" rather than "Treatise" because method consists more in practice than in theory, and practice can be shown but not prescribed. The word "fable" also anticipates Part Five, where the physics is presented as the fable of a new world. Among the examples not to imitate is the very resolution to discard all one's opinions (AT 15).

(AT 4–5) Raised on letters and promised by them clear and assured knowledge of everything useful to life, he burned to learn them. At the end of the course he changed his mind entirely: he found himself caught in so many doubts and errors that his only gain seemed to be the discovery of his ignorance. Yet he had studied at one of the most celebrated schools in Europe, learned what the others learned, read every rare and curious book that came his way, was not judged inferior to fellow students destined to become teachers, and lived in an age as rich in good minds as any. He concluded that no doctrine in the world was what he had been led to hope. The argument works by elimination: the school, the student and the century are each cleared of blame, so the failure lies in the doctrine itself. The school's promise, clear and assured knowledge useful to life, is the very promise Part Six makes for his own practical philosophy (AT 61–62). He keeps the goal and replaces the means.

(AT 5–6) He still valued the school's exercises. Languages open ancient books; fables wake the mind; histories raise it and form judgment; good books are a conversation with the best minds of past ages, and a studied one; eloquence and poetry have incomparable powers; mathematics has subtle inventions that serve the curious and ease the arts; moral writings exhort to virtue; theology teaches the way to heaven; philosophy lets one speak plausibly of everything and win the admiration of the less learned; law and medicine bring honours and wealth; and even the superstitious arts are worth examining to know their value. Each discipline is credited with an effect, never with truth. The praise of philosophy is the indictment, since it is valued for plausibility, and he is about to treat the merely plausible as nearly false (AT 8). Mathematics appears only as a source of useful inventions; its true use is still to be found.

(AT 6–7) He had given enough time to languages and to ancient books with their histories and fables. Conversing with other ages is like travelling: it helps us judge our own customs more soundly, but too much travel makes one a foreigner at home, and too much curiosity about the past leaves one ignorant of the present. Fables make impossible events seem possible. Even faithful histories leave out the lowly circumstances, so the rest looks other than it was, and those who model their conduct on them fall into the extravagances of romance knights. The objection is practical: histories and fables misguide action because action depends on exact circumstances, which these genres distort. Descartes has just called his own book a history and a fable, so he owes his reader the plain circumstances, and he gives them, down to the heated room of Part Two. The travel analogy becomes literal at AT 9, where he swaps books for the world.

(AT 7) He prized eloquence and loved poetry, but held both to be gifts of mind rather than fruits of study. Those who reason most strongly and order their thoughts best, so as to make them clear and intelligible, will always persuade best, even speaking only Low Breton and knowing no rhetoric; those with the most pleasing inventions, expressed with most grace, will be the best poets without knowing the art of poetry. Persuasion is reduced to clarity and order of thought, so that rhetoric becomes a by-product of method. The verb for ordering thoughts, to digest them, matches the third precept's ordering of thoughts (AT 18). The claim also licenses the Discourse's own manner: written in French for those who use their natural reason (AT 77), it counts on order rather than art to persuade.

(AT 7–8) Mathematics pleased him most for the certainty and evidence of its reasonings, but he had not yet seen its true use. Thinking it served only the mechanical arts, he was surprised that nothing loftier had been built on foundations so firm. By contrast he compared the moral writings of the ancient pagans to magnificent palaces built on sand and mud: they exalt the virtues but do not teach how to recognise them, and what they honour with the name is often insensibility, pride, despair or parricide. The two halves of the image complement each other: mathematics is a foundation with no building, ancient ethics a building with no foundation. The programme follows: build on mathematical foundations everything, including morals, as the "highest and most perfect morality" of the Principles preface proposes. The four counterfeit virtues have recognisable owners — Stoic impassivity, the sage's self-sufficiency, Cato's suicide, Brutus's murder of Caesar. Part Three will nonetheless adopt the Stoic discipline of desire, so the target is virtue praised without knowledge of what it is, not the Stoic practice itself.

(AT 8) He revered theology and hoped for heaven as much as anyone. But he had learned that the way there is open to the ignorant as much as to the learned, and that revealed truths exceed our understanding. He would not dare submit them to his weak reasoning, and thought that to examine them successfully one would need extraordinary help from heaven and to be more than a man. Theology is set aside on two grounds, neither of them doubt. Salvation does not depend on learning, so there is no practical need; revealed truths exceed reason, so there is no competence. The exclusion concerns revealed theology only. Part Four proves God's existence by reason, and Descartes always kept natural theology within philosophy while leaving the mysteries to faith (cf. Principles I.25).

(AT 8) Of philosophy he says only this: cultivated for centuries by the best minds, it still contains nothing that is not disputed, and so nothing that is not doubtful. He lacked the presumption to hope he would do better. And since there can be many opinions on one matter defended by learned men, of which at most one is true, he held as almost false whatever was only probable. The inference from dispute to doubt assumes that evident truth compels the assent of anyone who grasps it, so that persistent disagreement proves the absence of evidence on both sides; Regulae II makes the same argument. The rule "almost false whatever is only probable" is the seed of the first precept and of the doubt of Part Four, where "almost" becomes "absolutely" (AT 31).

(AT 8–9) The other sciences borrow their principles from philosophy, so nothing solid can have been built on such foundations. Honour and gain did not tempt him: by God's grace his circumstances did not force him to make a trade of learning, and while he did not scorn glory like a Cynic, he cared little for glory he could only win under false pretences. As for the bad doctrines, he knew them well enough not to be deceived by alchemists, astrologers, magicians or any who profess to know more than they do. The dependence of the particular sciences on philosophy is the structural thesis that decides where reform must begin (AT 21–22). His independent means is not a biographical aside. It frees inquiry from the market, and Part Six returns to the material conditions of research — the cost of experiments, the refusal of favours.

(AT 9–10) As soon as age freed him from his teachers, he left the study of letters and resolved to seek no knowledge but what he could find in himself or in the great book of the world. He spent the rest of his youth travelling, seeing courts and armies, mixing with people of every temper and rank, gathering experiences, testing himself in the encounters fortune offered, and reflecting on everything for profit. He expected more truth from the reasonings men make about their own affairs, where the outcome soon punishes bad judgment, than from a scholar's speculations in his study, which have no consequences except vanity — vanity that grows the further they stray from common sense, since more ingenuity was needed to make them plausible. He always wanted to learn to tell true from false, so as to see clearly in his actions and walk with assurance in this life. The criterion of practical reasoning, correction by consequences, is an early form of the test by experience. The motive he gives is practical from the outset: theoretical clarity is sought for the sake of conduct, which explains why Part Three must supply a morality before inquiry can proceed.

(AT 10–11) Observing the customs of other men gave him as little assurance as the opinions of philosophers had, since he found just as much diversity. Its chief profit was negative: seeing many things accepted by great peoples that seem absurd to us, he learned to believe nothing too firmly that example and custom alone had persuaded him of, and so freed himself gradually from errors that darken the natural light. After some years he resolved to study within himself as well, and to use all his powers to choose the roads he should follow, which succeeded better than if he had never left his country or his books. The book of the world reproduces the disagreement of the schools, so it cannot supply truth; it can only discredit custom as a ground of belief. Custom and example are named as a source of error here and will be named again at AT 16. The turn inward is the hinge between the critique of the received sciences and the founding of the method.

### Part Two

(AT 11–13) He was in Germany, drawn by wars not yet over, returning from the emperor's coronation (Frankfurt, 1619), when winter held him in quarters. With no company and no cares, he stayed all day alone in a stove-heated room (poêle), free to converse with his thoughts. One of the first was that works assembled from many pieces by many hands are seldom as perfect as works by one. Buildings planned and finished by one architect are handsomer than those patched from old walls; ancient towns grown out of villages are ill-proportioned beside towns an engineer lays out on a plain, though their single houses may show as much art; peoples civilised gradually, who made laws as crimes forced them to, are worse governed than those that kept the constitution of a prudent legislator; the true religion, whose ordinances God alone made, is incomparably better ordered than all others; Sparta flourished not because each law was good, for some were strange, but because one man made them and they tended to one end. So too the book-sciences, at least those with merely probable reasons, compiled from many opinions, come less near the truth than the simple reasonings of a man of good sense. And because we were children before we were men, governed by appetites and tutors who often contradicted one another, our judgments cannot be as pure as if we had used reason fully from birth. Perfection here is a property of order — unity of design and end — not of the quality of parts. The argument is restricted to probable sciences: a demonstration is not weakened by having several authors. The examples rise from houses to towns, states, religion, the sciences and finally the single mind, which is the only one of them he will reform. The argument from childhood introduces the theory of prejudice (Principles I.71): our first judgments were made before reason had its full use.

(AT 13–14) Nobody pulls down every house in a town just to rebuild it differently, but individuals do rebuild their own, and are sometimes forced to when the foundations are unsound. On this model he concluded that a private person has no business reforming a state from its foundations, or even the body of the sciences or the order of teaching in the schools. For his own opinions, though, he could do no better than remove them all at once and then put back better ones, or the same ones once adjusted to the level of reason. Public reform is another matter. Great bodies are hard to raise once thrown down, or even to hold steady once shaken, and their fall is violent; their imperfections, which their mere diversity proves exist, have been softened by use, which also corrects many faults that prudence could not foresee; and they are nearly always more bearable than their change would be, as mountain roads worn smooth by traffic are better followed than a straighter path over rocks and precipices. The same structural reasoning that recommends total demolition for one mind forbids it for public bodies, because institutions store corrections accumulated by use that no single prudence can reproduce. The phrase "or the same ones" matters: the overthrow is a test, not a verdict on content, and an opinion may return once examined. The Latin's "omnes simul et semel" uses the formula with which the First Meditation announces the same resolution.

(AT 14–15) He cannot approve the restless tempers who, called to public affairs neither by birth nor by fortune, keep devising reforms in their heads; if anything in this writing could make him suspected of that folly, he would be sorry to have it published. His design never went further than reforming his own thoughts and building on ground wholly his own. If he shows the model of a work that pleased him, he does not advise anyone to copy it; those God has endowed more richly may have loftier designs, but he fears his own is already too bold for many. The resolution to rid oneself of all opinions previously accepted is not an example everyone should follow. The world consists largely of two kinds of mind unsuited to it: those who think themselves abler than they are, judge hastily and lack the patience to order their thoughts, and who, once off the common road, would wander all their lives; and those modest enough to see that others can instruct them, who should follow those others rather than search for themselves. The political disclaimer shields a radical epistemic act by confining it to private ground. The first kind of mind displays precipitation, the vice the first precept forbids; the second exercises a legitimate deference. Method therefore requires self-reliance and patience at once, and few people have both.

(AT 16) He would have belonged to the second kind had he had only one master or not known how the learned have always disagreed. But he learned at school that nothing is too strange or incredible to have been said by some philosopher. Travelling showed him that those with views opposed to ours are not therefore barbarians, many using reason as well as we do or better. The same man with the same mind becomes different raised among French or Germans than among Chinese or cannibals, and the fashion we liked ten years ago, and may like again, now looks absurd. So custom and example persuade us far more than certain knowledge does; and a majority proves nothing for truths hard to find, since one man is likelier to find them than a whole people. Unable to choose anyone whose opinions should be preferred, he found himself constrained to guide himself. His autonomy is forced on him: authority fails because the authorities conflict. Cultural diversity serves to discredit custom as a ground of belief, not to support relativism. The reversal of the majority principle for hard truths grounds his solitary inquiry and connects back to the single architect of AT 11.

(AT 16–17) Like a man walking alone in the dark, he resolved to go so slowly and so carefully that if he advanced little he would at least not fall. He would not begin rejecting the opinions that had slipped into his belief without reason until he had spent enough time planning the work and seeking the true method for reaching knowledge of everything his mind could grasp. The order is decisive for the whole book: the method comes first, then the provisional morality, and only then the doubt. Doubt in the Discourse is an application of a method already in hand, not the starting point of inquiry. The goal stated, knowledge of all things the mind is capable of, recalls the Regulae's demand to determine the limits of human knowledge (Rule VIII).

(AT 17–18) In his youth he had studied logic, the analysis of the geometers and algebra, three arts that seemed likely to help. Logic's syllogisms and most of its other precepts serve to explain to others what one already knows, or, like Lull's art, to talk without judgment about what one does not, rather than to learn; and its good precepts are so mixed with harmful or superfluous ones that separating them is as hard as drawing a Diana or a Minerva from an unhewn block. The analysis of the ancients and the algebra of the moderns deal only with abstract matters of no apparent use; the former is so tied to figures that it cannot exercise the understanding without tiring the imagination, while the latter is so bound to rules and ciphers that it has become a confused and obscure art that troubles the mind instead of a science that cultivates it. He therefore sought another method that would keep the advantages of the three and none of their defects. And as a state with few laws strictly observed is better ruled than one with many, he judged that four precepts would suffice, provided he made a firm and constant resolution never once to fail to observe them. The criterion that condemns logic is invention against exposition: syllogistic presents what is known and discovers nothing (cf. Regulae X). Geometry and algebra are faulted not for falsity but for being tied to particular materials — figures, numbers, notations — so what he wants from them is their form freed from that matter, the general science of order and measure of Regulae IV. Geometry Book I carries out the repair on its own ground. The closing condition shows that the method is a discipline of the will as well as of the understanding: four rules suffice only if one resolves never to break them.

(AT 18) The first precept: never accept anything as true that he did not know evidently to be so; carefully avoid precipitation and prejudice (prévention); and include nothing more in his judgments than what presented itself so clearly and distinctly to his mind that he had no occasion to doubt it. The rule governs judgment, not perception, and locates error in judgment exceeding what is presented — the theory of the Fourth Meditation in germ, where will outruns understanding. Precipitation is the haste of judging before evidence; prejudice is the weight of earlier judgments, chiefly those of childhood (AT 13). "Clearly and distinctly" appears here without justification. Part Four will extract it as a rule from the cogito (AT 33) and then ground it in God (AT 38). The Latin requires that the thing be known "certainly and evidently" to be true and presented "to my reason," which restates Regulae II's definition of knowledge as certain and evident cognition.

(AT 18) The second precept: divide each difficulty under examination into as many parts as possible and as needed to resolve it better. The qualifier sets the limit. Division is relative to the solution sought, not an atomisation pursued for its own sake. Leibniz mocked the rule as saying no more than "take what you must, do as you should," but its content comes from the third precept, which supplies the notion of the simple at which division should stop — the simple natures of Regulae VI and XII.

(AT 18–19) The third precept: conduct his thoughts in order, beginning with the simplest objects and the easiest to know, and rising by degrees to knowledge of the most composite; and even supposing an order among those that do not naturally precede one another. The order is epistemic, ranked by ease of knowledge, not by rank of being. The final clause is the most original: where no natural order exists the mind imposes one, as Regulae X recommends with weaving and deciphering, and as the Geometry does when it ranks curves by the degree of their equations.

(AT 19) The fourth precept: make enumerations so complete and reviews so general as to be sure of omitting nothing. Two operations are named. Enumeration inventories the cases or steps; review runs through the chain quickly enough that memory's weakness does not break it (Regulae VII and XI). The Latin specifies the scope: complete enumeration "both in seeking the middle terms and in going through the parts of the difficulties." That addition makes the fourth precept the check on the other three — on the continuity of deduction and on the division of the problem — rather than a separate counsel of thoroughness.

(AT 19–20) The long chains of simple reasonings geometers use to reach their hardest demonstrations led him to suppose that all things within human knowledge follow from one another in the same way. Provided we accept nothing false as true and keep the order needed to deduce one from another, nothing is so remote that it cannot be reached or so hidden that it cannot be found. He knew where to begin: with the simplest. Since only mathematicians had found demonstrations, he would start with what they had examined, expecting no profit except that his mind would grow used to feeding on truths and refusing false reasons. He did not mean to learn every branch of mathematics. Since all branches consider only relations or proportions, he would examine proportions in general, supposing them only in subjects that made them easier to know and binding them to none, so as to apply them later to everything they fit. To consider proportions singly he would represent them by lines, the simplest thing he could picture to imagination and the senses; to retain or grasp many together he would express them by the shortest possible symbols. So he would borrow the best of geometrical analysis and algebra and correct each by the other. The universality of the method rests on an unproved thesis, drawn by analogy from geometry: that everything knowable is linked in deductive chains — the unity of the sciences of Regulae I. Mathematics is valued here as training, not as an end. The French leaves a gap in the inference from the mathematicians' success to where one should begin; the Latin fills it, saying he understood that they had dealt with "the easiest thing of all." The division of labour between lines and letters founds the Geometry: lines present magnitudes to imagination, letters spare memory.

(AT 20–21) He dares to say that exact observance of these few precepts gave him such ease in disentangling the questions of geometry and algebra that in two or three months, beginning with the simplest and most general, each truth found becoming a rule for finding others, he solved many he had once thought very hard, and could even determine, for those he did not know, by what means and how far they could be solved. This is not vanity. Each thing has only one truth, so whoever finds it knows as much as can be known about it: a child who has added correctly by the rules knows everything the human mind could find about that sum. The method, which teaches true order and exact enumeration of the circumstances of what is sought, contains everything that gives certainty to the rules of arithmetic. The claim to fix in advance how far a problem can be solved is the classification of problems — plane, solid and beyond — in Geometry Books II and III. The child example removes any hint of boast: completeness is relative to the question asked, and certainty has no degrees. The method generalises arithmetic's certainty by locating its source in order and enumeration rather than in number.

(AT 21–22) What pleased him most in the method was the assurance of using his reason in everything, if not perfectly, then as well as he could. He also felt his mind growing used to conceiving its objects more sharply and distinctly, and since he had tied the method to no particular subject, he hoped to apply it as usefully to other sciences as to algebra. He did not attempt all of them at once, which would have broken the order the method prescribes. Their principles all had to be borrowed from philosophy, where he found none certain, so he must first try to establish some there. That being the most important task of all, and the one where precipitation and prejudice were most to be feared, he should not attempt it before an age far riper than his twenty-three years, and only after long preparation: uprooting bad opinions, gathering experiences as material for later reasoning, and practising the method until he was confirmed in it. The method's first fruit is a change in the mind itself, a habituation, and it is a discipline before it is a doctrine. Its order also governs its own use: it cannot be applied to the principles of philosophy until the mind has been cleared and trained. The dating sets the interval Part Three fills: from the winter of 1619 to the metaphysical beginning nine years later.

### Part Three

(AT 22) Before rebuilding the house one lives in, it is not enough to demolish it, gather materials and architects or learn architecture oneself, and draw the plan carefully; one also needs another house to live in comfortably while the work goes on. So, lest he stay irresolute in his actions while reason obliged him to be irresolute in his judgments, and so that he might live as happily as possible from then on, he formed a provisional morality (morale par provision) of three or four maxims. The provisional morality solves a problem the method itself creates. Doubt suspends judgment, but action cannot wait. Because the maxims are not derived from evident knowledge, they cannot be the definitive ethics the tree of philosophy places at the end. "Par provision" is a legal phrase for an interim measure pending final judgment. The notice called this morality "drawn from" the method, and the phrase holds: the morality is the method applied to a domain where evidence is not yet available. The count of "three or four" is exact, since the fourth is not a maxim but the choice of occupation that grounds the other three (AT 27).

(AT 22–24) The first maxim was to obey the laws and customs of his country, keeping the religion in which God had given him the grace to be raised, and governing himself in all else by the most moderate opinions, farthest from excess, commonly practised by the most sensible of those he would live among. Since he now counted his own opinions as nothing, he could do no better than follow theirs; there may be people as sensible among the Persians or Chinese, but it was most useful to follow those he lived with. To learn their real opinions he watched what they did rather than what they said, both because corrupt manners make few willing to say what they believe and because many do not know it themselves: the act by which one believes something differs from the act by which one knows one believes it, and the one often occurs without the other. Among equally received opinions he chose the most moderate, as easiest to practise and probably best, excess being usually bad, and because a mistake would take him less far from the right road. He counted among excesses every promise that cuts off part of one's freedom — not disapproving laws that permit vows or binding contracts for weak minds or for the security of trade, but because nothing in the world stays the same, and he meant to improve his judgments; binding himself to keep holding something good after it had ceased to be so, or he had ceased to think it so, would be a fault against good sense. Conformity is chosen on grounds of utility, not because custom is true, so the maxim remains consistent with the critique of custom in Parts One and Two. The distinction between believing and knowing that one believes is a significant early text: it allows mental acts to occur without second-order awareness, which complicates the later definition of thought as everything of which we are immediately conscious (Principles I.9). The Latin narrows it to the act of judging something good or bad, a practical judgment, which fits the maxim's subject better. The Latin also has him keep "the religion I judged best" in which he was raised, making adherence a judgment rather than an inheritance. The refusal of vows keeps the morality revisable, and so provisional, by design.

(AT 24–25) The second maxim was to be as firm and resolute in action as he could, and to follow the most doubtful opinions, once decided on, as constantly as if they were certain. Travellers lost in a forest should not wander this way and that, still less stop, but walk as straight as they can in one direction and not change it for weak reasons, even if chance alone first chose it: they will at least end up somewhere better than the middle of the forest. Since the actions of life often allow no delay, it is a very certain truth that when we cannot discern the truest opinions we must follow the most probable; and even when none seems more probable, we must settle on some and afterwards regard them, as far as practice is concerned, not as doubtful but as very true and certain, because the reason that settled us is so. This freed him from the regrets and remorse that trouble weak minds, which act on things as good and then judge them bad. The certainty transferred to the chosen opinion is the certainty of the practical rule ("act on the probable") — it is second-order, so firmness here is not dogmatism; Descartes defends the point against the charge of obstinacy in the letter to Reneri for Pollot (April 1638). The maxim inverts exactly what Part Four will do in theory, where the doubtful is to be treated as false (AT 31). The Latin applies firmness even to opinions adopted "for no reason at all," which makes the forest example literal: an arbitrary choice, once made, earns constancy.

(AT 25–27) The third maxim was to try always to master himself rather than fortune, and to change his desires rather than the order of the world; and to accustom himself to believe that nothing is wholly in our power except our thoughts, so that once we have done our best concerning external things, whatever we fail to obtain is, as far as we are concerned, absolutely impossible. This alone seemed enough to keep him from desiring what he could not get, and so to make him content. Our will naturally desires only what the understanding represents as in some way possible; if we regard all goods outside us as equally beyond our power, we will no more regret lacking those our birth seemed to promise, when we lose them through no fault of ours, than we regret not owning the kingdoms of China or Mexico. Making a virtue of necessity, we will no more wish to be well when sick, or free in prison, than we now wish for bodies as incorruptible as diamonds or wings to fly like birds. This takes long exercise and repeated meditation, and here lay the secret of those philosophers who escaped fortune's rule and, despite pain and poverty, rivalled their gods in happiness: persuaded that nothing was in their power but their thoughts, they disposed of these so completely that they could count themselves richer, freer and happier than other men. The maxim joins a psychological law — will follows the understanding's representation of what is possible — to a discipline of judgment that reclassifies what fails as impossible, so that desire is governed through judgment. The Latin marks the reclassification as a stipulation: what fails is to be counted among things "called impossible in philosophical vocabulary." "Thoughts" here includes volitions, and the doctrine matures in the Passions (§§144–146, 152), where only the free use of the will is truly ours. Part One's critique of Stoic morality is now qualified: the Stoic discipline of desire is endorsed, while their pride and impassivity remain rejected.

(AT 27–28) To conclude this morality he reviewed the occupations of men to choose the best and, without judging others', decided he could do no better than continue in his own: to spend his life cultivating his reason and advancing in knowledge of truth by the method. It had given him contentments so extreme and innocent that he thought none sweeter possible, and the truths it disclosed daily filled him so that nothing else touched him. Besides, the three maxims rested only on his design to keep instructing himself. God having given each of us some light to tell true from false, he would not have contented himself with others' opinions for a moment had he not meant to examine them in time; he could not have followed them without scruple had he not hoped to lose no chance of finding better ones; and he could not have limited his desires or been content had he not followed a road that assured him of all the knowledge, and thereby all the true goods, within his reach. Since the will pursues or flees nothing except as the understanding represents it good or bad, it suffices to judge well in order to do well, and to judge as well as one can in order to do one's best, that is, to acquire all the virtues and all other goods within reach; and whoever is sure of this cannot fail to be content. The choice of occupation is the fourth maxim and grounds the other three: each is rational only as a moment of continuing inquiry. The intellectualist thesis that judging well suffices for doing well restates the Socratic position; the Fourth Meditation and the letters to Mesland (1645) qualify it by the will's power to withhold or give assent. Contentment comes from being certain one has done one's best, an ethics of intention that the letter to Elisabeth of 4 August 1645 reformulates around the same three maxims.

(AT 28–30) Having secured these maxims and set them apart with the truths of faith, which always came first in his belief, he judged himself free to get rid of the rest of his opinions. Hoping to do this better among men than shut up in the heated room, he resumed his travels before winter's end and for nine years did nothing but roam the world, a spectator rather than an actor in its comedies. Reflecting in each matter on what could make it suspect and mislead us, he meanwhile uprooted from his mind the errors that had slipped in. He did not imitate the sceptics, who doubt for the sake of doubting and pretend to be always undecided; his whole aim was to reach assurance, to clear away loose earth and sand and find rock or clay. He succeeded, since he examined propositions not by weak conjectures but by clear and sure reasonings, and found none so doubtful that he could not draw some fairly certain conclusion from it, if only that it contained nothing certain. As the rubble of an old house is kept for building a new one, so the destruction of his ill-founded opinions yielded observations and experiences that later served to establish more certain ones. He kept practising the method, generally in all his thinking and specially, some hours at a time, on mathematical difficulties or others he made nearly mathematical by detaching them from the uncertain principles of other sciences, as several in this volume show. Living outwardly like those who pass a pleasant, innocent life, he advanced in knowledge more than if he had only read books or frequented scholars. The nine years' doubt is local and gradual, matter by matter, unlike the global doubt of Part Four. Its anti-sceptical point is that even a negative result yields a certainty — certainty that something is uncertain — and this prefigures the moment when doubt itself yields the first certain truth. Detaching questions from uncertain principles is the procedure of the Essays, which Part Six calls their "suppositions" (AT 76). The truths of faith are exempt from doubt along with the maxims, on parallel grounds of conduct.

(AT 30–31) Those nine years passed before he took any position on the questions disputed among the learned or began to seek the foundations of a philosophy more certain than the common one. The failure of several excellent minds made him think the task so hard that he might have delayed longer, had he not heard that some were already reporting he had succeeded. He could not say what they based this on; if he contributed, it was by confessing his ignorance more openly than the half-learned do, or by showing his reasons for doubting much that others think certain, not by boasting of any doctrine. Having too much integrity to want to pass for what he was not, he resolved to make himself worthy of the reputation. Exactly eight years ago this desire made him leave every place where he had acquaintances and retire here, to a country where a long war has established such order that its armies seem to serve only to let people enjoy the fruits of peace more securely, and where among a busy people more concerned with their own affairs than curious about others' he has lived as solitary as in the remotest desert. The metaphysical beginning is presented as an obligation to deserve a reputation, which links the epistemic project to an ethics of self-presentation that returns in Part Six (AT 74). The dates fix the turn: retreat to the Netherlands in 1628–29 and the short metaphysical treatise of 1629 mentioned to Mersenne (15 April 1630). Solitude amid a crowd, not withdrawal from the world, is the condition of meditation.

### Part Four

(AT 31–32) He hardly knows whether to report his first meditations there, so metaphysical and uncommon that they may not suit everyone; yet to let readers judge whether his foundations are firm, he is somewhat obliged to. He had long noted that in conduct one must sometimes follow very uncertain opinions as if they were indubitable. But now, wishing to devote himself only to seeking truth, he thought he must do the opposite and reject as absolutely false everything in which he could imagine the least doubt, to see whether anything entirely indubitable would remain. Because the senses sometimes deceive us, he supposed nothing to be such as they make us imagine. Because men err in reasoning even on the simplest points of geometry, and he was as liable to error as anyone, he rejected as false all the reasonings he had taken for demonstrations. And because all the thoughts we have awake can come to us asleep without any being true, he resolved to pretend that everything that had ever entered his mind was no more true than the illusions of his dreams. But immediately he noticed that while he wished to think everything false, it was necessary that he, who thought it, be something. Seeing that this truth — I think, therefore I am — was so firm and assured that the most extravagant suppositions of the sceptics could not shake it, he judged he could accept it without scruple as the first principle of the philosophy he sought. The doubt has three grounds of increasing scope: the senses, then reasoning, then all the contents of thought through the dream. There is no deceiving God or evil genius, and the doubt about demonstrations rests on human fallibility, coming before the dream rather than, as in the Meditations, after it. The first truth appears inside the act of doubt, as its condition: it is the doubter's own thinking that resists being thought false. Descartes later denied that the "therefore" marks a syllogism (Second Replies); the connection is seen in a single intuition. The Latin reads "Ego cogito, ergo sum, sive existo" and has him unable to doubt "that I myself existed meanwhile," where the French says only that he "was something." The Latin thus makes explicit that the first principle asserts existence during the act, not a predicate. "Almost false" (AT 8) has become "absolutely false."

(AT 32–33) Examining next what he was, he saw he could pretend to have no body and that there was no world or place where he was, but not that he did not exist; on the contrary, from his very thinking of doubting other things it followed most evidently and certainly that he existed. Whereas had he merely ceased to think, even if all he had ever imagined were true, he would have no reason to believe he existed. He knew from this that he was a substance whose whole essence or nature is only to think, which needs no place and depends on no material thing to exist. So this "I," that is, the soul by which I am what I am, is entirely distinct from the body, is even easier to know, and would not cease to be all it is if the body did not exist. The step from what he can pretend away to what his essence excludes is the one the preface to the Meditations (AT VII 7–8) concedes was too quick: the Discourse shows only that he is aware of nothing else belonging to his essence, not that nothing else does, and the full proof of real distinction waits on God in the Sixth Meditation. The counterfactual "had I ceased to think" ties the evidence of existence to thinking alone. The Latin makes three changes that move the text toward the Meditations. Existence follows from "pretending the rest false or thinking anything else at all," which generalises from doubt to every mode of thought. It would vanish "if I ceased to think for even a moment," which brings in the temporal condition of "as long as I think." And the French "soul" becomes "mind, by which alone I am what I am" (mens per quam solam), which strips the term of its vegetative and sensitive connotations, a distinction Descartes insists on in the Fifth Replies.

(AT 33) He then considered in general what a proposition needs to be true and certain, since, having found one he knew to be so, he ought to know what that certainty consists in. Noting that nothing in "I think, therefore I am" assures him he speaks the truth except that he sees very clearly that in order to think one must exist, he judged he could take as a general rule that the things we conceive very clearly and very distinctly are all true, with only some difficulty in noticing which things we conceive distinctly. The rule is extracted by reflecting on a single paradigm, so its generality is provisional until God guarantees it (AT 38), and the passage from cogito to rule to God is the site of Arnauld's charge of circularity. "To think one must exist" is seen within the cogito, not laid down as a premise before it, which supports the reading that the cogito is not a syllogism (Principles I.10). The difficulty of telling which conceptions are distinct, admitted in passing, is what Principles I.45–46 addresses with its definitions of clarity and distinctness.

(AT 33–36) Reflecting that he doubted, and so that his being was not wholly perfect — knowing being clearly a greater perfection than doubting — he sought where he had learned to think of something more perfect than himself, and saw evidently that it must be from a nature really more perfect. Thoughts of things outside him, such as sky, earth, light and heat, raised no such question: finding nothing in them that set them above him, he could believe that, if true, they depended on his nature insofar as it had some perfection, and if false, that he had them from nothing, because he had some defect. But the idea of a being more perfect than his own could not come from nothing, which is manifestly impossible; nor from himself, since it is no less contradictory that the more perfect should follow from and depend on the less perfect than that something should come from nothing. It remained that it had been put in him by a nature truly more perfect, containing all the perfections he could have any idea of — in a word, God. He added that, knowing perfections he lacked, he was not the only being that existed (to use, he says, the language of the schools), but there must be another, more perfect, on which he depended and from which he had all he had. For had he been alone and independent, having from himself the little he shared of perfect being, he could for the same reason have given himself all the rest he knew he lacked, and been infinite, eternal, immutable, omniscient, omnipotent. To know God's nature as far as his own allowed, he had only to ask, of each thing he had an idea of, whether possessing it was a perfection: doubt, inconstancy, sadness and the like could not be in God. He also had ideas of many sensible and corporeal things, which were truly in his thought even if he was dreaming. But since he had already known clearly that intelligent nature is distinct from corporeal, and all composition shows dependence, which is a defect, he judged that God could not be composed of these two natures; and if there were bodies, or intelligences, or other natures not wholly perfect, their being must depend on God's power so that they could not subsist without him for a moment. Two proofs are compressed here: from the idea of the more perfect, by the principle that the more perfect cannot come from the less, and from his own dependence, since a self-caused being would have given itself every perfection — the two proofs of the Third Meditation. A third result follows from the second: continual dependence, the doctrine that conservation is continued creation, on which Part Five's cosmogony relies (AT 45). The French argument has a gap: an idea, taken as an act of thought, is no more perfect than any other thought, so "the idea of a more perfect being" needs a distinction the text does not draw. The Latin supplies it in a note: "idea" means "every thing thought, insofar as it has only a certain objective being in the understanding." The preface to the Meditations (AT VII 8) admits the same ambiguity between idea taken materially and idea taken objectively. Without objective reality the causal proof does not go through.

(AT 36) He then sought other truths. Taking the object of the geometers, which he conceived as a continuous body or a space indefinitely extended in length, breadth and height or depth, divisible into parts of various shapes and sizes that can be moved or transposed in every way, he went through some of their simplest demonstrations. Their great certainty rests only on their being conceived evidently, according to the rule; yet nothing in them assured him that their object existed. Supposing a triangle, its three angles must equal two right angles, but nothing assured him there was a triangle in the world. Returning to the idea of a perfect being, he found existence contained in it as the equality of the angles is contained in the idea of a triangle, or the equidistance of all points from the centre in that of a sphere, or even more evidently; so it is at least as certain that God exists as any geometrical demonstration can be. The ontological proof is introduced by contrast with mathematics: in geometry, essence without existence; in God, an essence that includes existence. The object of geometry is already defined as continuous extended body, which is the matter of Part Five's physics; the Fifth Meditation keeps the same order, essence of material things first and then God. The claim that God's existence is as certain as geometry, or more so, sits uneasily with the next paragraph, where geometry's certainty itself depends on God (AT 38).

(AT 37) What makes many think it hard to know God, and even their own soul, is that they never raise their mind above sensible things. They are so used to considering nothing except by imagining it — a way of thinking proper to material things — that whatever cannot be imagined seems to them unintelligible. This is plain from the scholastic maxim that nothing is in the understanding that was not first in the senses, though the ideas of God and of the soul certainly never were. Using imagination to grasp them is like using the eyes to hear sounds or smell odours, with one difference: sight assures us of its objects as much as smell or hearing of theirs, whereas neither imagination nor the senses can ever assure us of anything without the understanding. Imagination is a mode of thinking specialised for bodies, which anticipates the Sixth Meditation's chiliagon and its distinction between imagining and understanding. The attack on the Peripatetic axiom strikes at the origin of ideas and implies innatism, defended at length in the Comments on a Certain Broadsheet (1648). The closing asymmetry ranks every faculty below the understanding: the senses and imagination have no certainty of their own.

(AT 37–40) If some are still unpersuaded of the existence of God and of their soul, they should know that everything else they think more certain — having a body, there being stars and an earth — is less certain. We have a moral assurance of these things such that only an extravagant person could doubt them; but where metaphysical certainty is in question, no reasonable person can deny there is reason enough for doubt, given that in sleep we can imagine another body, other stars and another earth that do not exist. How do we know dream thoughts are falser than waking ones, when they are often as vivid? Let the best minds try; no reason will remove this doubt without presupposing God's existence. The very rule taken earlier, that what we conceive clearly and distinctly is true, is assured only because God is or exists, is perfect, and everything in us comes from him. Our ideas, being real things that come from God, can only be true insofar as they are clear and distinct; if we often have false ones, they must be confused and obscure ones, which in that respect share in nothingness, being confused in us only because we are not wholly perfect. It is no less contradictory that falsity or imperfection come from God as such than that truth or perfection come from nothing. Once God and the soul have made the rule certain, dreams give no reason to doubt waking thoughts. A very distinct idea, such as a new demonstration found in sleep, would be true all the same. The commonest dream error — presenting objects as the external senses do — does not matter, since the senses deceive us awake too, as when the jaundiced see everything yellow or distant stars look small. Awake or asleep, we should be persuaded only by the evidence of our reason — reason, not imagination or the senses. We see the sun very clearly yet should not judge it as small as it looks; we can distinctly imagine a lion's head on a goat's body without concluding that a chimera exists. Reason does not dictate that what we see or imagine is true, but it does dictate that all our ideas have some foundation of truth, since God, wholly perfect and truthful, would not have put them in us otherwise. And because reasoning is never as evident or complete in sleep as awake, though imaginings may be more vivid, reason also dictates that what truth our thoughts have must be found in waking ones rather than dreams. The distinction between moral and metaphysical certainty enters here and returns in Part Five's tests (AT 56–57) and in Principles IV.205–206. God guarantees the rule that was first drawn from the cogito, which is the passage on which the charge of the Cartesian circle fastens. Truth is treated as a perfection and falsity as a privation that "shares in nothingness," the theory of error of the Fourth Meditation. Dream and waking are told apart not by vividness but by the completeness of reasoning, a version of the coherence criterion that ends the Sixth Meditation. That every idea has "some foundation of truth" leaves room for the partial truth of the senses. The Latin speaks of whatever "of being" is in us coming from God and calls ideas "beings" (entia quaedam), which states outright that truth follows the being of ideas and falsity their lack of it.

### Part Five

(AT 40–41) He would gladly set out the whole chain of truths he deduced from these first ones, but that would mean entering controversies with the learned; he will only say in general what they are, and let the wisest judge whether the public should hear more. He has held to his resolution to assume no principle but the one he has just used to prove God and the soul, and to accept nothing that did not seem clearer and more certain than the geometers' demonstrations had seemed before. Yet he has found how to satisfy himself on all the main difficulties of philosophy, and has noticed certain laws that God established in nature and whose notions he impressed on our souls, such that after reflection we cannot doubt they are exactly observed in everything that is or happens. Following their consequences, he has found truths more useful than anything he had learned or hoped to learn. Physics depends on metaphysics twice over: God is the legislator of nature and also the author of our innate notions of its laws. The laws are therefore known a priori, from God's perfections, as Principles II.36–42 later derives them from his immutability. The reluctance to enter controversy is the first sign of the decision Part Six explains.

(AT 41–45) He tried to explain the main points in a treatise, which certain considerations keep him from publishing, and will summarise it. He meant to include everything he knew about material things, but, as painters who cannot show every face of a solid on a flat canvas light one face and leave the rest in shadow, he set out at length only his conception of light. He then added something about the sun and fixed stars, its source, the heavens, which transmit it, the planets, comets and earth, which reflect it, terrestrial bodies, which are coloured, transparent or luminous, and finally man, its spectator. To speak freely without following or refuting received opinions, he left this world to the disputes of the learned and described what would happen in a new world if God now created enough matter somewhere in imaginary spaces, stirred it into a chaos as confused as poets could imagine, and then only lent nature his ordinary concourse, letting it act by the laws he had established. He described this matter as more clearly intelligible than anything except God and the soul, expressly supposing in it none of the forms or qualities disputed in the schools, nothing whose knowledge is not so natural to our souls that we could not even pretend to be ignorant of it. He showed the laws of nature, proving any that might be doubted from God's infinite perfections alone and showing that they would hold in any world God might create. He then showed how most of the chaos would arrange itself into heavens like ours, and parts of it into an earth, planets, comets, a sun and fixed stars; how light in the sun and stars crosses the heavens in an instant and is reflected from planets and comets to the earth; how the earth's parts tend toward its centre though he had supposed no weight in its matter; how the moon causes tides and an east-west current in the tropics; how mountains, seas, springs, rivers, metals, plants and mixed bodies arise; and above all how fire works — heat without light, light without heat, colours, melting, hardening, burning to ash, and the making of glass from ash, which he described with particular pleasure. The fable of the new world serves two purposes. It avoids disputes and censorship, and it makes things intelligible by showing how they arise from laws. Matter is defined by what we cannot pretend not to know, which is extension and its modes; forms and real qualities disappear. The laws hold in every possible world because they rest on God's immutability, not on the arrangement of this one. Weight is explained rather than assumed. Instantaneous transmission of light is the premise the Optics' analogies (the blind man's stick, the wine vat) are built to illustrate. The list of topics — light, fire, colours, the earth's bodies — maps the treatise The World (1629–33) onto the Essays now being offered.

(AT 45) He does not infer that this world was made in the way he described; it is far likelier that God made it as it was to be from the start. But theologians commonly hold that the action by which God now conserves the world is the same as that by which he created it. So even if he had given it at first only the form of chaos, provided he established the laws of nature and lent his concourse, one may believe without wronging the miracle of creation that purely material things could in time have become as we now see them; and their nature is far easier to conceive when we see them arise gradually than when we consider them only as finished. The cosmogony is counterfactual and its value is explanatory, not historical, which guards against reading it as a denial of Genesis; Principles III.45 repeats the move, calling such hypotheses false yet useful. The licence comes from Part Four's thesis of continual dependence (AT 36): if conservation is creation continued, the present working of nature is continuous with a genetic process. Genesis serves understanding because it exhibits the order of causes, the order the third precept requires.

(AT 45–46) From inanimate bodies and plants he passed to animals, and especially to man. Not yet knowing enough to treat them in the same style, demonstrating effects from causes and showing from what seeds and how nature produces them, he supposed that God formed a human body exactly like ours, inside and out, from the matter described, without any rational soul or anything to serve as a vegetative or sensitive soul, but only kindling in its heart a fire without light, like the heat of hay stored damp or of new wine fermenting on the grape skins. Examining the functions that would follow, he found all those that can occur in us without our thinking, and so without our soul — the part distinct from body whose nature is only to think — contributing; these are the functions in which animals resemble us. He found none of those that depend on thought and belong to us as men, but found them afterwards on supposing that God created a rational soul and joined it to this body in a certain way. Physiology changes method. Instead of a genesis from causes, it offers a model: suppose the body built, then check its functions — the statue-machine of the Treatise on Man. The vegetative and sensitive souls are eliminated: one soul remains, rational, and every function of life is mechanical. The line between body and soul is drawn functionally, between what happens without thought and what depends on it. The heart's heat as a fermentation fire is the premise of the next paragraphs and the point on which Descartes parts from Harvey.

(AT 46–49) To show how he treated the matter he gives his account of the movement of the heart and arteries, the first and most general movement in animals. Readers unfamiliar with anatomy should have the heart of a large animal with lungs cut open before them. On the right side are the vena cava, the main reservoir of the blood and trunk of all the veins, and the "arterial vein," wrongly named since it is an artery, branching into the lungs; on the left the "venous artery," wrongly named since it is a vein coming from the lungs, and the great artery, which sends its branches through the whole body. Eleven small membranes open and close the four orifices: three at the vena cava, letting blood into the right cavity but not out; three at the arterial vein, letting it pass to the lungs but not back; two at the venous artery, letting it into the left cavity but not back; three at the great artery, letting it out but not back. Two suffice at the venous artery because its opening is oval, while the round ones are better closed by three. The great artery and arterial vein are much harder than the venous artery and vena cava, which swell into the auricles before entering the heart; the heart is hotter than any other part, and its heat makes any drop of blood entering it swell and expand at once, as liquids do when dripped into a very hot vessel. The anatomy is set out so that the explanation will follow from visible structure plus heat, and the reader is invited to verify by dissection. Even the number of valves is explained by the geometry of the openings rather than by purpose. The one premise not open to inspection, the heart's special heat, is the one his critics contested (Plempius, 1638).

(AT 49–50) Nothing more is needed to explain the heart's movement. When its cavities are not full, blood flows from the vena cava into the right and from the venous artery into the left, since these vessels are always full and their openings toward the heart cannot then be closed. As soon as a drop enters each cavity — large drops, since the openings are wide and the vessels full — the heat rarefies them, they swell the whole heart, push shut the five valves at the entrances, and, expanding further, push open the six at the exits, swelling the arterial vein and great artery almost at the same instant as the heart. The heart and arteries then subside as the blood cools; the six valves close and the five reopen to admit two more drops, and the cycle repeats. Since the blood passes through the auricles, their movement is opposite to the heart's. Those who do not know the force of mathematical demonstrations, and cannot distinguish true reasons from probable ones, should not reject this unexamined: the movement follows as necessarily from the disposition of organs visible in the heart, the heat felt with the fingers and the nature of blood known by experience, as a clock's movement from the force, position and shape of its weights and wheels. The active phase is diastole, produced by ebullition of the blood, whereas Harvey made systole a muscular contraction; on this point Descartes was wrong. The clock comparison states the mechanist ideal of explanation, but the necessity it claims is conditional on empirical premises, chiefly the heat. Mechanical intelligibility is being offered as if it were demonstration.

(AT 50–52) If one asks why the veins are not emptied and the arteries overfilled, the answer has been given by Harvey, an English physician, "who must be praised for breaking the ice" and first teaching that small passages at the ends of the arteries let blood into the veins and back to the heart, so that its course is a perpetual circulation. A marginal note gives the source, Harvey's De motu cordis (1628). Harvey proves this by the surgeons' practice: a moderately tight ligature above a venesection makes blood flow more freely, while a ligature below, or a very tight one above, does the opposite. The moderate ligature stops venous return but not arterial inflow, since the arteries lie deeper, have harder walls and carry blood with greater force; and since blood leaves by a vein below the ligature, there must be passages near the extremities from the arteries. He proves it also by the valves along the veins, which let blood flow only toward the heart, and by the experiment showing that all the blood can drain from one cut artery even when it is tied close to the heart and cut between heart and ligature. Descartes accepts Harvey's circulation, argued by experiment and quantity, while rejecting his account of what drives it. This was among the first public endorsements of Harvey by a major philosopher, and it is strategic: Harvey's experimental proofs are placed in support of a mechanical physiology Harvey did not hold. Descartes recasts even the ligature argument as a mechanical inference from depth, wall hardness and force.

(AT 52–55) Much else confirms that the true cause of the blood's movement is the one he gives. Arterial blood differs from venous because it has been rarefied and distilled in the heart, and the difference is clear only near the heart. The hardness of the arterial walls shows the blood strikes them more forcefully. The left cavity and great artery are larger because blood returning from the lungs is finer and rarefies more readily. Physicians reading the pulse must assume that blood rarefies more or less depending on its state. Heat reaches the limbs by the blood, so that removing blood removes heat. Respiration brings enough fresh air into the lungs to condense the blood vaporised in the right cavity before it falls into the left, without which it could not feed the fire there; animals without lungs have one cavity, and the foetus, which cannot use its lungs, has a passage from the vena cava to the left cavity and a duct from the arterial vein to the great artery. Digestion depends on heat and fluid parts of the blood sent by the arteries; sanguification on the chyle being distilled through the heart a hundred or two hundred times a day; nutrition on particles lodging in the members and sorted by the pores like grain by sieves. Most remarkable is the generation of the animal spirits, a very subtle wind or rather a very pure flame, which rises from the heart to the brain and passes through the nerves into the muscles. The most agitated particles go to the brain because the arteries leading there run straightest from the heart, and by the rules of mechanics, "which are the same as those of nature," when many things tend one way with no room for all, the weaker are pushed aside by the stronger. The cascade of explanations from one principle, heat and rarefaction, functions as confirmation, the pattern Part Six formalises: effects certified by experience prove the causes that explain them (AT 76). The identity of the rules of mechanics with those of nature is mechanism stated as a thesis. The foetal anatomy serves as a natural experiment. The animal spirits, purely material particles, are the hinge between the circulation and the account of nerves, sensation and movement that follows.

(AT 55–56) He had explained all this in detail in the treatise he once meant to publish. He showed there how nerves and muscles must be built for the spirits to move the limbs — heads still move and bite the ground shortly after being cut off, though no longer animated — and what changes in the brain cause waking, sleep and dreams. He showed how light, sounds, smells, tastes, heat and the other qualities of external objects imprint various ideas on it through the senses, and how hunger, thirst and other inner passions send theirs too. He showed what should be taken there for the common sense, where these ideas are received, for memory, which keeps them, and for fancy, which can alter and combine them, and which, by distributing the spirits to the muscles, moves the limbs in as many ways, and as suitably to the objects and passions, as our own can move without the will directing them. None of this will seem strange to those who know how many automata human industry makes with few parts, compared with the multitude of bones, muscles, nerves, arteries and veins in an animal body: they will regard that body as a machine made by God's hands, incomparably better ordered and capable of more admirable movements than any human invention. "Ideas" here are corporeal impressions in the brain, figures traced on its surface as in the Treatise on Man, not the forms of thought of Part Four; the term is equivocal, and Descartes later restricts it (Second Replies, definition II). The scholastic internal senses — common sense, memory, fancy — are relocated as brain functions. The severed head shows that movement does not imply soul. The automaton comparison turns on scale: divine craftsmanship makes the body's greater complexity plausible, so the analogy favours mechanism.

(AT 56–57) He stopped here to show that if there were machines with the organs and shape of a monkey or another irrational animal, we would have no way to tell they were not of the same nature as those animals; whereas if there were machines resembling our bodies and imitating our actions as far as morally possible, we would always have two very certain means of recognising that they were not real men. First, they could never use words or other signs, composing them as we do to declare our thoughts. A machine might utter words, even words prompted by bodily actions — asking what we want when touched here, crying that it hurts when touched there — but not arrange them variously to answer the sense of whatever is said in its presence, as the dullest men can. Second, though they might do some things as well as we, or better, they would inevitably fail in others, revealing that they act not by knowledge but only by the disposition of their organs. Reason is a universal instrument usable in every kind of encounter, whereas organs need a particular disposition for each particular action, so it is morally impossible for a machine to have enough of them to act in all the occurrences of life as reason makes us act. The tests are behavioural criteria for thought: appropriate, open-ended composition of signs, and general flexibility of response. The asymmetry is the argument's core. A machine-monkey cannot be distinguished from a monkey because the monkey is itself a machine, while a machine-man can be distinguished from a man, so the conclusion presupposes the mechanical physiology just given. "Morally impossible" gives the tests the modality of moral certainty defined at AT 37–38: finite organs cannot be shown impossible for every situation, only beyond credible number. The Latin renders this as "plainly incredible" (plane incredibile), losing the precise modal term. It also requires that the machine arrange words "of its own motion," which locates the first test in self-initiated composition.

(AT 57–59) The same two means show the difference between men and beasts. No man is so dull or stupid, not even the insane, that he cannot arrange words into a discourse that makes his thoughts understood, and no other animal, however perfect or well-born, does the like. This is not for lack of organs: magpies and parrots utter words as we do but cannot speak as we do, showing that they think what they say, whereas men born deaf and mute, lacking the organs of speech as much as beasts or more, invent signs to make themselves understood by those who live with them. This shows not that beasts have less reason than men but that they have none. Little reason is needed to speak, and since animals of one species differ as men do, some easier to train, it is incredible that the most perfect monkey or parrot would not equal the stupidest child, or at least a disturbed one, unless its soul were of a wholly different nature. Words must not be confused with the natural movements that express passions, which machines and animals can imitate, nor should one think with some ancients that beasts speak a language we do not understand: having organs like ours, they could make themselves understood to us as to one another. Again, animals that show more skill than we in some actions show none in many others, so what they do better proves not that they have a mind — they would then have more than any of us and excel in everything — but that they have none, and that nature acts in them by the disposition of their organs, as a clock of wheels and springs counts hours more exactly than all our prudence. The argument from variation within a species does the decisive work. If reason came in degrees, the best animals would show at least the minimal reason speech requires; since none does, reason is absent altogether. This all-or-nothing conclusion rests on Part One's thesis that reason admits no degrees. The Latin makes the criterion explicit, contrasting speech and "all signs that signify thoughts by human institution" with "natural signs by which bodily affections are indicated": the test turns on conventional signs. The "ancients" are Plutarch and Porphyry as transmitted by Montaigne's Apology. Descartes denies animals thought, not life or the bodily feeling that the letters to Newcastle (1646) and More (1649) allow them.

(AT 59–60) He had then described the rational soul, showing that it cannot be drawn from the power of matter like the other things he had treated but must be expressly created, and that it is not enough for it to be lodged in the body like a pilot in his ship, except perhaps to move its limbs; it must be joined and united more closely with it to have feelings and appetites like ours and so make a real man. He has dwelt on the soul because nothing, after the error of denying God, leads weak minds further from virtue than imagining that the souls of beasts are of the same nature as ours, so that we have no more to fear or hope after this life than flies and ants. Knowing how they differ, one better understands the reasons proving our soul independent of the body and so not bound to die with it; and since no other causes are seen that could destroy it, one is naturally led to judge it immortal. The pilot image is rejected in favour of union: feelings and appetites belong to neither soul nor body alone and are the evidence of their union, which anticipates the Sixth Meditation ("not merely present as a sailor in a ship") and the primitive notion of union in the letters to Elisabeth (1643). Immortality is argued only as a natural inference from independence and the absence of destroying causes, not demonstrated; the second edition of the Meditations duly promises only the soul's distinction from the body. The moral stake of the beast-machine is now explicit: denying animals reason protects the hope of an afterlife. "Drawn from the power of matter" is the scholastic eduction from the potency of matter, which the Latin names (educi), and it marks the line between the animal soul as scholastics understood it and a created mind.

### Part Six
