(AT 60–61) Three years ago, having finished the treatise and begun revising it for the printer, he learned that persons to whom he defers, and whose authority over his actions is hardly less than his own reason's over his thoughts, had condemned an opinion in physics published a little earlier by someone else. He will not say he held it, only that before their censure he had seen nothing in it harmful to religion or the state, and so nothing that would have stopped him writing it had reason persuaded him. This made him fear some opinion of his own might be similarly mistaken, despite his care to accept nothing new without very certain demonstrations and to write nothing harmful to anyone. That was enough to change his resolution to publish; his inclination, which always made him hate writing books, quickly found other excuses. The reasons on both sides are worth stating, for him and perhaps for the public. The allusion is to the condemnation of Galileo (June 1633) and the motion of the earth, which The World did hold. The formula "authority over my actions as reason over my thoughts" reproduces the two jurisdictions of Part Three: the decision not to publish is an action, governed by the first maxim, and leaves his judgment where it was. The care with which he declines to say whether he held the opinion is itself an application of that division.

(AT 61–63) He never thought much of what came from his own mind, and as long as the method yielded only satisfaction in speculative questions or rules for his conduct, he felt no obligation to write. In morals everyone is so full of his own sense that there would be as many reformers as heads, if anyone but those God made sovereigns or endowed with grace and zeal enough to be prophets could undertake changes; and others' speculations please them as much as his please him. But once he had gained some general notions of physics and, testing them on particular difficulties, saw how far they could lead and how they differed from received principles, he could not hide them without sinning against the law that obliges us to procure the general good of all. They show that we can reach knowledge very useful to life and, instead of the speculative philosophy taught in the schools, find a practical one by which, knowing the force and actions of fire, water, air, stars, heavens and the bodies around us as distinctly as we know the trades of our craftsmen, we could use them for all their proper purposes and so make ourselves, as it were, masters and possessors of nature. This is desirable for inventing countless devices that let us enjoy the earth's fruits without labour, but chiefly for preserving health, the first good and foundation of all others, since the mind depends so much on the temperament and disposition of the bodily organs that if men can be made wiser and abler, the means must be sought in medicine. Present medicine knows almost nothing compared with what remains, and we could be spared countless diseases of body and mind, perhaps even the weakness of age, if we knew their causes and remedies. Resolved to spend his life on this necessary science, and having found a road that must lead to it unless cut short by brevity of life or lack of experiments, he judged the best remedy was to publish faithfully what he found and invite good minds to contribute experiments and publish theirs, so that the later might begin where the earlier ended and, joining many lives and labours, we might go much further together than anyone alone. Publication becomes a duty of charity, reversing the earlier privacy. The practical philosophy is modelled on the craftsman's knowledge of causal powers, and its "as it were" (comme; Latin velut) qualifies the mastery: we become like owners of nature, which remains God's. The mastery has a priority, medicine, and through medicine the mind, because the mind depends on the body's temperament, a premise of the Passions of the Soul. Morals stay closed to private reformers, as Part Two required. The collective, cumulative research programme recalls Bacon.

(AT 63–65) He also noted that experiments become more necessary the further knowledge advances. At first it is better to use only those that present themselves to the senses and cannot be missed with a little reflection than to seek rare and contrived ones, which mislead while the causes of the common ones are unknown and whose circumstances are so particular and slight as to be hard to notice. His order was this. First he sought in general the principles or first causes of everything that is or can be in the world, considering for this only God who created it and drawing them only from certain seeds of truth naturally in our souls. Then he examined the first and most ordinary effects deducible from these causes, and found heavens, stars, an earth, and on it water, air, fire, minerals and similar things, the most common and simple and so easiest to know. When he tried to descend to more particular things, so many different ones appeared that he did not think the human mind could distinguish the forms or species of bodies on earth from countless others God might have put there, or turn them to our use, unless we come to the causes through the effects and use many particular experiments. Reviewing every object his senses had met, he found nothing he could not fairly easily explain by his principles. But nature's power is so vast, and the principles so simple and general, that nearly every particular effect can be deduced from them in several ways, and his chief difficulty is finding which way it actually depends on them. The only remedy is to seek experiments whose outcome differs depending on which explanation is right. He sees how to make most of them, but they are so many that neither his hands nor his income, even a thousand times larger, would suffice; his progress will depend on his means. He had hoped the treatise would show the public's benefit so clearly that all who wish the general good — the truly virtuous, not the merely apparent — would share their experiments and help with the rest. The principles underdetermine the particulars: many possible worlds are consistent with the laws, and only experience selects ours. Experiment here does not generate principles by induction; it decides among deductions already possible from them — the logic later called the crucial experiment. The order of experience follows the third precept: common experiences before rare ones. The underdetermination explains why particular explanations in physics reach only moral certainty (Principles IV.204–205) and why the Essays proceed by "suppositions" (AT 76).

(AT 65–68) Since then other reasons have changed his mind. He should keep writing down everything important as he discovers its truth, with the same care as if for print: one looks more closely at what one expects many to see, and things that seemed true when first conceived have often seemed false once he put them on paper. It also loses no chance of benefiting the public, since those who have his writings after his death can use them. But he should not consent to their publication in his lifetime, lest opposition, controversy or even reputation cost him the time he means to spend instructing himself. Everyone must procure the good of others, and to be useful to no one is to be worthless; but our care should reach beyond the present, and it is right to forgo what might profit the living in order to do what profits posterity more. What he has learned is almost nothing beside what he hopes to learn. Those who discover truth gradually are like men growing rich, for whom large gains come easier than small ones did when they were poor, or like generals whose forces grow with their victories and who need more skill to hold together after a defeat than to take towns after a victory. Overcoming the difficulties and errors that bar the way to truth is truly fighting battles, and accepting a false opinion on a general and important matter is losing one, after which it takes more skill to recover than to advance once sure principles are held. His own truths are consequences of five or six main difficulties overcome, battles won; two or three more like them would complete his designs, and he is not too old to manage it. Writing serves as an instrument of examination: the page tests what conception alone accepted. Duty to the living is weighed against duty to posterity, and the second justifies withholding publication. The comparisons share one structure: knowledge compounds, so a false general principle costs more than ignorance, and this asymmetry justifies the severity of the doubt.

(AT 68–69) It may be said that objections would help, by showing him his faults, by giving others a better grasp of whatever is good in his work and, since many see more than one, by letting them improve it with their own discoveries. But although he knows himself very liable to error and almost never trusts his first thoughts, his experience of objections gives him no hope of profit. He has often tried the judgments of friends, of people he thought indifferent, and of some whose malice and envy he knew would expose what affection hid from friends, yet rarely has anyone objected something he had not wholly foreseen, unless it was far from his subject; he has hardly ever met a critic who seemed as rigorous or as fair as himself. Nor has he seen the disputations of the schools discover any truth unknown before: each side tries to win, practising plausibility rather than weighing reasons, and good advocates do not thereby become better judges. The objection to disputation is that it is adversarial: it rewards plausibility, the scholastic virtue already condemned in Part One. Advocacy and judgment are distinct capacities. The confidence that no critic would surprise him sits oddly beside AT 75's request for objections and beside the solicited Objections of 1641, which did surprise him on several points.

(AT 69–72) Nor would others gain much from his thoughts, since he has not taken them far enough to be applied without much addition, and he can say without vanity that if anyone can complete them it is he — not because there are no better minds, but because one cannot understand a thing so well and make it one's own when learning it from another as when inventing it oneself. He has often explained opinions to very able people who seemed to understand perfectly, yet when they repeated them they changed them so much he could no longer own them. He therefore asks posterity never to believe anything attributed to him that he has not published himself. He is not surprised at the extravagances ascribed to the ancient philosophers whose writings are lost; they were the best minds of their time and have simply been misreported. Their followers have hardly ever surpassed them: the most ardent Aristotelians would be happy to know as much about nature as Aristotle did, even on condition of never knowing more. They are like ivy, which never climbs higher than the trees that support it and often comes down after reaching the top; for such men come down too, becoming less learned than if they had not studied, when, not content to know what their author explains intelligibly, they seek in him solutions to difficulties he never mentions and perhaps never thought of. Their way of philosophising suits mediocre minds: the obscurity of their distinctions and principles lets them talk of everything as boldly as if they knew it and defend whatever they say against the subtlest opponents without being refuted, like a blind man who, to fight a sighted one on equal terms, has drawn him into a dark cellar. Such people have an interest in his not publishing his principles, since, being very simple and evident, publishing them would be like opening windows and letting daylight into the cellar. Even the best minds have no reason to want them. If they want to talk about everything and be thought learned, plausibility serves better than truth, which is found only gradually and forces one to admit ignorance of the rest. If they prefer a few truths to the vanity of seeming to know everything and wish to follow a design like his, they need nothing beyond what this discourse says: whoever can go further than he can find for himself all he has found, since, having examined everything in order, what remains is harder than what he has already found. They will enjoy finding it themselves more than learning it from him, and the habit gained by moving from easy things to harder ones will serve them better than his instructions. Had he been taught in youth all the truths whose demonstrations he later sought, he might never have learned any others, or at least never acquired the habit and facility of finding new ones. If any work can be finished only by the one who began it, it is his. Understanding requires reconstruction, not reception, the point Regulae X makes when it tells the reader to invent for himself. The critique of discipleship anticipates Cartesianism's own fate, beginning with the quarrel with Regius in 1647. Obscurity is a defence and clarity a weapon. The claim that a capable reader needs only the Discourse confirms that the method is meant to be enacted, not handed down.

(AT 72–74) One man cannot make all the experiments the work needs, but he cannot usefully employ hands other than his own, except those of craftsmen or people he can pay, whom hope of gain, a very effective means, would make carry out exactly what he prescribed. Volunteers who offer help from curiosity or love of learning usually promise more than they deliver, make fine proposals that never succeed, and would expect payment in explanations or in compliments and useless conversation that would cost him time. Experiments already made by others, even if they were willing to share them — those who call them secrets never would — are mostly so loaded with circumstances or superfluous ingredients that the truth is hard to extract, and so badly explained, or even falsified because their authors tried to make them fit their principles, that any useful ones would not repay the time spent choosing them. So if there were someone known to be capable of the greatest and most useful discoveries, and others wished by every means to help him, they could do nothing for him but pay for the experiments he needed and keep anyone from intruding on his leisure. He does not presume so much of himself as to promise anything extraordinary, nor imagine the public much concerned with his designs, nor is his spirit so low that he would accept any favour he might be thought not to deserve. Thinking cannot be shared, but manual labour can be bought, and the division of labour follows from that. The complaint that others' experiments are tailored to their principles is an early recognition that observation is shaped by theory. The appeal for funding is made and withdrawn in the same breath; refusing undeserved favour is a matter of self-respect, an early form of the Passions' generosity.

(AT 74–75) All these considerations together led him, three years ago, not to publish the treatise, and even to resolve to show during his lifetime no other work so general that the foundations of his physics could be learned from it. Since then two reasons have obliged him to include some particular essays here and give the public some account of his actions and designs. First, those who knew he once meant to publish might imagine his reasons for abstaining were more discreditable than they are. Though he does not love glory to excess, and even, if he may say so, hates it as contrary to the repose he values above all, he has never hidden his actions as crimes or taken many precautions to stay unknown, both because that would have wronged him and because it would have brought a disquiet contrary to the perfect tranquillity he seeks; and since, indifferent between being known and not, he could not avoid some reputation, he should at least avoid a bad one. The second reason is that, seeing his design of instructing himself delayed more each day by the countless experiments he needs and cannot make without help, he does not want those who survive him to reproach him for failing to tell them how they could contribute. The Essays are a compromise: specimens that show what the method can do without revealing its foundations. The Latin's word for them, specimina, which gave the 1644 volume its title, puts that function exactly. Publication is regulated by the value of repose, and even the management of reputation serves tranquillity. The second reason restates the request for collaborators in the form of a duty toward posterity.

(AT 75–76) He thought it easy to choose subjects that would not provoke much controversy or force him to reveal more of his principles than he wished, yet would show clearly enough what he can and cannot do in the sciences. He will not judge whether he succeeded, but he asks anyone with objections to send them to his bookseller; informed by him, he will try to append his reply, so that readers can judge the truth more easily with both before them. He will not write long replies, only confess his faults frankly if he sees them or, if not, say simply what is needed to defend what he wrote, without explaining any new matter, so as not to be drawn endlessly from one subject to the next. Optics, meteorology and geometry are chosen as relatively uncontroversial and detachable from first principles, which turns the Essays into a public test of the method. The procedure for objections and replies is the direct ancestor of the Meditations' apparatus and was put into practice at once in the exchanges with Morin, Fromondus and Plempius (1637–38). The refusal to open new matter in replies protects the order of exposition: answers must defend what is written, not advance the system.

(AT 76–77) If some things at the start of the Optics and the Meteorology shock at first because he calls them suppositions and seems unwilling to prove them, readers should go through the whole attentively. The reasons follow one another so that, as the last are demonstrated by the first, which are their causes, the first are demonstrated in turn by the last, which are their effects. This is not the circle logicians condemn: since experience makes most of these effects very certain, the causes from which he deduces them serve not so much to prove as to explain them, and it is rather the causes that are proved by the effects. He calls them suppositions only so that it is known he thinks he can deduce them from the first truths explained above but has deliberately not done so, to stop certain minds — who imagine they know in a day what another thought out in twenty years once told two or three words, and who err the more readily the more quick and penetrating they are — from building some extravagant philosophy on what they take for his principles and having it blamed on him. As for the opinions wholly his own, he does not excuse them as new: weighed properly, they will be found so simple and so consistent with common sense as to seem less extraordinary than any others on the same subjects. He does not claim to be the first to discover any of them, only that he never accepted them because others had said them or because they had not, but solely because reason persuaded him. The logic of hypotheses rests on two warrants running in opposite directions: experience certifies the effects, and the effects then confirm the causes, while the causes explain the effects without being needed to prove them. Since proving and explaining use different grounds, there is no vicious circle, as the letter to Morin of 13 July 1638 argues at length. The suppositions thus have a double status — hypothetical in the Essays, deducible in principle from the metaphysics — so the Essays can test the system without disclosing it. The disclaimer about originality makes reason the only authority: neither novelty nor tradition counts for or against an opinion.

(AT 77–78) If craftsmen cannot at once carry out the invention explained in the Optics, it should not be thought bad. Skill and practice are needed to make and adjust the machines he describes without missing any detail, and he would be no less surprised if they succeeded at the first attempt than if someone learned to play the lute excellently in a day from good tablature. If he writes in French, the language of his country, rather than Latin, that of his teachers, it is because he hopes those who use only their pure natural reason will judge his opinions better than those who believe only in ancient books; and those who join good sense with study, the only judges he wants, will not be so partial to Latin as to refuse his reasons because he gives them in the vernacular. Theory does not transmit craft. Knowing how to grind a hyperbolic lens is a separate competence, as the correspondence with the instrument-maker Ferrier (1629–32) had taught him. The choice of French addresses the good sense of the opening sentence and bypasses the authority of learned Latin culture, so the discourse ends with the reader it began with. The Latin version omits this whole passage (AT 583, editors' note). Addressed to the learned in their own language, it cannot repeat the argument, and the omission shows the passage belongs to the French text's mode of address, not to its doctrine.

(AT 78) He will not speak here of the progress he hopes to make, nor bind himself by any promise he is not sure of keeping. He says only that he has resolved to spend the rest of his life acquiring a knowledge of nature from which more reliable rules of medicine can be drawn than those we have, and that his inclination keeps him so far from every other design, above all those that can help some only by harming others, that if circumstances forced him into them he does not think he could succeed. He makes this declaration knowing it will not make him important in the world, which he has no wish to be, and he will always be more grateful to those whose favour lets him enjoy his leisure undisturbed than to those who might offer him the most honourable employments on earth. The close returns to medicine, the end Part Six assigned to the tree of the sciences, and repeats Part Three's choice of occupation as a public declaration. The designs that help some only by harming others are war, politics and the pursuit of office, the career of the soldier he once was. The last request is for leisure, the condition of inquiry stated at AT 73, so the book ends by asking only that the research be left free to continue.

## Optics

### First Discourse: On Light

(AT 81–83) The conduct of life depends on the senses, and sight, the most universal and noble of them, gains most from inventions that increase its power. None does so more than the telescope, which has already shown new stars in the sky and new objects on earth and seems to open the way to a far greater knowledge of nature than our ancestors had. To the shame of the sciences, it was found by experience and luck. About thirty years ago Jacob Metius of Alkmaar, an unschooled man who liked making mirrors and burning glasses, happened to look through a convex and a concave lens together and fitted them to the ends of a tube. Every telescope since has copied this pattern, and nobody has yet determined what figures the lenses ought to have. Because the work will be done by craftsmen, who are usually unlearned, Descartes will make himself intelligible to everyone and presuppose nothing from other sciences. He will explain light and its rays, describe the eye, show how vision occurs, list what could perfect it, and teach how instruments can supply it. The opening sentence ties the Optics to the motive of Part One, to see clearly in one's actions (AT 10). The complaint about chance invention states the Essay's ambition: to replace a lucky artefact with one derived from science, a first instance of the practical philosophy promised in Part Six. The plan follows the order of causes, from light to the organ to the art that perfects it. Addressing craftsmen who know no Latin or mathematics is the Discourse's choice of French carried into the treatise.

(AT 83) Since he speaks of light only to explain how its rays enter the eye and how bodies deflect them, he need not say what its nature truly is. Two or three comparisons will let us conceive it in the most convenient way for explaining the properties experience shows and deducing those less easily observed. He imitates the astronomers, whose suppositions are almost all false or uncertain yet yield many true and certain consequences because they fit the observations. The comparisons are models judged by their fit with phenomena, not claims about what light is, and the analogy with astronomers puts them in the register of "saving the appearances." Part Six adds the other half of the story: these suppositions could be deduced from first principles but are deliberately left undeduced (AT 76). So the instrumentalist language is partly a mask. Morin pressed the point in 1638, objecting that comparisons prove nothing, and Descartes replied that in physics comparisons drawn from sensible things are the most appropriate way to make insensible ones understood.

(AT 83–86) Walking at night without a torch, one uses a stick and feels through it the objects around — trees, stones, sand, water, grass, mud. The sensation is confused in those not used to it, but in people born blind it is so exact that one could almost say they see with their hands, or that the stick is the organ of a sixth sense. Light in luminous bodies, accordingly, is only a certain very quick and lively movement or action passing to our eyes through air and transparent bodies, as the motion or resistance of the bodies the blind man meets passes to his hand through the stick. This removes surprise at light spreading instantly from the sun to us, since a push on one end of a stick reaches the other at once, however long it is. Colours too may be nothing in coloured bodies but the different ways they receive light and send it back, just as the blind man's differences between trees, stones and water are only different ways of moving or resisting the stick. There is then no need to suppose that anything material passes from objects to the eyes, or that anything in objects resembles our ideas or sensations of them, and the mind is freed from the "intentional species" flying through the air that trouble the philosophers' imagination. The old dispute over whether the action of sight comes from the object or the eye is also settled: the blind man feels bodies both by their action on the stick and by his hand's action against them, so seeing by an action in the eyes is conceivable; but since that action would be light, only animals like cats, who see in the dark, have it, and men see by the action of objects, which must be lit to be seen. The stick does three jobs. It makes instantaneous transmission intelligible as action rather than transport; it turns colours into ways of acting on the medium; and it grounds the thesis of non-resemblance that Discourse IV generalises and The World opens with. Sight is remodelled on touch, as remote contact through a medium, which is the starting point of Merleau-Ponty's reading of the Optics in Eye and Mind. The settlement of the extramission dispute shows the method at work: both hypotheses fit the model, and experience (objects must be lit) decides between them.

(AT 86–88) Because the stick differs greatly from air, another comparison is needed. Take a vat at vintage full of half-trodden grapes, with one or two holes in its bottom, A and B, through which the new wine can run. Since nature contains no void, as almost all philosophers agree, and yet all bodies have pores, the pores must be filled with a very fine fluid matter extending without break from the stars to us. Compare this matter to the wine and the coarser parts of air and other transparent bodies to the grapes. The parts of wine near C tend to descend in a straight line through A the instant it is opened, and at the same time through B, as do those near D and E, without these tendencies hindering one another or being hindered by the grapes, which, propped on one another, do not tend to descend at all and may meanwhile be moved in other ways by those treading them. So all the parts of the fine matter touched by the sun's facing side tend in straight lines toward our eyes the instant they are open, unhindered by one another or by the coarser parts of transparent bodies, whether those move like air in the wind or are still like glass. One must distinguish motion from the action or inclination to move: the wine at C tends toward B and A at once, though it cannot move both ways at once, and tends exactly straight, though the grapes keep it from moving exactly straight. Since light is this action rather than motion, its rays are only the lines along which the action tends, and infinitely many cross from every point of luminous bodies to every point of the bodies they light, without interfering. Distinguishing tendency from motion makes rectilinear propagation possible in a full world: a pressure can tend along many lines at once where a moving particle could follow only one. The concept returns as the conatus of Principles III.55–57. The plenum is introduced through a near-consensus of philosophers, which avoids having to prove it here. Rays become geometrical lines of tendency, not physical things, and that is what makes a purely geometrical optics possible.

(AT 88–93) Rays must be imagined exactly straight while they pass through one uniform transparent body, but other bodies deflect or weaken them as they deflect or stop a thrown ball, since the action or inclination to move must follow the same laws as motion. The third comparison works this out. Soft bodies such as cloth, sand or mud stop a ball; hard ones send it back, in different ways depending on whether the surface is even or uneven, flat or curved, and whether its unevenness lies only in variously curved parts or also in angles, parts of unequal hardness, or parts in motion. A ball can also acquire a second motion, a rotation about its centre, in varying proportion to its straight motion, as tennis players know when they "cut" the ball or it strikes uneven tiles. Balls striking a smooth surface rebound in the same order; striking an uneven one, they scatter; entering a liquid obliquely, they change course. Correspondingly, bodies called black deaden rays; polished ones reflect them in order, as mirrors; others scatter them, either without altering their action (white bodies) or with a change like the spin given to a cut ball (red, yellow, blue and other colours). Descartes thinks he can determine the nature of each colour and show it by experiment, but that lies beyond his subject. Rays falling on coloured, unpolished bodies are reflected in all directions, so a sheet of paper lit by one torch can be seen from anywhere, even through it. Rays entering a transparent body obliquely are deflected, and this is refraction. The transfer of the laws of motion to a mere tendency is the model's real premise, granted as "easy to believe." The comparisons do not form one consistent picture taken literally: the stick transmits instantly, while the ball's speed will change from medium to medium in Discourse II. Each models a different property and none is meant as a description of light. The ratio of spin to straight motion as the essence of colour is worked out in Meteorology VIII, where it explains the rainbow.

### Second Discourse: On Refraction

(AT 93–96) To measure refraction exactly, he first treats reflection with the ball. A ball sent from A strikes at B a perfectly flat, hard ground CBE and turns aside; assume it keeps the same speed, and set aside what keeps it moving after the racket, its weight, size and shape, which have no counterpart in the action of light. The force that keeps the ball moving differs from what determines it to move one way rather than another: the racket's force could have moved it in any direction, while the racket's position directs it toward B. So the ground can change the ball's determination without changing the force of its motion, and there is no need to suppose, as several philosophers do, that the ball stops for a moment at B, since once interrupted its motion would have no cause to start again. A determination can be divided, like motion and every quantity, into parts: the ball's is composed of one carrying it down from line AF to CE and one carrying it rightward from AC to FE. The ground can oppose only the first. Draw a circle about B through A and three equidistant perpendiculars AC, HB, FE: in the time taken from A to B the ball must return to a point on the circle and advance as far right as before, which it can do only at F. The angle of reflection therefore equals the angle of incidence. The distinction between the force of motion (a scalar, the speed) and its determination (a direction that can be resolved into components) does all the work and anticipates the treatment of collision in Principles II. The argument against the instant of rest relies on the persistence of motion that Part Five derived from God's immutability. Fermat and later Hobbes attacked the decomposition: what entitles one component of the determination to keep its size while the other is cancelled?

(AT 96–98) For refraction, suppose the ball at B meets a cloth so thin that it breaks through, losing say half its speed. Again motion and determination must be weighed separately. Of the two parts of the determination, only the downward one can be affected by the cloth; the rightward one stays the same, since the cloth does not oppose it. Draw the circle about B and perpendiculars AC, HB, FE with FE twice as far from HB as HB is from AC. Having lost half its speed, the ball takes twice as long below the cloth to reach the circle as it took above to go from A to B; keeping its rightward determination intact, it advances twice as far right in that time, and so it reaches the circle where it meets FE, at I. The hidden premise is that the parallel component of velocity keeps its absolute magnitude while total speed changes. That is what Fermat's objection of 1637 fastened on: "determination" here carries a quantity measured by speed, so the separation between force and determination is less clean than the reflection case suggested.

(AT 98–99) Now let the ball meet water, whose surface takes away half its speed as the cloth did. It is deflected toward I in the same way, and the rest of the water through which it passes does not deflect it further, since water opens as easily in one direction as another, provided weight, size, shape and the like are ignored. The more obliquely the ball strikes, the more it is deflected; striking perpendicularly, it goes straight on; and if it strikes so obliquely that FE no longer meets the circle, it cannot penetrate at all but rebounds as from earth — which people have learned to their cost when cannon fired into a river for sport wounded those on the far bank. All the refraction happens at the surface; inside, the medium is isotropic. The ricochet case falls out of the construction as a limit at which no intersection exists, the model's counterpart of total reflection, which the account of light will need.

(AT 99–100) A further supposition: at B a racket strikes the ball again and increases its force by a third, so that it then covers in two moments what it covered in three. This has the same effect as meeting at B a body it passes through a third more easily than air. By what has been shown, placing FE a third closer to HB than HB is to AC gives the point I toward which the ball turns. Here the ball bends toward the perpendicular, the case light presents on entering water or glass. The racket makes the "easier passage" of a medium concrete as an added push at the boundary.

(AT 100) The conclusion can be reversed. If a ball moving straight from A to B turns at B toward I, the force or ease with which it enters the body CBEI is to that with which it leaves ACBE as the distance between AC and HB is to that between HB and FI, that is, as CB to BE. This inverse problem turns a path into a measurement: from the geometry of incidence and refraction one reads off a ratio characterising the two media. The ratio of these perpendicular distances, taken in a circle of fixed radius, is the ratio of sines, so the law is already in place here.

(AT 100–101) Since the action of light follows the same laws as the ball's motion, its rays passing obliquely from one transparent body into another that receives them more or less easily are deflected so as always to be less inclined to the surface on the side of the body that receives them more easily, exactly in proportion to how much more easily it does so. Inclination must be measured by straight lines such as CB or AH and EB or IG compared with each other, not by angles such as ABH or GBI, still less by the angles of refraction such as DBI. The ratio between angles varies with the inclination of the rays, while the ratio between lines such as AH and IG stays the same in all refractions between the same bodies: for rays AB, KB and PR refracted into glass, KM is to LN and PQ to ST as AH is to IG, but the angles do not keep the same ratio. This is the sine law, stated as a constant ratio of perpendiculars in a circle, against the ancient tables and Kepler's approximations, which worked with the angles themselves. Snell had found the law by about 1621 without publishing it, and Huygens later suspected Descartes of borrowing it. Descartes offers it as derived from the model rather than read off measurements.

(AT 101–102) This shows how refractions should be measured. Their quantity depends on the particular bodies and must be found by experience, but it can be found easily and with certainty once all refractions are brought under one measure. One ray suffices to know all refractions at a given surface, and testing a few more rays rules out error: for the surface between air and glass, measure the ratio of AH to IG for ray ABI, then confirm with KBL and PRS. Here is the role of experiment in Cartesian physics as Part Six describes it (AT 64–65). The form of the law comes from the model; experience supplies a constant and repeated trials check it. A single measurement fixes a parameter instead of building a law by induction.

(AT 102–103) One may be surprised to find that rays bend more toward the surface in air than in water, and more in water than in glass, the opposite of a ball, which bends more in water than in air and cannot enter glass at all. A ball sent from A to B on the water surface CBE turns toward V; a ray turns the other way. The surprise ends if one remembers that light is only a movement or action received in a very fine matter filling the pores of other bodies. A ball loses more of its motion striking a soft body than a hard one and rolls less easily on a carpet than on a bare table; likewise the action of the fine matter is impeded much more by the parts of air, which are soft and loosely joined, than by those of water, and more by water than by glass or crystal. The harder and firmer the small parts of a transparent body, the more easily they let light pass, since light does not have to push them out of place as a ball must push aside the parts of water. Here the ball analogy is reversed to fit experience. This consequence of the model — light passing "more easily" in denser media — was the point Fermat's least-time derivation of 1662 contradicted, and Foucault's measurement of 1850 decided against it.

(AT 103–105) Refractions in water, glass and the transparent bodies around us must be the same on the way in and on the way out: if a ray from A bends at B toward I entering glass, one returning from I to B bends toward A. There may be other bodies, especially in the heavens, where refractions arise from other causes and are not reciprocal, and cases where rays curve within a single transparent body, as a ball's path curves under weight and impulse together. The three comparisons are so apt that every particular they show corresponds to something in light, though he has explained only what bears on his subject. Finally, curved surfaces bend each ray as the flat surface touching them at that point would; rays from a torch A falling on a crystal ball are refracted as by the tangent planes at B, C and D, so curvature makes rays converge or diverge. The tangent-plane principle reduces the optics of lenses to plane refraction plus geometry, which is the programme of Discourse VIII. The claim that every particular of the comparisons corresponds to something in light overreaches, given the conflict between the instantaneous stick and the ball's variable speed. It shows that Descartes takes the models to be more than convenient fictions.

### Third Discourse: On the Eye

(AT 105–108) If the eye could be cut in half without spilling its fluids, the section through the pupil would show the following. A hard, thick outer skin forms a round vessel; its front part is transparent and a little more convex than the rest. A thin second skin lines it like a tapestry. The optic nerve's many fibres spread over the back to form, with veins and arteries, a very tender third skin. Three clear humours fill the inside: the middle one, the crystalline, refracts about as much as glass, and the other two somewhat less, about as much as water. The second skin is black inside and has in front a small round hole, the pupil, which floats in the liquid front humour and acts like a small muscle, narrowing for near objects, bright light, or close attention to detail, as one can see in a child's eye. This movement should be called voluntary even though those who make it usually do not know it, because it follows their will to see well, just as the movements of lips and tongue in speech are voluntary though we often do not know what they must be for each letter. Small black fibres around the crystalline, like tendons, make it more or less convex according to whether we mean to look at near or distant things, changing the shape of the whole eye; after staring at a distant tower one cannot read a book held up until this change occurs. Six or seven external muscles move the eye and perhaps help change its shape. Many other anatomical details are deliberately omitted as useless for the subject. The anatomy is selected by function: only what refracts light or regulates it is kept. Calling the pupil's movement voluntary separates willing an effect from knowing its mechanism, the model of Passions §44, where we move the gland by willing the result rather than the motion. Accommodation by a change in the lens's curvature, against Kepler's suggestion that the lens moves, is the explanation Helmholtz confirmed in the nineteenth century.

### Fourth Discourse: On the Senses in General

(AT 109–114) Before treating sight in particular he says something about the senses in general. It is the soul that senses, not the body, since in ecstasy or deep contemplation the body feels nothing though objects touch it. The soul senses not insofar as it is in the organs of the external senses but insofar as it is in the brain, where it exercises the faculty called the common sense, since injuries to the brain alone abolish all sensation while the body remains alive. Impressions reach the soul by the nerves, since damage to one nerve removes feeling from the parts it serves. Nerves contain three things: membranes derived from those around the brain, forming small branching tubes; an inner substance of fine independent fibres running from the brain to the extremities; and animal spirits, a very subtle wind flowing from the brain's cavities through the tubes into the muscles. Physicians, seeing nerves serve both sense and motion and finding paralyses that remove motion but not feeling, have supposed two kinds of nerves, or put feeling in the membranes and motion in the inner substance, both contrary to experience and reason: no nerve serves motion without serving some sense, and membranes could not carry the variety of impressions. The spirits cause motion by inflating the muscles, and the fibres serve sensation. Held taut inside tubes kept open by the spirits, a fibre touched at one end moves the corresponding place in the brain at the same instant, as a stretched cord does, and it can be finer than silk without breaking. Nor should we suppose, as philosophers do, that the soul must contemplate images sent from objects to the brain — or at least we must conceive them quite differently. Considering only their resemblance to objects, the philosophers cannot explain how such images are formed, received and transmitted, and their only reason for positing them is that a painting excites our thought to conceive its object. But many things besides images excite thought, such as signs and words, which in no way resemble what they signify. If we keep the language of images, no image can resemble its object in everything, or there would be no difference between them; resemblance in a few respects is enough, and an image is often more perfect for resembling less. Copperplate engravings, a little ink on paper, represent forests, towns, men, battles and storms while resembling them only in shape, and imperfectly even there, since perspective represents circles better by ovals and squares by rhombs. The same holds for the images formed in the brain: the question is how they enable the soul to sense the qualities of their objects, not how they resemble them. The blind man's objects send him nothing but movements of his stick, and his soul senses as many qualities as there are differences in the movements caused in his brain. This is the Optics' central theory of perception. The paradigm of representation shifts from picture to sign, and perspective proves the point, since fidelity of representation can require dissimilarity. Words are the model for sensory signification here as in the first chapter of The World and Principles IV.197. The cord-fibre account of the nerve completes the chain begun with the stick: perception is a series of instantaneous actions, and nothing of the object travels. Locating sensation in the brain rather than the organ fixes the site of union. Merleau-Ponty's Eye and Mind takes this passage on engravings as the emblem of Cartesian vision: a thought of seeing that reads signs rather than a vision that sees.

### Fifth Discourse: On the Images Formed at the Back of the Eye

(AT 114–115) The soul does not need to contemplate images resembling what it senses; but objects do imprint fairly perfect images at the back of our eyes. Some have explained this ingeniously by comparison with the images in a room shut except for one hole fitted with a lens, which light from outside forms on a white sheet behind it: the room is the eye, the hole the pupil, the lens the crystalline humour or rather all the refracting parts, the sheet the inner skin made of the optic nerve's endings. The retinal image is kept as a physical fact while denied any role as the thing perceived; it is a stage in a causal chain, not an object for an inner viewer. The unnamed "some" are Kepler (1604) and Scheiner. Discourse VI's first sentence follows directly from this distinction.

(AT 115–117) You can be still more certain by taking the eye of a man just dead, or failing that of an ox or other large animal. Carefully cut away the three skins at the back so that much of the rear humour is exposed but none spills, cover it with something white and thin enough to let light through, such as paper or eggshell, and set the eye in a hole made in a shutter, its front facing sunlit objects and its back facing a dark room. On the white surface you will see, perhaps with wonder and pleasure, a picture representing in perspective all the objects outside, provided the eye keeps its natural shape, proportioned to their distance: squeezed a little more or less, the picture becomes less distinct, and it must be squeezed a little more, lengthening the eye, for very near objects. The experiment turns the analogy into a demonstration on the organ itself. Lengthening the dead eye to focus on near objects reproduces artificially the change of shape that Discourse III attributed to accommodation. The word "picture" (peinture) recalls the engravings of Discourse IV: here is a picture with nobody inside the eye to look at it.

(AT 117–121) From each point of the objects as many rays enter as the pupil admits, and the refractions at the three surfaces bring those from one point together again near one point. For the picture to be as perfect as possible, all rays from one point should meet at one point — those from X at S, from V at R, from Y at T — and conversely no rays should reach S except from X. Given what was said of light and colour, the white surface must then show the likeness of the objects: light pushed by object V, supposed red — that is, disposed to make the fine matter's parts spin in the proportion that produces the sensation of red — reaches R, and the white body, which returns light in every direction unchanged, sends it to your eyes as red; likewise yellow at S and blue at T, in the same order as V, X, Y. The picture's perfection depends on three things. The pupil must have some size, so that many rays come from each point, since a single ray would be too weak. The refractions must gather the rays from different points onto different points; otherwise each point of the sheet would receive rays from V, X and Y and show a colour mixed of red, yellow and blue. And the black lining and dark chamber must exclude other light. Refraction too strong or too weak for the eye's size makes the rays meet before or beyond the sheet and blurs the picture. "Likeness" here means a correspondence of points and of order, not a sharing of qualities, consistent with Discourse IV. The analysis into point-to-point gathering, intensity and exclusion of stray light already gives the requirements of Discourse VII. Blur is explained as a mixture of signals arriving at one receiving point.

(AT 121–124) The picture also has defects. Whatever the shapes of the eye's parts, they cannot make rays from all points meet in as many distinct points; the best they can do is gather one point's rays exactly, say X's at S in the middle, while those from V and Y meet only approximately. The picture is therefore never as distinct at its edges as in the middle, which is why writers on optics speak of an axis of vision through the centres of the crystalline and pupil. A larger pupil spreads each point's rays more, making the colours stronger but the figures less distinct, so its size must be moderate; and objects nearer or farther than the distance to which the eye is adjusted also blur. The demonstrations of this will follow once the proper figures of transparent bodies are known. The picture's other defects are that it is inverted and that its parts are diminished and foreshortened according to distance and position, as in a perspective painting: a smaller but nearer object takes the same room as V, and the straight line VXY is represented by the curve RST. The trade-off between aperture and distinctness makes the eye a compromise instrument, which leaves art something to add. Inversion, foreshortening and curvature are listed as defects of likeness, yet none of them impairs vision. That fact is the strongest argument that seeing does not work by inspecting a likeness, and Discourse VI draws the conclusion.

(AT 124) Having seen this picture in a dead animal's eye and understood its causes, one cannot doubt that a similar one forms in a living man's eye on the inner skin, and even better, since the humours, full of spirits, are clearer and more exactly shaped; the ox's pupil, not being round, may also spoil the picture somewhat. The inference from the dead organ to the living one runs a fortiori, conceding the differences and turning them in its favour. Cartesian physiology works this way throughout, as the heart of Part Five showed, treating the dissected organ as the working model of the living one.

(AT 124–128) Images on a white sheet in a dark room form in the same way and for the same reason; being larger and producible in more ways, they make several particulars easier to observe. Without a lens, images appear only if the hole is very narrow, and they are confused, the more so the wider the hole; their size is to the distance from the hole as the object's size is to its distance. With a lens there is one distance at which the images are sharp, measured from the lens, not the hole; it depends on the lens's figure and on the objects' distance, the sheet going farther back for a flatter lens or nearer objects; and on it the size of the images depends. With a lens the hole can be much larger without much loss of sharpness, and the larger it is the brighter the images: covering part of the lens darkens them without shrinking them. The larger and brighter the images, the better they are seen, so an eye of great depth and a wide pupil, with refracting surfaces proportioned to that size, would form more visible images. Several fairly flat lenses placed together act like one as convex as both, while lenses set at certain distances can right the image the first inverted, and so on. These things are easily deduced from what has been said, and they will be "more your own" if they cost you a little reflection. The camera serves as a laboratory for the eye, and its findings prepare the instruments. The imagined deeper eye is the principle of the telescope, which Discourse VII builds as an artificial lengthening of the eye. The appeal to work things out for oneself repeats the Discourse's view that what one finds oneself is understood better (AT 69).

(AT 128–130) The images do not stop at the back of the eye but pass on to the brain. Rays from V touch at R the end of an optic fibre that starts at point 7 on the brain's inner surface; those from X move the fibre S8, and those from Y the point 9. Since light is only a movement or action tending to cause movement, these rays move the whole fibre and its point in the brain, and the fibres reached by differently coloured objects are moved in different ways. So a picture 789 forms again on the inner surface of the brain facing its cavities, fairly similar to the objects. Descartes could follow it to a small gland near the middle of these cavities, the proper seat of the common sense, and even show how it sometimes passes from there through the arteries of a pregnant woman to a particular limb of her child, forming the birthmarks the learned so admire. This is the first published mention of the gland, whose role was worked out in the unpublished Treatise on Man. The material image is followed right up to the seat of the common sense, and the next discourse at once denies that perception consists in looking at it. The remark on maternal impressions takes over a popular marvel for mechanism and shows how far the model is meant to reach.

### Sixth Discourse: On Vision

(AT 130–132) Although the picture passing into the head keeps some resemblance to its objects, we must not think it makes us sense them by that resemblance, as if there were other eyes in the brain to see it. Rather, the movements composing it act immediately on the soul, insofar as the soul is united to the body, and are instituted by nature to give it such sensations. All the qualities we perceive in visible objects reduce to six: light, colour, situation, distance, size and figure. Light and colour alone belong properly to sight. The soul's nature is such that the force of the movements in the parts of the brain where the optic fibres begin makes it sense light, and their manner makes it sense colour, as the movements of the auditory nerves make it hear sounds, those of the tongue taste flavours, and those of the nerves of the whole body feel tickling when moderate and pain when violent, without any resemblance between the ideas it conceives and the movements that cause them. Two facts confirm this. A blow to the eye makes one see countless flashes even with closed eyes or in the dark, so the sensation can be due only to the force of the blow moving the optic fibres as a strong light would; the same force on the ears would produce a sound, elsewhere pain. And after looking at the sun one sees, with closed eyes, colours that change as they fade, because the fibres, strongly moved, cannot stop at once, and their weakening agitation represents weaker colours, which shows that colour consists only in a diversity of motion. Colours also appear in transparent bodies where nothing could cause them but the way light is received, as in the rainbow or a faceted glass. The phrase "instituted by nature" carries the theory: the link between motion and sensation is fixed but not based on resemblance, as a sign is linked to what it means. It reappears as the teaching of nature in the Sixth Meditation and in Principles IV.197. The objection to "other eyes in the brain" is the first statement of the regress argument against picture theories of perception. The phosphene argument — one stimulus, different sensations depending on the nerve — anticipates Johannes Müller's law of specific nerve energies (1826).

(AT 132–134) The quantity of light seen, that is, the force with which each optic fibre is moved, is not equal to the light in the objects. It varies with their distance, the size of the pupil, and the area that each point's rays occupy at the back of the eye. A near eye with a narrow pupil may receive as many rays from a point as a farther eye with a wide one; and if the same rays spread over a smaller area in one eye than another, they act more strongly on each fibre — if one image covers four thousand fibres and the other one thousand, each fibre receives a thousandth of the total force in one and a quarter of a thousandth in the other. We distinguish the parts of things only by differences of colour, and distinct vision depends not only on the rays from each point meeting at one point but also on the number of fibres in the area the image occupies: if an object has ten thousand parts disposed to show ten thousand colours and the image covers only a thousand fibres, at most a thousand colours can be distinguished, since the parts acting together on one fibre move it in only one way and each fibre counts as one point. That is why a meadow painted with countless colours looks all white or all blue from afar, why distant things are less distinct, and why the larger the image of an object at the back of the eye, the more distinctly it is seen — which will matter later. The retina is treated as discrete, each fibre a unit that registers one motion, and resolving power is set by the density of receptors. This gives magnification its rationale, since enlarging the image spreads the object over more fibres. The trade-off between image area and force per fibre, computed here, is the reason Discourse VII must widen the aperture of the telescope.

(AT 134–137) We perceive situation — the direction of each part of the object relative to our body — no differently through the eyes than through the hands. Knowing it depends on no image or action from the object, only on the position of the small parts of the brain where the nerves begin. That position changes slightly whenever the limbs in which the nerves end change theirs, and it is instituted by nature to let the soul know not only where each part of the body it animates lies relative to the others, but also to transfer its attention to all the places on the straight lines that can be imagined extending from each part to infinity. The blind man, turning his hand A or C toward E, knows through the change in his brain not only the place of his hand but every place on line AE or CE, so that he can direct his attention to objects B and D and locate them without knowing or thinking of where his hands are. So when eye or head turns, the soul is informed by the nerves of the muscles that turn them; and the position of each optic fibre, at R, S or T, is matched by a position of a brain part, 7, 8 or 9, that lets the soul know all the places on lines RV, SX or TY. It is therefore no wonder that objects are seen in their true position though the picture in the eye is inverted, just as the blind man feels B on his right with his left hand and D on his left with his right, the sticks being crossed. And as he does not judge a body double when touching it with both hands, our two eyes, directed so as to bring attention to one place, show one object though a picture forms in each. The inversion problem disappears once resemblance plays no part, since the orientation of the image is irrelevant to how its information is read. Location is given by the body's own sense of its posture plus a projection of attention along lines, the cane again, a structure that later phenomenology would call the body schema. Single vision with two eyes follows from the same convergence of attention on one place.

(AT 137–140) Distance is not seen by any images from objects either. It is perceived first through the shape of the eye: as we alter that shape to suit the object's distance, we alter a part of the brain in a way instituted by nature to make the soul perceive the distance, usually without any reflection, as we shape our hand to a body we grasp without thinking of its movements. Second, through the relation between the two eyes: as the blind man holding two sticks of unknown length, knowing only the interval between his hands and the angles at them, can know "as by a natural geometry" where the point E is, so when both eyes turn toward X, the length of the line between them and the two angles tell us where X is. We can do the same with one eye by moving it: the line and angles then come together in our imagination and make us perceive the distance "by an action of thought which, though only a simple imagination, implicitly contains a reasoning" like that of surveyors measuring inaccessible places from two stations. Third, through the distinctness or confusion of the figure together with the strength or weakness of the light: rays from objects nearer or farther than the fixation point meet less exactly, and stronger light makes us judge an object nearer. Finally, prior knowledge of an object's size, position, distinctness of figure and colour, or strength of light helps us not properly to see its distance but to imagine it: we judge better the distance of a familiar body, a sunlit mountain beyond a shaded forest is judged farther because of the forest, and of two ships at sea that look equal we can judge which is farther by their figures, colours and light. Three levels can be distinguished: signs instituted by nature, such as accommodation, which work without thought; a "natural geometry," an act of thought that implicitly contains an inference; and judgments learned from experience, which make us imagine distance rather than see it. The middle level is the contested one. It is an act of the soul, yet it is not experienced as reasoning, and Malebranche's "natural judgments" and Berkeley's New Theory of Vision (1709) both start from this ambiguity. The Latin settles it toward the intellect: the blind man knows where E is "as by a geometry innate in all" (ex Geometria quadam omnibus innata), which makes the inference an exercise of innate mathematical knowledge rather than a habit. The Sixth Replies' three grades of sense — bodily motion, the immediate perception, and the judgment habitually joined to it — systematise this passage.

(AT 140–141) Size and figure need no separate treatment, being contained in the way we see distance and the position of parts. Size is estimated by our knowledge or opinion of the distance compared with the size of the images printed in the eye, not by the images' size alone: images a hundred times larger when the object is near than when it is ten times farther away do not make it look a hundred times larger, but nearly the same, unless we misjudge the distance. Figure is judged by our knowledge or opinion of the position of the object's parts, not by the resemblance of the pictures in the eye, which usually contain ovals and rhombs when they make us see circles and squares. Perceptual constancy of size and shape is explained as the work of judgment, which corrects the retinal data by an estimate of distance and orientation. Because the estimate is "knowledge or opinion," it can be wrong, which the next paragraph uses. The ovals and rhombs return from Discourse IV: the perspective engraving was a model of the eye's own working.

(AT 141–147) To leave no doubt that vision works this way, he considers why it sometimes deceives us. First, since the soul sees, not the eye, and sees only through the brain, the frenzied and the sleeping often see, or think they see, objects not before their eyes, when vapours dispose the parts of the brain used in vision as those objects would. Second, since external impressions reach the common sense through the nerves, if a nerve's position is forced by some unusual cause, objects appear in places other than their own: an eye pushed by a finger shows objects shifted and, together with the other eye, doubled, just as crossed fingers touching one small ball seem to touch two. Third, being used to judging that the impressions moving our sight come from the places we must look toward, we are deceived when they come otherwise: the jaundiced, or those looking through yellow glass, see everything yellow; the man in the dark room attributes the objects' colours to the white sheet; and lenses and mirrors make objects seem displaced, larger or smaller, or inverted — which shows how the ancients erred in locating images in concave and convex mirrors. All our means of knowing distance are also very uncertain. The eye's shape hardly varies beyond four or five feet; the angles between the eyes, or between two stations of one eye, hardly vary at any distance; and our common sense seems unable to receive the idea of a distance greater than about one or two hundred feet. Hence the sun and moon, whose diameters are to their distance about as one to a hundred, look only a foot or two across, though we know by reason that they are extremely large and distant — not because we cannot conceive larger things, for we conceive towers and mountains, but because we cannot conceive them farther away. Position helps deceive us too: the sun and moon look smaller high at noon than at rising or setting, when objects between them and us make their distance more noticeable, and astronomers measuring them find the same angle in both cases, which shows that the old axiom that apparent size is proportional to the visual angle is not always true. White or luminous bodies look somewhat nearer and larger than they are. They look nearer because the contraction of the pupil against their light is so joined to the movement adjusting the eye for near objects that one rarely happens without the other, as the third finger bends a little when the first two close. They look larger because each optic fibre, though tiny, has some width and can move in only one way at a time, so when part of it is touched by a very bright object it follows that motion entirely; a star's image therefore spreads over the six fibres around the one it strikes, perhaps over the next twelve too, making stars look larger than their distance warrants, and round even if they are not, as a square tower looks round from afar. Perspective paintings show how easily distance is misjudged from size, figure, colour or light. Each illusion is predicted by the mechanism just given, so the errors confirm the theory: a model proves itself by explaining where vision fails. The case of dreams and frenzy returns to Part Four's dream (AT 38–39) with a physiological cause. The limit on the distance the common sense can represent separates what the bodily imagination can picture from what reason knows, which is exactly the contrast of the Third Meditation's two ideas of the sun, one small and from the senses, the other vast and from astronomy. The moon illusion is explained by judged distance, against the visual-angle axiom of ancient optics. Irradiation is explained by the finite width of the fibres, a consequence of the discreteness argued at AT 133–134.

### Seventh Discourse: On the Means of Perfecting Vision

(AT 147–149) Having examined how vision occurs, he gathers the conditions of its perfection so as to see what nature has already provided and make an exact enumeration of what remains for art. Three things matter: the objects, the internal organs that receive their actions, and the external organs that dispose those actions to be received properly. Of objects it is enough to know that some are near or accessible and others far and inaccessible, some more lit than others: accessible ones can be moved and lit as we please, inaccessible ones not at all. Of the internal organs, the nerves and brain, art can add nothing to their structure, since we cannot make ourselves a new body, and whatever physicians can do lies outside the subject. There remain the external organs: the transparent parts of the eye and any bodies placed between it and the object. What they must secure reduces to four points. First, the rays reaching each end of the optic nerve should come, as far as possible, from one part of the object only and be unchanged on the way, or the images will be neither similar to their original nor distinct. Second, the images should be very large — not in area, since the back of the eye is small, but in the scale of their lines, which are easier to distinguish the larger they are. Third, the rays should be strong enough to move the optic fibres and be felt, but not so strong as to harm sight. Fourth, the images of as many objects as possible should form at once, so that as many as possible are seen at a glance. The discourse applies the fourth precept explicitly — "an exact enumeration" — and its threefold division of objects, internal organs and external organs secures completeness before any solution is proposed. The four conditions translate the analyses of Discourses V and VI into design requirements: point-to-point correspondence, scale for the sake of the fibres, intensity, and field.

(AT 149–152) Nature has provided for the first condition in several ways. She filled the eye with clear, colourless fluids so that actions from outside reach the back unaltered. By the refractions at their surfaces she made the rays from one point gather at one point on the nerve, and those from other points at as many other points, as exactly as possible — "for we must suppose that nature has done everything possible here, since experience shows us nothing to the contrary." To reduce the unavoidable defect she let the pupil narrow almost as much as the light allows; she dyed black all the non-transparent parts facing the nerve so that no other rays reach those points; and by changing the eye's shape she made rays from objects at different distances still gather as exactly as possible. She has not fully provided for this last point. None of us can focus on objects a finger's breadth away, and some people's eyes are suited only to distant things, which happens mostly to the old, others' only to near things, which is commoner in the young, so that eyes seem to start a little long and narrow and grow flatter and wider with age. To remedy this by art we must find lens surfaces that bend rays from one point so that they are disposed as if they came from a nearer point, for the short-sighted, or a farther one, for the old and for anyone wanting to see objects nearer than their eyes allow. Since many figures can do this, we should choose the simplest to describe and cut, and those that make rays from the object's other points enter the eye approximately as if from other points. "Approximately" is deliberate: determining exactly the best of infinitely many figures would be hard and useless, since the eye itself does not gather rays exactly and its precise figure cannot be known. And in placing anything before our eyes we should imitate nature in everything she observed in making them and lose none of her advantages except to gain a more important one. The presumption that nature has done all that is possible rests on the absence of contrary experience, not on final causes, which Principles I.28 banishes from physics. It is a working rule of optimality, an expectation that experience could overturn. Design is constrained by craft, preferring figures simple enough to cut, and by ignorance of the eye's exact figure, so precision beyond what can be used is rejected as idle. "Imitate nature" is offered as an engineering rule, not a piety.

(AT 152–155) The size of the images depends only on three things: the distance between the object and the place where the rays from its different points cross on the way to the back of the eye, the distance from that place to the back of the eye, and the refraction of the rays. The image would be larger if the object were nearer the crossing point, if the eye were longer, or if refraction bent the rays outward instead of inward; nothing else can enlarge it. The third is negligible, since it can enlarge the image only slightly and with great difficulty, and nature has neglected it, bending the rays inward and so making the image a little smaller. The first applies only to accessible objects: the closer we look, the larger the images. Nature lets us look no closer than about half a foot to a foot, so art can only interpose a lens like P that makes rays from a very near point enter the eye as if from a farther one. At most this cuts the distance to a twelfth or fifteenth, and since the rays begin crossing at the lens rather than the eye, the image's diameter becomes twelve or fifteen times larger and its area about two hundred times, so the object is seen about two hundred times more distinctly. It also looks much larger, not exactly two hundred times but more or less according to the distance at which we judge it: if the eye is adjusted as for an object twenty or thirty paces away and, knowing nothing else of where X is, we judge it to be thirty paces off, it will look more than a million times larger, and a flea will become an elephant, since the image a flea makes so close is no smaller than an elephant's at thirty paces. This alone is the basis of the single-lens "flea glasses" in common use, though the true figure they should have is still unknown; and because one knows the object is very near when using them, it does not look as large as it would if imagined farther away. Magnification is split into two effects. The physical gain is the larger retinal image, which brings distinctness by engaging more fibres (AT 133–134). Apparent size is a judgment and varies with the distance attributed to the object. The flea-elephant example applies Discourse VI directly: the same retinal image yields a different perceived size depending on judged distance.

(AT 155–157) The one remaining way to enlarge images is to make the rays from the object's different points cross as far as possible from the back of the eye. It is by far the most important, since it alone serves for inaccessible objects as well as accessible ones, and its effect has no limit: images can be enlarged indefinitely. Since the eye's front humour refracts about as much as water, place against the eye a tube full of water ending in a lens shaped like the skin covering that humour and in the same proportion to the eye's depth. There will then be no refraction at the eye's entrance; it will occur at the tube's entrance, where the rays will cross and form an image all the larger the longer the tube, as if nature had made the eye longer by the tube's whole length. The real pupil then becomes useless and even harmful, since by its narrowness it excludes rays that could reach the sides of the back of the eye. The slightly different refractions in the glass do not matter, since a glass of uniform thickness straightens at its second surface what its first bends, which is also why he ignored the refractions of the skins enclosing the eye's humours. The telescope is conceived as a prosthesis that lengthens the eye, the "deeper eye" of AT 127 made real. The claim of unlimited magnification in principle means that art, guided by reason, can go beyond the limits nature fixed. The water tube is a thought-model meant to isolate the principle, and the next paragraph sets it aside as impractical.

(AT 157–159) Joining water to the eye would be very inconvenient, and since the exact figure of the skin over the eye cannot be known, the lens to replace it cannot be determined. It is better to use one or more lenses enclosed in a tube but not joined to the eye, with some air between. At the tube's entrance they should bend the rays from each point of the object toward a point where the middle of the back of the eye will be when the tube is in place; at its exit they should straighten them so that they enter the eye as if they had not been bent at all but came from a nearer place; and rays from different points, having crossed at the entrance, should not uncross at the exit but go to the eye as if from a larger or nearer object. If the tube were filled with solid glass, its first surface would make all rays from X tend toward S, and its concave second surface would bend them again so that they reach the eye as if from a point x placed so that xC is to CS as XH is to HS. Rays from V would cross at the first surface, and the concave second surface could not bring them back together, so the image would grow with the tube's length, and the figure of the eye's surface would not need to be known. The design separates the instrument from the eye's unknown parameters: the tube is built to deliver rays in a standard state, as if from a nearer point, which any eye can then handle. The concave second surface places the design in the Galilean lineage, the pattern of Metius's instrument.

(AT 159) Since it would again be inconvenient to find glass thick enough to fill the tube and clear enough not to block the light, the tube can be left empty with a lens at each end doing what the two surfaces were to do. This alone is the basis of the telescopes made of two lenses at the ends of a tube, "which gave me the occasion to write this treatise." The deduction ends by reconstructing from principles the instrument Metius found by chance, which answers the complaint of the opening page (AT 82). The admission that the telescope occasioned the treatise confirms that the Optics is ordered to practice, and that the theory of light and vision was built to explain and improve an artefact.

(AT 159–160) For the third condition, that the actions moving each optic fibre be neither too strong nor too weak, nature has provided well by letting us narrow and widen our pupils, but art can add something. When the actions are too strong to bear by narrowing the pupil, as when looking at the sun, one can place against the eye a black body with a very narrow hole that does the pupil's work, or look through crape or some other dark body that lets in only enough rays to move the nerve without hurting it.
