When they are too weak, accessible objects can be exposed to sunlight gathered by a mirror or burning glass, as strongly as can light them without damaging them. Art supplements nature by copying its device — the artificial pupil reproduces the natural one — which applies the rule of imitation laid down at AT 152.

(AT 160–162) With telescopes the pupil becomes useless, and the opening through which they receive light does its work, so it is this opening that must be widened or narrowed to make vision stronger or weaker. If it were no wider than the pupil, the rays would act less strongly on each part of the back of the eye than without the telescope, in the same proportion as the images were larger, not counting what the lens surfaces take away. But it can be made much wider, the more so the nearer the straightening lens is to the point toward which the first lens directs the rays. If the first lens makes all rays from the target point tend toward S and the lens KLM makes them parallel toward the eye, take KM equal to the pupil's diameter and draw lines from S through K and M to the first lens: their span gives the largest useful opening, since a wider one admits no more rays from the target point, only rays from elsewhere that confuse vision. With a lens klm, which by its figure must sit nearer S, the opening found the same way is larger in proportion as SL exceeds Sl. If Sl is no longer than the eye's diameter, vision will be about as strong and clear as without the telescope, and the objects as much nearer as they look larger: an object thirty leagues away, imaged as if thirty paces off, will be seen as clearly as if it really were thirty paces away. Magnification spreads light over more fibres, so the aperture must grow with magnification to keep the force on each fibre (AT 133). Making KM equal to the pupil is the rule later stated in terms of the exit pupil. The upshot is that an instrument can at best match, not exceed, the brightness of naked-eye vision at the apparent distance.

(AT 162) This matters chiefly for inaccessible objects. For accessible ones the tube's opening can be narrower the nearer they are brought, without loss of clarity, since as many rays from X enter the small lens as the large one. The opening cannot exceed the size of the lenses, which their figures limit in a way to be determined later. For near objects, proximity does the work that aperture does for distant ones. The limit on lens size points forward to the figures of Discourse VIII and the construction of Discourse IX.

(AT 162–163) If the light from objects is too strong, it is easy to weaken it by covering the edges of the lens at the tube's entrance, which is better than adding cloudy or coloured glass as many do for viewing the sun, since the narrower the entrance, the more distinct the vision, as with the pupil. It is better to cover the lens outside than inside, so that reflections at the edges of its surface send no rays to the eye, which serve no purpose in vision and may harm it. Stopping down improves distinctness because the rays from each point then meet more exactly, which is the trade-off between aperture and distinctness of AT 123. Masking the outer edge against stray reflections reapplies the third requirement of Discourse V: no light other than the image's.

(AT 163–164) The last condition required of the external organs is to see as many objects as possible at once. It is not required for seeing better, only for the convenience of seeing more, and it is impossible to see more than one object distinctly at a time; the confused view of others serves mainly to show where to turn one's eyes next. Nature has provided for this so well that art cannot add anything; on the contrary, the more a telescope enlarges the lines of the image, the fewer objects it represents, since the image's area cannot be increased, except perhaps slightly by inverting it, which he rejects for other reasons. But accessible objects can be placed where they are seen most distinctly through the instrument, and for inaccessible ones the telescope can be mounted on a machine that turns it easily toward any chosen point. The thesis that only one object is seen distinctly at a time matches the Regulae's teaching on intuition, which grasps one thing at a time, and treats peripheral vision as a guide for attention. Magnification costs field of view. The rejected inverting design is the Keplerian telescope with two convex lenses, which gives a wider field but an inverted image, so Descartes's preference for the Galilean pattern is a deliberate choice.

(AT 164–165) Finally, the defects of the eye that consist in being unable to change the crystalline's figure or the pupil's size enough can be reduced gradually by use, since the crystalline and the skin containing the pupil are true muscles whose functions improve with exercise, like all other muscles. So hunters and sailors, who practise looking at distant things, and engravers and other craftsmen, who practise looking at very near ones, usually see them more distinctly than others. So too the Indians said to have looked fixedly at the sun without harm must first have accustomed their pupils, by often looking at very bright things, to narrow more than ours. But this belongs to medicine, whose aim is to remedy defects of sight by correcting the natural organs, rather than to dioptrics, whose aim is to remedy them by adding artificial ones. The plasticity of the organ through habit is explained mechanically, as the training of a muscle. The disciplines are divided by their ends: medicine corrects nature, dioptrics supplements it. The division repeats the boundary drawn at AT 148 and the priority Part Six gave to medicine.

### Eighth Discourse: On the Figures That Transparent Bodies Must Have to Turn Rays by Refraction in All the Ways Useful to Sight

(AT 165–166) Before saying exactly how the artificial organs should be made, he must explain the figures that the surfaces of transparent bodies need in order to bend rays in every way his design requires. The matter is somewhat difficult geometry; if he cannot be clear to everyone, he will try at least to be clear to those who know the first elements. To keep them from suspense he announces the result at once: all the figures will be composed only of ellipses or hyperbolas, and of circles or straight lines. The design space is cut down before any proof is given, and the "differences" at the end of the discourse (AT 185–195) will justify the restriction. The reader has changed: the Optics was addressed to craftsmen (AT 82), but this discourse assumes Euclid, and Discourse X will translate its results back into instructions for the workshop.

(AT 166–169) The ellipse is usually presented by mathematicians as the section of a cone or cylinder, but gardeners draw it in their flower beds in a way that, though rough, shows its nature better. They plant two stakes, pass a loop of cord around them, and draw the curve with a finger keeping the cord taut. With the same cord, stakes set closer give an ellipse of another species, and stakes joined give a circle; shortening the cord in the same proportion as the distance between the stakes gives ellipses of the same species in different sizes. There are thus infinitely many species, and for any point B on an ellipse the two lines to the stake points H and I together equal the major axis DK, as the cord shows directly; ellipses with the same ratio of DK to HI are of one species. Because of a property to be explained, H and I are called "burning points." The line through B perpendicular to the bisector of angle HBI touches the ellipse without cutting it, as geometers know. The property that matters is this: draw from B, outside the ellipse, a line BA parallel to DK and equal to BI, and drop perpendiculars AL and IG onto the bisector LG; then AL is to IG as DK is to HI. So if the ellipse bounds a solid transparent body through which light passes more easily than through air in the ratio DK to HI, a ray AB is turned at B toward I, and since B was arbitrary, all rays parallel to the axis converge on I. The gardener's construction defines the ellipse by the constant sum of distances to its foci, which is exactly the property optics needs, and Descartes prefers a definition that exhibits how the curve is generated to one that merely locates it in a solid. "Burning points" names the foci by their optical effect. The result solves the problem of the anaclastic line, the curve refracting parallel rays to one point, which Regulae VIII used as its model case of a question solved in the right order.

(AT 169–170) The proof runs through similar triangles. Since AB is parallel to NI and AL to GI, triangles ALB and IGN are similar, so AL is to IG as AB to NI, that is, as BI to NI, AB being equal to BI. Drawing HO parallel to NB and extending IB to O, BI is to NI as OI to HI. The angles HBG and GBI being equal by construction, triangle HBO is isosceles, so OB equals HB and OI equals HB plus BI, which is DK. Therefore AL is to IG as DK to HI. The text printed here is Descartes's second version of the proof, which he sent to Mersenne to replace the 1637 one in any reprint; the first went through additional perpendiculars BF and NM. The proof keeps the promise of AT 166 by using nothing beyond the first elements. AL and IG are exactly the lines by which Discourse II measured refraction (AT 101), so the ellipse is the curve that realises the sine ratio at every one of its points with respect to rays parallel to its axis. The revision for a second edition shows Descartes still trimming elementary demonstrations for economy.

(AT 170–171) So if one draws an ellipse whose DK and HI stand in the ratio found by experience to measure the refraction of rays passing from air into a given glass, and gives a body of that glass the figure the ellipse would describe by turning about its axis DK, all rays parallel to the axis in air will be gathered at I, the burning point farther from where they come. A ray AB is bent at B by the curved surface exactly as it would be by the flat tangent surface CBE, and must go to I, since AL is to IG as DK to HI, as the measure of refraction requires; and the same holds for every other parallel ray. Three earlier results come together here: the tangent-plane principle (AT 104–105), the law stated as a ratio of lines (AT 101), and the constant measured once by experiment (AT 102). The measured constant fixes the eccentricity, so the physics of the glass determines which species of ellipse the lens must have. The focusing is exact for rays from one direction, which is the whole advantage over spherical lenses.

(AT 171–172) Rays directed toward the centre of a circle or sphere meet its surface perpendicularly and are not refracted. So if a circle BQB is drawn about I at any distance between D and I, the lines DB and QB turning about the axis DQ describe a lens that gathers at I, in air, all rays parallel to the axis on the other side, and conversely makes all rays from I emerge parallel. The second surface is chosen so as to do nothing: the circle centred on the focus lets the converging rays leave without further bending. Each lens therefore has one active and one inert surface, which keeps the design calculable. The converse use, a source at I turned into a parallel beam, follows from the reciprocity of refraction stated at AT 104.

(AT 172–173) Draw from I a circle RO beyond D, take B on the ellipse no farther from D than from K, and draw BO toward I: the lines RO, OB and BD turning about the axis describe a lens that makes rays parallel to the axis on the ellipse side spread out on the other side as if they came from I. A ray PB falling on the hollow surface at B is turned as much as AB was by the convex one, so BO lies on the line through BI, as PB lies on the line through BA. The same ellipse yields a diverging lens simply by putting the glass on the other side of the curve. Converging and diverging elements now share one geometric relation, and Descartes is assembling a small set of parts from which the instruments of Discourse IX will be composed.

(AT 173–174) Inside the ellipse DBK describe a smaller one of the same species, dbk, with its burning point at the same I and its other focus on the line DH on the same side, take B as before and draw Bb toward I: the lines DB, Bb and bd turning about the axis describe a lens that keeps parallel rays parallel while narrowing the beam on the side of the smaller ellipse. To avoid the thickness of this glass, circles QB and ro drawn about I give two thinner lenses, DBQ and robd, with the same effect. The single thick block is an afocal beam reducer, a telescope cut from one glass. Splitting it into two thin lenses sharing a focus, with inert circular faces where the block was removed, is the conceptual origin of the two-lens telescope.

(AT 174) Two similar but unequal lenses DBQ and dbq, placed with their axes in one line, their outer burning points at the same place I, and their circular surfaces facing, have the same effect. This is the arrangement of two converging lenses sharing a focus, in which the rays cross at I and the image is inverted. Discourse VII rejected inversion for reasons of field (AT 163–164), but the geometry admits it, and Descartes lists the possibility for completeness.

(AT 174) Joining the two similar unequal lenses DBQ and dbq, or setting them at any distance provided their axes are in one line and their elliptical surfaces face each other, makes all rays from the burning point of one gather at the burning point of the other. The first lens makes the rays parallel and the second refocuses them, so the distance between the lenses does not matter. The parallel beam between the lenses makes the spacing free, which gives a practical tolerance as well as a theoretical result.

(AT 174) Joining two different lenses, DBQ and DBOR, again with surfaces DB facing, makes rays from i, the burning point of DBQ, spread as if they came from I, the burning point of DBOR; conversely, rays tending toward I are gathered at i. Here a real point source is turned into an apparent one at another place. This is exactly the task Discourse VII set for spectacles: to make rays from one point reach the eye as if from a nearer or farther point (AT 151).

(AT 175–176) Finally, joining two lenses DBOR with their surfaces DB facing makes rays that tend toward I beyond the first spread, after the second, as if they came from the other I. The distance of each point I can be made as large or small as desired by changing the size of the ellipse. So with the ellipse and the circle alone one can make lenses that turn rays coming from a point, tending toward a point, or parallel into any of these three dispositions in every way imaginable. The fourth precept governs the series. There are three possible states of a pencil of rays, and every transformation between them has been shown realisable, so the set of optical elements is closed and complete. The free choice of focal distances completes the claim.

(AT 176–178) The hyperbola is also a conic section, but a gardener again shows it better. He plants stakes at H and I, fixes a cord a little shorter than a long ruler to the ruler's end, puts the ruler's other end on stake I through a hole and loops the cord's free end over stake H, then slides his finger down from X, keeping the cord taut and pressed against the ruler, so that the ruler turns about I and the finger traces part of a hyperbola, XBD; turning the ruler the other way gives YD, and swapping the stakes gives the opposite hyperbola. A slightly longer cord gives another species, and a cord equal to the ruler gives a straight line; changing the stakes' distance in proportion to the difference between ruler and cord gives hyperbolas of one species in different sizes; lengthening ruler and cord equally traces more of the same hyperbola, which can be extended indefinitely without its ends meeting. The hyperbola thus stands to the straight line as the ellipse stands to the circle. There are infinitely many species, and for any point B the difference between its distances to the two burning points equals DK, the distance between the opposite branches; hyperbolas with a constant ratio of DK to HI are of one species; and the bisector of angle HBI is tangent at B. The construction defines the curve by its focal difference and generates it by a continuous, regulated motion, the kind of tracing the Geometry admits as exact (Book II). The limiting cases place the ellipse and hyperbola as deformations of the circle and the straight line, the two lines that relate uniformly to their points. The hyperbola's kinship with the straight line is what will make it the better lens (AT 188).

(AT 179) From B draw BA inside the hyperbola parallel to DK and LG through B perpendicular to the tangent CE, take BA equal to BI, and drop perpendiculars AL and IG onto LG: again AL is to IG as DK to HI. So a glass in which refractions are measured by the ratio of DK to HI, given this hyperbola's figure, makes all rays parallel to its axis inside the glass gather outside at I if the glass is convex, or spread as if from I if it is concave. The hyperbola solves the reverse passage, from glass to air, where the ellipse solved the passage from air to glass. The ratio is inverted accordingly, as the next paragraph says: DK must now be the shorter line.

(AT 179–181) The proof parallels the ellipse's. Triangles ALB and IGN are similar, so AL is to IG as BI to NI; with HO drawn parallel to LG, BI is to NI as OI to HI; the triangles BEH and BEO are equal, since EB bisects the angle and HO is perpendicular to CE, so BO equals BH and OI is the difference between BI and BH, which is DK. Hence AL is to IG as DK to HI. (This too is Descartes's later revision of the 1637 proof.) Therefore, keeping between DK and HI the proportion that measures refraction in the chosen glass — DK now the shorter, where for the ellipse it was the longer — and tracing a portion DB of hyperbola as large as desired with BQ dropped perpendicular to the axis, the lines DB and QB turning about the axis DQ describe a lens that gathers at I all rays parallel to the axis on the side of its flat face, where they undergo no refraction. The flat face plays the inert role that the circle played for the ellipse, since rays perpendicular to it pass straight through (AT 99). A plane is easier to make than a sphere, so the plano-hyperbolic lens is already marked as the practical optimum, as the first "difference" will confirm (AT 185–186).

(AT 181–182) Trace a hyperbola db like the last, draw a line ro anywhere perpendicular to its axis without cutting it, and join b and o by a line parallel to the axis: ro, ob and bd turning about the axis describe a lens that makes rays parallel to its axis on its flat side spread out on the other side as if from I. This is the plano-concave counterpart, again with one active hyperbolic surface and one inert plane. The pair of plano-hyperbolic elements, one converging and one diverging, is the material Discourse IX will use for its telescopes.

(AT 182) If the hyperbola for robd is traced with a shorter HI than that for DBQ, and the two lenses are placed with their axes in one line, their burning points I at one place, and their hyperbolic surfaces facing, all rays parallel to their axes before meeting them remain parallel after passing through both, and are narrowed on the side of robd. This is the Galilean telescope in ideal form: a convex objective and a concave eyepiece with coincident foci, differing in focal length. The instrument Metius found by chance (AT 82) now follows from the geometry.

(AT 182–183) Two similar unequal lenses DBQ and dbq, with axes in one line, burning points at one place, and hyperbolic surfaces facing, do the same, narrowing the beam on the side of the smaller lens. With two converging elements the rays cross at the common focus, so this is the inverting arrangement again, now built with hyperbolic surfaces. The two telescopic schemes stand side by side for both families of curves.

(AT 183) Joining the flat faces of DBQ and dbq, or setting them at any distance with their flat faces facing, even without their axes in one line, or making one lens with the figure of the two joined, makes rays from one burning point gather at the other. Between flat faces the rays travel parallel to the axes, so a sideways offset of one lens changes nothing, which is why alignment of the axes is not required. The biconvex hyperbolic lens joins one point to another exactly, and the remark about axes shows Descartes thinking about what assembly errors the design can tolerate.

(AT 183) A lens with the figure of DBQ and robd joined at their flat faces makes rays from one burning point spread as if from the other. This convex-concave element shifts the apparent source along the axis without bringing the rays to a real focus. It gives the spectacle lens of Discourse IX a hyperbolic form.

(AT 183–185) Lastly, a lens with the figure of two lenses like robd joined at their flat faces makes rays that converge toward a point I beyond it spread after passing through as if they came from the other point I. The biconcave case completes the series of combinations. Every pairing of the two hyperbolic elements has now been assigned its effect, as the ellipse's were at AT 175.

(AT 185) All this is so clear, he thinks, that one need only open one's eyes and look at the figures to understand it. The appeal is to intuition through the diagram: the configurations exhibit their properties to the eye, as the Regulae (XII, XIV) wanted figures to support the imagination. The Optics proves by drawing, where the Geometry will prove by equation, so the two essays use different means to the same evidence.

(AT 185) The same changes can be produced by pairs of lenses one elliptical and one hyperbolic. One can also imagine infinitely many other lenses that turn rays from a point, toward a point, or parallel exactly into one another. He need not discuss them here, since he can explain them more conveniently in the Geometry, and the ones described are the best suited to his design, as he will now try to prove by considering the main ways in which they differ. The "other lenses" are the Cartesian ovals of Geometry Book II (AT 424 ff.), which join a point to a point by a single refraction. Exactness alone does not settle the choice, since many figures are exact, so further criteria are needed, and the discourse now enumerates them.

(AT 185–186) The first difference is that some figures are much easier to trace than others. After the straight line, the circle and the parabola — none of which alone can serve to trace any of these lenses — none are simpler than the ellipse and hyperbola. Since the straight line is easier than the circle and the hyperbola no harder than the ellipse, lenses composed of hyperbolas and straight lines are the easiest to cut, then those of ellipses and circles, and all others less so. Simplicity of description ranks the curves, in anticipation of the classification by degree in Geometry Book II. The parabola is excluded because it focuses by reflection, not refraction, and it reappears in its proper role as the illuminating mirror of AT 207. A criterion of craft — what can be cut — counts as a reason within the theory.

(AT 186–189) The second difference is that among lenses that act alike on rays from one point, or parallel from one side, those least curved, or least unequally curved, act more exactly on rays from other points or other sides. Only unequal curvature prevents a lens from treating several points as exactly as one. If a lens with flat faces could gather all rays from A at B, it would also gather rays from every point of a line AC parallel to its faces at corresponding points of BD, since flat surfaces relate equally to all those points; likewise a lens with two equal spherical surfaces would map points on one concentric sphere onto points on another. But only the straight line and the circle relate all their parts in the same way to several points, and neither suffices to gather rays from one point exactly at another. So no lens that does this for one point can do it exactly for others, and to keep the other points' rays as close as possible to where they should go one must choose the least and least unequally curved lines, those nearest the straight line — rather than the circle, whose parts relate alike only to points equidistant from its centre. The hyperbola therefore surpasses the ellipse, and no lens of any other figure gathers rays from different points onto as many other points as exactly as a hyperbolic one; for the other ways of changing the rays, hyperbolic lenses are either best or not notably worse, which their ease of cutting outweighs. The argument contains an impossibility result — no refracting figure images every point perfectly — and an optimisation within that limit: the error off the axis grows with inequality of curvature, so the curve nearest the straight line does best. It is argued rather than demonstrated, as Descartes admits ("without stopping to give a more exact demonstration"). This is the first attempt to reason about what later optics calls off-axis aberrations.

(AT 189–190) The third difference is that some lenses make rays crossing within them spread a little more on one side, and others the reverse. If G, G are rays from the centre of the sun and I, I and K, K rays from its left and right edges, they spread a little more after the hyperbolic lens DEF than before, and less after the elliptical ABC, which brings the image points L, H, M closer together the thicker it is; yet however thick, it brings them only about a quarter or a third closer than the hyperbolic lens does. The difference grows with the amount of refraction, so rock crystal, which refracts a little more, increases it. No lens of any other figure makes these points notably farther apart than the hyperbolic or nearer than the elliptical. The two curves bound the possible scale of the image of an extended source, which fixes the lens's power to concentrate the sun's image. The result prepares the treatment of burning glasses two paragraphs on.

(AT 190–192) In what sense, then, do rays from different points "cross at the first surface" that gathers them, as was said of the eye's surface BCD (AT 153)? The rays VCR, XCS and YCT truly cross at C on BCD. VDR crosses YBT much higher and VBR crosses YDT much lower, but since they tend to the same points as VCR and YCT, they can be treated as crossing at the same place, and since it is the surface BCD that makes them tend there, the crossing is rightly assigned to it, whatever the other surfaces of the eye may further change. Likewise two curved sticks crossing at C really cross there, though they bend away from the points they would reach if straight, and if curved enough they could cross again elsewhere. So rays through the two convex lenses DBQ and dbq cross at the first lens and cross again at the second — those from different sides; rays from one side cross only at the burning point I. The crossing point is defined by the ray through the centre of the surface, the later notion of the principal ray, and the convention protects the earlier claims about image size from apparent counterexamples. The curved sticks recall the blind man's sticks of Discourse VI, which carried the same geometry of direction.

(AT 192–194) The sun's rays gathered by the elliptical lens should burn more strongly than when gathered by the hyperbolic. One must consider not only the rays from the sun's centre but those from every point of its surface, which are hardly weaker, so the heat should be measured by the size of the gathering body compared with the size of the space where it gathers them: if the lens's diameter is four times the distance between M and L, its rays will be sixteen times stronger than through a flat glass. Since that distance grows with the distance of the image from the lens, and the lens's diameter and figure can change it by a quarter or a third at most, the "burning line to infinity" some have imagined is a fantasy. Of two burning mirrors or lenses of the same figure, the larger gathers the rays in a larger space farther away, but with no more force in each part; very small ones can burn as violently as the largest. A burning mirror whose diameter is about a hundredth of its distance to the burning place — the ratio of the sun's diameter to its distance — "even if polished by an angel," cannot heat more than the sun's direct rays. The half-learned in optics believe many impossible things, and the mirrors with which Archimedes reportedly burned ships at a distance must have been enormous, or rather fabulous. The principle is a conservation law: concentration is limited by the apparent size of the source, so no optical system can make an image hotter than the source seen directly. The angel marks an impossibility no craft can overcome, a limit theory sets on art. Debunking marvels — the infinite burning line, Archimedes' mirrors — exercises the method on received opinion in the manner of Part One.

(AT 194–195) The fourth difference concerns lenses that change rays from a fairly near point: those whose surface toward that point is most hollow relative to their size can receive more of its rays, even without a larger diameter. Here the elliptical lens NOP, made so large that its edges N and P reach the ends of the ellipse's minor axis, surpasses any hyperbolic lens QRS however large, and no other figure surpasses it. Finally, lenses differ in that some must be more numerous than others to produce the same effects, or must make rays from different points cross more often. With elliptical lenses, turning rays from one point to another point, or making them diverge as if from another point, always takes two lenses where one hyperbolic lens suffices; and parallel rays can be kept parallel in a narrower beam either by two convex hyperbolic lenses, which make rays from different sides cross twice, or by one convex and one concave, which make them cross once. One should never use several lenses where one will do, nor make rays cross several times when once suffices. The fourth difference measures how much light a lens gathers from a near source, a criterion that matters for the microscope and favours the ellipse. The fifth is economy: every surface costs light by reflection (AT 160, 196–197), and every crossing adds error. The preference for a single crossing — convex with concave — confirms the choice of the Galilean pattern.

(AT 195–196) In general, then, hyperbolic and elliptical lenses are to be preferred to all others imaginable, and hyperbolic lenses are preferable to elliptical ones in almost everything. He will now say how each kind of telescope should be composed to make it as perfect as possible. The verdict weighs the five criteria rather than deducing the result from one, the kind of comparative judgment the provisional morality makes when evidence runs out. The ovals of the Geometry do not upset it, being curves of higher degree and harder to cut. Discourse IX applies the verdict to the instruments.

### Ninth Discourse: The Description of Telescopes

(AT 196–197) First one must choose a transparent material easy enough to cut, hard enough to keep its shape, as little coloured as possible, and causing as little reflection as possible. Nothing yet found has these qualities better than very clear, pure glass made of very fine ashes. Rock crystal seems clearer, but experience suggests its surfaces reflect more rays, so it may suit the purpose less. The cause: light in transparent bodies is the action of a very fine matter filling their pores, and each body's pores are so even and straight that the fine matter flows easily along them; but the pores of two different bodies, such as air and glass, never match exactly, so some parts of the fine matter strike the solid parts of the surface and are reflected, both from air into glass and from glass back toward air, for air too has parts that are solid compared with the fine matter. Crystal, being harder and heavier, has larger solid parts and tighter pores, so it reflects more strongly and lets fewer rays through, though it lets those it admits pass more freely, as said above. Reflection is explained as a mismatch of pores at the interface, which accounts for its happening at surfaces rather than within the bulk. Crystal therefore transmits less light but bends it more freely, which is consistent with the thesis that harder bodies pass light more easily (AT 103). The treatise now proceeds from matter to figure to assembly.

(AT 197–198) Having chosen the purest, least coloured and least reflecting glass, the best figures for correcting the sight of those who see distant objects less well than near ones, or the reverse, are those traced by hyperbolas. If an eye B or C is disposed so that rays from H or I gather exactly at the middle of its back, but not those from V or X, then to make it see objects at V or X distinctly one must place before it a lens O or P whose two surfaces, one convex and one concave, are traced by hyperbolas such that H or I is the burning point of the concave surface, turned toward the eye, and V or X that of the convex one. The spectacle lens is the converter of apparent sources of Discourse VIII (AT 174, 183): it takes rays from the object's point and delivers them as if from the point the eye can focus. Its meniscus form joins one hyperbola of each kind.

(AT 198–199) If the point I or V is fairly far away, say fifteen or twenty feet, a straight line will do instead of the hyperbola whose burning point it should be, and one surface of the lens can be flat: the inner one, toward the eye, if I is far, or the outer one if V is. For then a part of the object as wide as the pupil can count as a single point, since its image will occupy hardly more space at the back of the eye than the end of one optic fibre. Nor are different lenses needed for objects at slightly different distances: two suffice in practice, one for the least distance one usually looks at and one for the greatest, or even one in between, since eyes are not wholly inflexible and can easily adjust to such a lens. The discreteness of the retina (AT 133–134) becomes a tolerance criterion: an approximation is acceptable when its error is smaller than one receptor. The eye's own accommodation absorbs what remains, so the theory decides when exactness no longer pays.

(AT 199–201) To make accessible objects — those that can be brought as close to the eye as one likes — appear much larger and more distinct with a single lens, the most convenient form is a lens flat on the side toward the eye and hyperbolic on the other, with its burning point where the object is to be placed. "Most convenient," because an elliptical surface with its burning point at the object, and a spherical one centred there, might do slightly better but could not be cut so easily. The burning point must be so close that only enough space remains to let in the light that illuminates the very small object. The lens should be mounted so that only its middle, about as wide as the pupil or a little less, is uncovered; the mount should be black on the side toward the eye, perhaps edged with black velvet so it can rest against the eye and admit no light except through the lens, and white or polished outside in the shape of a concave mirror that sends all the light it receives onto the object. The object can be held in the small glass vials already common in France, or better by one or two little spring arms coming from the mount, and the whole should be turned toward the sun, whose rays, kept from the eye by lens and object, strike the mirror, go from there to the object and from the object to the eye. The magnifier is designed around illumination, which the reduction of the image's light per fibre makes the critical problem (AT 133). Its reflecting collar anticipates the device later named after Lieberkühn. The trade-off between performance and ease of cutting is stated openly as "most convenient," not best.

(AT 201–205) The most perfect telescope for stars and other distant, inaccessible objects should be made of two hyperbolic lenses, one convex and one concave, at the two ends of a tube. The concave lens's outer surface abc should be a hyperbola with its burning point at the distance at which the user's eye sees most distinctly — flat for the old, who see far objects best, quite concave for the very short-sighted; its other surface def a hyperbola whose burning point I lies about an inch away, near the back of the eye when the lens is against it. These proportions are not strict: the same telescope can serve all eyes if the tube is lengthened or shortened, and def may need a farther burning point because of the difficulty of hollowing it so deeply — experience will judge better than reasons. In general, the nearer I is, the larger objects appear and the stronger and clearer vision can be, since the other lens can be larger, but too near and vision becomes less distinct because some rays fall too obliquely. The concave lens's uncovered part need only slightly exceed the pupil's widest opening, and its thickness cannot be too small: added thickness enlarges the image a little but darkens it, and size is better gained otherwise. The convex lens should be flat toward the objects and hyperbolic on the other side, with its burning point exactly at I, the more distant from its vertex the more perfect the telescope; its diameter is fixed by lines drawn from I through the edges of def, taken equal to the pupil. A smaller diameter makes objects look no smaller and no fewer, only darker, so rings of black card can stop it down when they are too bright; thickness matters only because glass always blocks a little more light than air. The tube must be firm, black inside, edged with velvet at the eye, its length and width fixed by the lenses, and mounted on a machine that turns it in all directions and holds it on the object, with a pair of sights. Since the more a telescope magnifies the less it shows at once, it should be joined to weaker ones, so that one can find the object by degrees: Jupiter found through the sights appears in the weaker telescope XX with its small accompanying planets; one of these, centred, appears alone and larger in YY, where regions can be made out; and the middle region appears in the strongest, where details can be seen — whose place could not be known without the others. The specification is derived from earlier results: the coincident foci of AT 182, the aperture rule of AT 161, the stopping-down of AT 162–163. The single instrument is made to fit individual eyes by adjusting the tube's length. The finder system applies the third precept to observation, moving "as by degrees" from what is easy to locate to what is hard. Jupiter's satellites, discovered by Galileo in 1610, serve as the example.

(AT 205–206) More perfect telescopes can be added to these three, if human skill can go so far; they differ only in having a larger convex lens and a more distant burning point. So if the craftsmen's hands do not fail us, this invention may let us see objects on the stars as particular and small as those we commonly see on earth. Magnification has no limit in principle (AT 155), only in execution. The promise rests on the homogeneous physics of Part Five, where heavens and earth are made of one matter under one set of laws, so the same observation could reach both. Discourse X will rank the microscope above this celestial prospect (AT 226).

(AT 206–210) The best telescope for near, accessible objects — far better than the single lens described above — also uses two hyperbolic lenses, concave and convex, in a tube. The concave lens and the inner surface of the convex one are as before, but the convex lens's outer surface must be strongly convex, a hyperbola whose outer burning point Z lies so close that only room for light remains between object and glass. Its diameter should be about such that the line NP passes through the inner burning point of that hyperbola: smaller, it would receive fewer rays from Z; larger, it would receive only a few more, and its much greater thickness would take away as much force as its size added, besides lighting the object less. The instrument should be mounted to face the sun, its convex lens set in the middle of a concave parabolic mirror that gathers the sun's rays at Z on the object, held there by a small arm. The arm also carries a black body the size of the lens to keep sunlight from falling directly on the lens and being reflected into the tube, which can never be perfectly black; this body has a hole the size of the object, so that a transparent object can also be lit from behind by direct sunlight or by sunlight gathered by a burning glass, as much as it can bear without being consumed, and part of the mirror or glass can be covered if there is too much. So much care is needed because the convex lens, which serves as the pupil and where rays from different points cross, is much nearer the object than the eye, so the rays spread over the optic nerve's ends in a far larger space than the object's surface, and the more they are spread the weaker they are. That fixes the telescope's length: the longer it is, the larger and more distinct the image, but the weaker its action, so that past some length it would no longer be felt; the greatest length can be found only by experience and varies with how much light objects can bear. Other ways of strengthening the light would be harder to apply, and few objects could bear more; other figures might gather slightly more rays but either gather them less exactly or require two lenses whose extra surfaces would take away as much as they gained, and would be much harder to make. Since such telescopes serve one eye only, the other should be covered with a dark veil rather than exposed to light or closed by the eyelid muscles, because the two eyes are so connected that one hardly moves without the other imitating it; and before looking one should rest one's sight in a dark place and dispose one's imagination as if to look at very distant, dark things, so that the pupil opens wider and a larger object can be seen. For the pupil's action does not follow immediately from the will to open it, but from the idea or feeling one has of the darkness and distance of what one looks at. The parabola, excluded from refraction (AT 185), returns here in its proper role as a reflector of parallel rays. The microscope's length is limited by light, a trade-off between magnification and intensity per fibre that only experiment can settle. The last instruction shows indirect voluntary control: we cannot will the pupil open, but we can open it by imagining darkness and distance, the doctrine of Passions §44 that we move the body by willing the represented effect.

(AT 210–211) Reflecting on what was required of the external organs for perfect vision, the reader will easily see that these telescopes add everything art can add, without a longer proof. He will also see why all telescopes made so far could not be perfect: the circle differs greatly from the hyperbola, and makers used the circle where the hyperbola is needed, succeeding only when, meaning to make spherical surfaces, they happily erred and made hyperbolic ones or their equivalent. This chiefly spoiled telescopes for inaccessible objects, whose convex lens is larger, since it is harder to hit on the right figure over a large surface, and the difference between hyperbola and sphere is much greater toward the edges. Since craftsmen may think cutting hyperbolic lenses very difficult, he will give them an invention for doing it conveniently. The completeness claim rests on the enumeration of Discourse VII. The success of past telescopes is put down to lucky error, as Metius's discovery was put down to chance (AT 82), and spherical aberration is diagnosed as the obstacle, worse at wide apertures. The diagnosis was wrong about what mattered most: Newton showed in 1672 that the chromatic aberration Descartes's theory of colour did not anticipate limits refracting telescopes far more than spherical aberration does.

### Tenth Discourse: On the Way of Cutting Lenses

(AT 211–213) Having chosen the glass or crystal, one must first find the ratio that measures its refractions, using an instrument. EFI is a flat, straight board, neither shiny nor transparent, so that light and shadow show clearly on it. EA and FL are two upright sights with small holes A and L exactly opposite, so that a ray through them runs parallel to EF. RPQ is a piece of the glass to be tested, cut as a triangle with a right angle at Q, and its three faces are flat and polished. With QP against the board and QR against the sight FL, a ray of sunlight through A and L enters QR perpendicularly and reaches B without refraction; meeting RP obliquely at B, it cannot leave without bending toward some point of the board, say I. The instrument's whole use is to find the relation between I — the centre of the small oval of light the ray makes on the board — and the points B, where the line through the holes meets RP, and P, where RP meets the board in the plane through B, I and the holes. The prism follows the lens design of Discourse VIII: one face is placed perpendicular to the ray so that it does nothing, and the measurement isolates a single refraction. Sunlight gives a parallel beam, and taking the centre of the oval is an elementary control of error.

(AT 213–214) Knowing B, P and I, and so the triangle they form, one transfers the triangle with a compass onto paper, draws from B the circle NPT through P, takes arc NP equal to PT, draws BN meeting IP extended at H, then from B through H draws a circle meeting BI at O: the ratio of HI to OI is the common measure of all refractions between air and the glass. If one is still unsure, other small right triangles of the same glass, used the same way, will always give the same ratio, removing all reason to doubt. The ratio of sines is obtained by construction, without tables or calculation, which suits workshop use and the Geometry's preference for constructions. Testing several prisms makes the agreement of independent trials the warrant of certainty, as at AT 102.

(AT 214) Then on HI take MI equal to OI and HD equal to DM: D is the vertex and H and I the burning points of the hyperbola the lens must have. The three points can be moved farther apart or closer as desired by drawing another line parallel to HI, farther from B or nearer, and drawing BH, BD and BI to meet it; the new points stand in the same relation. With them the hyperbola can be traced as explained, with stakes at H and I and a cord fixed to the ruler so it cannot fold back past D. The measured ratio fixes the species of the hyperbola directly, and its size is set by similar triangles centred at B. The physical constant passes into the shape of the lens without any numerical step.

(AT 214–215) To trace it with an ordinary compass instead, by finding many points it passes through, put one point of the compass at H, open it a little past D, say to 1, and describe a circle; take M2 equal to H1 and from I through 2 describe a circle cutting the first at two points, which lie on the hyperbola; repeating with larger openings gives as many points as one likes. This is a construction point by point, from the constant difference of distances. The Geometry distinguishes such curves, known only by points, from curves traced by one continuous motion, and the next paragraph explains why that distinction matters in the workshop.

(AT 215–216) Such points may serve for a rough model of the lens, but to give the lenses their exact figure one needs a way of describing hyperbolas in one stroke, as a compass describes circles, and he knows none better than this. From T, the midpoint of HI, describe the circle HVI; raise a perpendicular from D cutting it at V; draw TV. If the angle HTV turns about the axis HT, TV describes a cone, and the section of that cone by the plane VX, parallel to the axis and perpendicular to DV, is a hyperbola equal to the one required; parallel planes cut similar but unequal hyperbolas with their burning points farther or nearer. The definition by conic section, set aside at AT 166 as less illuminating than the gardener's, returns because it serves exact making: a cone generated by a turning line and cut by a plane is a continuous motion that a machine can reproduce. Different definitions of the same curve suit different purposes, the gardener's for understanding and the cone's for tooling.

(AT 216–218) The machine follows. AB is a roller of wood or metal turning on poles 1 and 2 and representing the axis. CG and EF are two flat plates touching each other, their common surface parallel to the roller and representing the cutting plane; the width NP of the upper one equals the lens's diameter or a little more. KLM is a ruler turning with the roller so that angle ALM always equals HTV, representing the line that describes the cone; it slides through a hole L in the roller, a weight or spring at K pressing it against CG, and its end M is a hardened steel point able to cut CG but not EF. Moving the ruler from N through O to P and back divides CG into two pieces whose edges NOP, convex on one and concave on the other, have exactly the figure of a hyperbola; made of steel, they can serve as models and perhaps as tools for cutting wheels. One defect remains: the steel point is turned differently near N or P than near O, so the edge it cuts cannot be uniform, which is why he prefers the following machine, though more complex. The cone-and-plane intersection is built as a mechanism, the geometry turned into a device. Descartes criticises his own design for the angle at which the steel point meets the plate. The editors compare the letters to the instrument-maker Ferrier of 1629, where an earlier machine pursued the same principle by other means, and the comparison shows how long the problem occupied him.

(AT 218–221) In the second machine, ABKLM is one piece turning on poles 1 and 2. Its part KLM is a ruler with parallel edges, inclined so that the line through the middle of its thickness makes with the axis an angle equal to HTV. CG and EF are plates parallel to the axis, now set apart just enough to let between them a perfectly round cylinder QR, and each has a slot NOP long and wide enough for the ruler to swing through and trace part of a hyperbola as wide as the lenses. The ruler also passes through the cylinder so that, as it moves, it carries QR with it while QR stays between the plates and parallel to the axis. Y67 and Z89 are the cutting tools; their flat handles slide between the polished plates, and each has a round hole holding one end of QR, which can turn about its own axis without turning them but drags them in every other motion. So as the ruler moves from N to P and back, every part of the tools describes exactly the hyperbola traced by the intersection of the line that describes the cone and the line that describes the cutting plane. The tools' edges can take various forms for various uses. For convex lenses, first cut several steel blades like CNOP with Y67; then, with these blades and Z89, hollow a wheel around its rim so that every section through its axis is the hyperbola; finally fix the glass on a lathe and press it against the wheel, turning both, so that the glass takes exactly the required figure. The exact form passes along a chain of copies — machine, blades, wheel, lens — and each link must preserve it. The second machine removes the first one's defect by moving the tools themselves along the curve rather than dragging a point across a plate. Turning glass and wheel together is the decisive step, as AT 224 explains.

(AT 221–222) In using the tool Y67, only half of each blade, say from n to o, should be cut at a time. A bar placed at P stops the ruler, moving from N toward O, once the line through its middle reaches the plane cutting the plates at right angles; the tool's iron must have all of its edge in that plane and its whole bevel turned toward N, but may be as blunt or sharp, inclined and long as one likes. Having forged the blades and filed them as near the right figure as possible, press them against the tool and move the ruler from N to O and back to cut one half. A stop then keeps the blades from advancing further; withdraw them a little, replace the iron with one whose edge lies exactly in the same plane and has the same form, but with its bevel toward P, so that the two irons laid face to face would seem to form one edge; move the bar to N and work the ruler from O to P and back until the blades have advanced as far as before. The vertex, where the cutting direction reverses, is the point where the first machine failed, and it is handled here with mirrored irons and stops. Craft procedure is spelled out step by step, which acknowledges what AT 77 said about the lute and its tablature: knowing the theory does not give the skill.

(AT 222–223) The wheel, of very hard material, after being filed as close as possible to its figure, is finished first with the blades — provided they were forged so well that tempering has not altered their figure — applied so that their edge and the wheel's axis lie in one plane and pressed against it by a spring or weight while it turns. Then it is finished with the tool Z89, whose iron must be bevelled equally on both sides, with all of its edge in a plane perpendicular to the plates; the ruler is moved from P to N and back while the wheel turns, so that the tool's edge removes irregularities from side to side across the wheel's thickness and its point those from top to bottom. Two operations correct errors in two directions, and each tool is assigned the error it can remove. The warning about tempering shows awareness that hardening steel can warp the form it is meant to carry.

(AT 223–224) Once the wheel has all the perfection it can have, the glass is easily cut by the two motions of the wheel and of the lathe holding it, provided a spring or similar device presses the glass against the wheel without hindering the lathe, and the bottom of the wheel always dips into a vessel of sandstone, emery, tripoli, putty or whatever other material is needed to cut and polish glass. The last transfer, from wheel to glass, is made by abrasion under steady pressure while both turn. Pressure and the double rotation are the essentials, and the abrasives belong to ordinary practice.

(AT 224) Concave lenses are made on the same model: first blades cut with Z89, then a wheel cut with those blades and Y67, and the rest as before. But while the wheel for convex lenses can be as large as one likes, the wheel for concave ones must be small enough that, with its centre opposite the line marking the cutting plane, its circumference does not rise above the axis line of the machine. For concave lenses the wheel should turn much faster than the lathe, for convex ones the lathe faster, because the lathe's motion wears the glass's edges more than its middle and the wheel's motion wears them less. The usefulness of the two motions is plain. Polishing by hand in a form, the only method used so far, can produce good results only by chance, even with perfect forms; polishing with the lathe alone on a model, every small defect of the model would mark whole circles on the glass. Combining two motions keeps the tool's local defects from being copied systematically onto the lens, an early piece of reasoning about how errors propagate in manufacture. The relative speeds are tuned to observed patterns of wear. The diagnosis of hand polishing — good results "only by chance" — repeats the theme of lucky invention that opened the treatise.

(AT 224–227) He omits the proofs of several points that belong to geometry: those versed in it will understand them unaided, and others will prefer to take his word. For things to go in order, one should first practise polishing lenses flat on one side and convex on the other, with hyperbolic figures whose burning points are two or three feet apart, enough for a telescope that shows distant objects quite well. Next one should make concave lenses of various figures, hollowing them more and more until experience finds the one that makes the telescope most perfect and best suited to the user's eye, which for the short-sighted must be a little more concave; once found, the same concave lens serves that eye for every kind of telescope. Then one need only practise making convex lenses to be placed farther and farther from the concave, and larger in proportion, noting that the farther they are from the eye the more exactly they must be cut, since the same defects throw the rays farther from where they should go. Last and most important, one should practise polishing lenses convex on both sides for telescopes that show accessible objects, beginning with those that make the instrument very short, which are easiest, and proceeding by degrees to longer ones. Their use is less attractive at first than that of telescopes that seem to raise us into the heavens and show us bodies on the stars as particular and varied as those on earth, but he judges them much more useful, since they will show the various mixtures and arrangements of the small parts of which animals, plants and perhaps other bodies around us are composed, and so help us to know their nature. For according to many philosophers all these bodies are made of parts of the elements variously mixed, and according to him the whole nature and essence of at least inanimate bodies consists only in the size, figure, arrangement and motions of their parts. The training programme follows the third precept, from easy to hard by degrees, and the apprenticeship is ordered as the method orders knowledge. The rule that distant lenses must be more exact rests on angular error multiplied by distance. The ranking of the microscope above the telescope ties the instrument to the core thesis of mechanism: if bodies are nothing but sized, shaped, arranged and moving parts, the microscope could make that structure visible and serve medicine, the end Part Six assigned to the sciences. The restriction "at least inanimate" is cautious, given Part Five's machine-animals; the Latin keeps it.

(AT 227) The difficulty of making the vertices of the two hyperbolas exactly opposite when a lens is curved on both sides can be met by turning its circumference on the lathe to exactly the size of the handles to which it is attached for polishing, and then, while the plaster, pitch or cement fixing it is still fresh and flexible, passing lens and handles through a ring they barely fit. He says nothing of many other particulars to be observed in cutting, nor of other things he said were needed in building telescopes, since none is so hard as to stop good minds. He does not measure himself by the ordinary reach of craftsmen, but hopes the inventions in this treatise will be thought fine and important enough to move some of the most curious and industrious men of the age to carry them out. Centring is solved by a self-aligning procedure, the ring forcing the axes into line. The audience shifts in the last sentence from the unlettered craftsmen addressed at AT 82 to the "curious and industrious," the collaborators Part Six hoped to enlist (AT 63). The Optics ends where the Discourse left the relation of theory to craft (AT 77): the invention is complete in principle, and its execution is handed to others.

## Meteorology

### First Discourse: On the Nature of Terrestrial Bodies

(AT 231) We naturally admire what is above us more than what is level with us or below. Although clouds hardly exceed the tops of some mountains, and are often lower than our church steeples, we must turn our eyes to the sky to see them, so we imagine them so high that poets and painters make them God's throne and show him with his own hands opening and shutting the doors of the winds, pouring dew on the flowers and hurling thunderbolts at the rocks. Descartes hopes that if he explains the nature of clouds so that nothing seen in them or falling from them remains an occasion for wonder, it will easily be believed that the causes of everything most admirable on earth can be found in the same way. Wonder is treated as an effect of the spectator's position, not of the thing, and the essay's aim is to remove it, which matches the Passions, where wonder (§§ 70–78) is useful only until knowledge replaces it. Meteorology is the traditional domain of signs, omens and divine action, so explaining it mechanically has a polemical edge: nature's most "elevated" phenomena belong to the same physics as the lowly. The argument a fortiori — if even the heavens' marvels yield, then all earthly ones will — makes the essay a test case for the universality of the method.

(AT 232) He sets out the plan. This first discourse treats the nature of terrestrial bodies in general, to explain vapours and exhalations in the next. Because vapours rising from the sea sometimes form salt on its surface, he will stop to describe salt and test on it whether one can know the forms of bodies that the philosophers say are made of the elements by perfect mixture, as well as those of meteors, which they say arise by imperfect mixture. Then he will follow vapours through the air to the winds, gather them into clouds, dissolve the clouds into rain, hail and snow — including the snow whose parts are perfectly formed six-pointed stars, unobserved by the ancients — and treat storms, thunder, lightning, fires and lights in the air. Above all he will try to depict the rainbow and explain its colours so that the nature of all colours elsewhere may be understood; and finally the colours of clouds, the circles round the stars, and multiple suns or moons. The Aristotelian frame is taken over in order to be undone. The Meteorologica's division between perfect and imperfect mixtures is the scholastic way of sorting minerals from meteors, and salt is chosen as the case where the new physics will rival the old on its own ground. The order runs from general to particular and from vapours to their products, following the generation of the phenomena. The rainbow is named as the centrepiece, the one place where the method's power is to be shown in full (Discourse VIII).

(AT 233) Since knowledge of these things depends on general principles of nature not yet properly explained, he must begin, as in the Optics, with some suppositions; he will try to make them so simple and easy that the reader may not hesitate to believe them, though he has not demonstrated them. The suppositions occupy the same place as the comparisons of the Optics (AT 83), and Part Six of the Discourse explains their status (AT 76): deducible from first principles but deliberately left undeduced, they are confirmed by the effects they explain. The appeal to their simplicity as a ground of belief is not a proof but a practical persuasion, which the end of the discourse turns into a criterion (AT 239).

(AT 233–237) He supposes first that water, earth, air and all surrounding bodies are composed of many small parts of various shapes and sizes, never so arranged or joined that no intervals remain, and that these intervals are filled with the very fine matter through which the action of light is transmitted. In particular, the parts of water are long, smooth and slippery, like little eels, which intertwine but never knot or hook together and so separate easily, while those of earth, air and most other bodies have very irregular shapes and catch on one another like branches in a hedge: bound together they make hard bodies like earth or wood; merely laid on one another and small enough to be moved by the fine matter, they make rare, light liquids like oils or air. The fine matter is always moving very fast, a little faster near the earth than at cloud height, faster near the equator than the poles, faster in summer than winter and by day than by night, as follows from light being an action by which luminous bodies push it in straight lines. It is composed of parts of different sizes, and the less fine, having more force, gather where agitation is greatest, while the finest slip everywhere; bodies whose pores admit only the finest, like marble, metals and ice, feel cold. Heat and cold require nothing more than the parts of the bodies we touch agitating the fibres of our tactile nerves more or less than usual. In our region near the ground the fine matter has just enough force to separate and bend most parts of water and so keep it liquid; higher in the air, or in winter near the ground, it lacks the force, and the parts stop, piled confusedly together, forming ice — like eels alive in a fisherman's perforated boat in a river, against the same eels stiff and dry with cold on the bank. Since water freezes only when the matter between its parts is finer than usual, the pores of ice fit only that finer matter, which is why ice stays very cold even when kept into summer and keeps its hardness instead of softening like wax. The suppositions replace the four qualities with geometry and motion: liquidity, hardness, rarity, heat and cold become arrangements and agitations of shaped parts. The eel and hedge images do the work of definitions, since the qualitative behaviour of water and earth is deduced from the shapes of their parts. Heat is a sensation caused by agitation, which applies the theory of sensation of Optics IV (no resemblance between the cause and what is felt) to touch. The grading of the fine matter by size and the explanation of ice's coldness by the fit of its pores are the kind of particular hypothesis Part Six said must be tested against experience (AT 64–65).

(AT 237–238) Among the long smooth parts of water, most bend or stop bending as the fine matter has a little more or less force than usual, but some are thicker and cannot be bent, and these make salts; others are thinner and can always be bent, and these make spirits or brandies, which never freeze. When the parts of ordinary water stop bending altogether, their most natural shape is not straight like reeds but variously curved, so they cannot pack into a small space unless the fine matter is strong enough to bend them and fit their shapes to one another. When it is stronger than needed for this, it makes them spread into more space again. Experience shows this: hot water in a long-necked flask set out in freezing air sinks visibly until it reaches a certain degree of cold, then swells and rises until it is all frozen, so that the same cold that first condensed it then rarefies it. Water long kept on the fire also freezes sooner, because the parts least able to stop bending evaporate while it is heated. The one supposition about parts of different stiffness yields three substances (salt, water, spirit) and a striking anomaly: water's expansion before freezing, here explained and checked by a flask experiment. The claim that boiled water freezes faster is offered as a confirmation, a kind of prediction from the model that observers since Aristotle had reported. A single structural variable doing so much work is what Part Six meant by principles "so simple and general" that many effects follow from them (AT 64).

(AT 238–239) To make these suppositions easier to accept, Descartes does not conceive the small parts of terrestrial bodies as atoms or indivisible particles; judging them all of one matter, he thinks each could be divided again in infinitely many ways, and that they differ only as stones of various shapes cut from one rock. And so as not to break the peace with the philosophers, he denies nothing they imagine in bodies beyond what he has said, such as their substantial forms and real qualities, but his reasons should be all the more approved for depending on fewer things. The disclaimer separates his corpuscles from Democritus: parts of one homogeneous, indefinitely divisible matter, which is the extended matter of Discourse Part Five and Principles II.20. The truce with the schools is diplomatic — the letter to Regius of January 1642 recommends the same caution, while Principles IV.198–203 argue openly against such forms — yet even here it carries a principle of economy: an explanation that needs fewer entities is preferable, so the forms are made idle rather than denied. The Latin keeps the sense and states the criterion more sharply, as principles "simpler and fewer."

### Second Discourse: On Vapours and Exhalations

(AT 239–243) If the fine matter in the pores of terrestrial bodies is more agitated at one time than another, by the sun or anything else, it agitates their small parts more, and those small enough and so shaped or placed that they separate easily from their neighbours scatter and rise into the air. They rise not through any inclination to ascend or any attractive force in the sun, but only because they find no other place where they can more easily continue their motion — as dust rises from a road kicked up by a passer-by, and higher when a whole crowd stirs the plain. Most parts so raised must have the shape attributed to those of water, since none separate so easily; these alone he calls vapours, reserving "exhalations" for those with more irregular shapes, though he counts as exhalations the finer water-like parts that make spirits, since they ignite, and excludes the branched parts so fine they make up air. The slightly coarser branched parts rarely leave hard bodies by themselves, but fire drives them out as smoke, and water slipping into their pores can carry them up, as wind sweeps leaves out of a hedge, or as water carries up oils in the alchemists' distillation of dried plants. Vapours occupy far more space than water though made of the same parts: in water the parts move only enough to bend and slide against each other, while in vapour they spin so fast in every direction that each stretches out to its full length and drives away all others that try to enter the small sphere it describes — like a cord threaded through a pivot that, spun fast, stands straight and fills its whole circle, but twists round the pivot when spun slowly. Rising is explained without levity, attraction or natural place: it is the path of least resistance for agitated parts, an application of the principle that motion persists and finds its way. The distinction between vapours and exhalations is redrawn by the shapes of parts, so the Aristotelian names are kept with new definitions. The spinning cord gives a mechanical model of expansion: a vapour occupies more volume because its parts sweep out spheres, not because a quality of rarity has been added.

(AT 243–245) Vapours can be more or less compressed or spread out, warmer or colder, more or less transparent and more or less moist. They are denser when their parts, less agitated, begin to bend and come together; when they are confined between mountains, between opposing winds, or under clouds, so that they cannot spread as far as their agitation requires; or when most of their agitation goes into moving together in one direction, so that they spin less, as when they leave a confined space and make a wind. A confined vapour as agitated as a free one must be much hotter, its denser parts having more force, as red-hot iron burns more than coals or flame, which is why the air feels most stifling in summer when it is calm and pressed alike on all sides before rain. A vapour whose parts are a little denser but much less agitated is colder; one much denser but only a little less agitated is hotter; and one whose parts move in unison is colder than a confined one equally dense and agitated, since parts moving together shake other bodies' parts less, as a steady strong wind stirs a forest's leaves less than a weaker gusty one. Experience shows heat consists in this agitation: breath blown hard on joined fingers feels cold on the back of the hand, where it passes fast and evenly, but warm between the fingers, where it passes unevenly and slowly; breath feels warm with the mouth wide open and cold with it nearly closed. That is why violent winds feel cold and warm ones are seldom strong. Temperature is reduced to two variables, density and the disorder of agitation, and the distinction between ordered and disordered motion is the most sophisticated idea in the discourse. A uniform current, however fast, heats little, because heat is disordered motion of parts relative to one another. The breath experiment — warm or cold from the same mouth — is an apt test because the source's temperature is held fixed while the manner of motion varies.

(AT 245–247) Vapours spinning fast and in time with the fine matter are transparent and invisible, since they do not hinder it from receiving the action of luminous bodies but receive it along with it; a vapour whose parts no longer obey the fine matter in all ways begins to turn opaque, less so if it is warmer. So horses' breath or sweat appears as thick smoke in winter but is invisible in summer, and the air often contains as many vapours or more when they cannot be seen as when they can; how else could the midday sun on a lake fail, without a miracle, to raise many vapours, when waters dry up faster in hot clear weather than in cold dark? Vapours confined and dense are moister, more disposed to turn into water and wet other bodies; those moving fast together are dry, since striking moist bodies they carry off their water, which is why violent winds are always dry and moist winds weak. In one sense the more agitated vapours are moister, since they penetrate pores better; in another less so, since their agitation keeps them from taking the form of water. Visibility is separated from presence: invisible vapour is still vapour, and the argument from the evaporating lake — denying it would require a miracle — appeals to continuity of cause and effect. Transparency is explained by the Optics' doctrine that light is an action carried by the fine matter (AT 84–88), so whatever moves in step with that matter does not interrupt it. The two senses of "moist" show Descartes clarifying a term by its causal definition rather than legislating one meaning.

(AT 247–248) Exhalations are capable of many more qualities than vapours, since their parts can differ more. It suffices to note that the coarsest are hardly more than earth, like the sediment left by melted snow or rain water; the finest hardly more than the spirits that rise first in distillation; and the middling ones partake of volatile salts or of oils, or rather of the smoke they give off when burned. Though most exhalations rise mixed with vapours, they can easily separate afterwards, by themselves as oils separate from the water they are distilled with, by winds gathering them into one or more bodies as village women churning cream separate butter from whey, or simply because, being heavier or lighter and more or less agitated, they settle in a lower or higher region than the vapours. Oils usually rise less high than spirits and earthy exhalations less high than oils, and none stop lower than the parts of common salt, which are not properly vapours or exhalations since they rise only to the surface of the water, but which come there by evaporation and have remarkable properties that he will not omit. The exhalations are sorted by analogy with the products of distillation, and the alchemists' laboratory supplies the model of atmospheric chemistry, as the kitchen and dairy supply comparisons for separation. The stratification by weight and agitation prepares the account of fiery meteors in Discourse VII, which burn where exhalations gather. Salt enters as a borderline case, which justifies the digression that follows.

### Third Discourse: On Salt

(AT 249–256) The saltiness of the sea consists only in those thicker parts of its water that the fine matter cannot bend like the others, nor even agitate except through the smaller ones. Water must be composed of such parts: otherwise it would divide equally easily in every direction and so would not enter wide-pored bodies like lime and sand more readily than narrow-pored ones like glass and metal; and if its parts lacked the shape attributed to them, they could not be driven from other bodies' pores by mere wind or heat, as oily liquids cannot. Since no bodies in nature are perfectly alike, the parts of water must differ a little in size, and in the sea, the receptacle of all waters, some are too thick to be bent. This alone gives them all the qualities of salt. They taste sharp and piercing because, unbendable, they always enter the tongue's pores point first, while fresh water's parts slide over it lying flat and are hardly tasted; entering meat point first, they draw out moisture and stand like little stakes among its parts, keeping the pliant parts from disarranging them, so the meat is preserved and hardens with time. Salt water is heavier because its more massive parts pack into less space, on which weight depends; they stay mixed with fresh water rather than sinking because common salt's parts are equally thick at both ends and straight, like little sticks — any thicker at one end sank long ago, and any curved ones got caught in hard bodies — and the fresh water's parts, always moving, roll round them and can move faster so wound than alone, spending no force in bending free of one another. So salt dissolves in fresh water, even in moist air, but only up to a fixed amount, as much as the pliant parts can wind round. Salt water should also be more transparent and refract a little more, and freeze less easily. This explains the "secret" of making ice in summer: salt mixed with snow or crushed ice round a vessel of fresh water makes the water freeze, because the coarser fine matter from the vessel's water passes into the salt water's pores, where it moves more freely, and is replaced by the finer matter from the snow, which cannot keep the water agitated. Salt's parts are fixed, not volatile: being long and straight, they can hardly stay suspended without turning point down, and so must sink rather than rise, since the resistance to their point is small and their weight constant. Since sea water is sweetened by passing through sand, whose winding channels the stiff parts cannot follow, springs and rivers, which come from vapours or have passed through much sand, are fresh, and rivers returning to the sea neither enlarge it nor make it less salty, since as much water leaves it, partly as vapour returning as rain or snow, mostly through underground channels under the mountains, where the earth's heat raises it as vapour to the springs; the sea should also be saltier at the equator than the poles. He would also explain, were he treating fire, why sea water extinguishes fires less well than river water and why it sparkles at night when agitated: salt parts, easily shaken loose and strong once moving, can feed a flame or spark by darting out of the water, as when a wave striking a sandbank throws them free, though this requires very straight, slippery parts (not brine or stale sea water), loosely held (more in warm weather), strong agitation, and parts moving point first. The digression keeps the promise of AT 232: here mechanism competes with "perfect mixture" in the chemistry of substances.
