A single supposition — some water parts are too stiff to bend — accounts for taste, preservation, weight, solubility and its limit, transparency, freezing point, the freezing mixture, non-volatility, desalination by sand, the water cycle and phosphorescence. The demonstrative style is cumulative confirmation: each property "is no wonder" once the parts are known. The argument that water must have parts because it does not enter all pores equally is the one place where the existence of corpuscles is argued for rather than supposed.

(AT 256–264) Next, how salt floats on water as it forms, though its parts are fixed and heavy, and how it forms small square grains shaped almost like a table-cut diamond with its broadest face slightly hollowed. Sea water must be held in pits, away from waves and fresh water, in hot dry weather strong enough to evaporate the fresh parts wound round the salt. The water's surface is always very even, since its parts move alike among themselves and so do those of the air above, but differently from each other, and the two surfaces polish each other like hard bodies, almost instantly; so the surface is harder to divide than the interior, as small steel needles show by floating until the surface breaks. When heat makes some fresh parts rise so fast that they bring salt parts up to the surface before unwinding, they unwind only after the hole they made has closed, leaving the salt parts floating, lying flat, bending the surface slightly under them. Others arriving roll down into these hollows and lie side by side with the first, since that lets them sink lowest; those leaning toward the ends lie across at right angles; hundreds together form a small, apparently square table, the base of the grain. As it grows it sinks slowly, bending the surface without breaking it, until new parts roll over its edges and form a second table on it, then a third, and so on, each a little larger than the last because the parts roll less easily on salt than on water and leave the middle empty — whence the hollow face and the stepped sides. The base's size depends on the water's agitation, the slope of the faces on whether the heat rises or falls, with steps if it varies; the edges are blunt and uneven because parts meeting the corners settle differently, which is why grains split there and why the middle hollow is nearly round. Fresh water trapped in pockets between salt parts bursts out with a crack when strongly heated, so whole grains crackle in the fire and powdered salt does not. Other finer parts in sea water give white salt its pleasant violet smell, black salt its dirty colour, and all the varieties depending on the waters salts form from. Salt is friable because of how its parts join, white or transparent because of their size, fusible when whole because of the trapped fresh water, hard to fuse when pulverised and dried because its stiff parts bend only with difficulty — the fresh water's parts having softened over time by winding round others, while the salt's have stayed rigid; both must be round, like cords and rods respectively, since bodies long moved in various ways become rounded. Finally the "spirit" or "oil" of salt that dissolves gold: drawn only by a very strong fire, its parts are salt's own, made volatile only after colliding until, from stiff rods, they became pliant and flattened like iris leaves, which, lying along the tongue and cutting its nerve ends, give an acid taste instead of a salty one. One could go on to infinity, but it is better to return to vapours and the winds. The crystal growth account is the discourse's showpiece: an observable macroscopic form — the hopper-shaped grain with its stepped faces and hollow centre — is generated step by step from the behaviour of rod-shaped parts on a polished surface, a genesis of form of the kind Part Five promised (AT 45). Surface tension is recognised and explained as a mutual polishing of two differently moving layers, with the floating needle as evidence. The final example turns the alchemists' strongest reagent into a mechanical modification of salt's parts — from rods to blades — whose taste follows from their new shape, which carries the challenge to substantial forms into the chemist's own domain.

### Fourth Discourse: On Winds

(AT 265–269) Any perceptible agitation of the air is called wind, and any invisible, impalpable body air. So water rarefied into very fine vapour is said to become air, though the great air we breathe consists mostly of parts quite unlike water's and much finer; and air blown from bellows is called wind, though the broad winds over sea and land are usually only vapours that, dilating, move from where they are to where they find more room. So in an aeolipile a little water turned to vapour makes a strong wind. A hollow copper ball with a tiny opening, partly filled with water and set on the fire: heat raises parts of the water, which spread, spin and push to separate, and since they can do so only as others leave through the hole, all their forces combine to drive out the nearest, causing a wind that lasts until the water is exhausted or the heat stops. Natural winds differ in two ways. Their vapours rise not only from water but from moist earth, snow and clouds, where the parts are already loosened; and they are not enclosed, only kept from spreading equally in all directions by other vapours, clouds, mountains or winds, while elsewhere other vapours condense and leave room toward which the dilating ones are directed. Thus vapours dilating at F and condensing at G make a wind from F to G, especially if mountains block A and B, another wind compresses E, and clouds above stop them rising. Vapours carry along the air and exhalations in their path, so they cause winds almost by themselves without being their only matter; air and exhalations contribute little, since air dilated occupies only two or three times its moderately condensed volume, while vapours occupy two or three thousand times more, and exhalations need great heat to rise and hardly ever recondense. The aeolipile is the experimental model, as the camera obscura was for the eye: a small artefact reproducing in controlled form the cause supposed for the natural phenomenon. Wind is redefined causally as the motion of dilating vapour toward regions of condensation, a pressure-difference account that displaces Aristotle's dry exhalation. The quantitative contrast — air expands two or three times, vapour thousands — selects the dominant cause by order of magnitude, a rare use of measurement to decide between causes.

(AT 269–274) Now the properties and generation of the principal winds. It is observed that the whole air circles the earth from east to west; this must be supposed here, since its reason can only be given by explaining the whole structure of the universe. East winds are usually drier and make the air clearer than west winds, because the latter, opposing the vapours' ordinary course, stop them and thicken them into clouds, while the former drive and scatter them. East winds blow mostly in the morning and west winds in the evening: vapours dilated by midday heat at B move round both ways toward D, where night's cool condenses them, making a west wind where the sun sets and an east wind where it rises; the east wind is stronger, following the general course of the air and moving toward the part that has been dark longer. North winds blow mostly by day, from above, violently, cold and dry: the polar regions are covered with clouds and fogs, while vapours raised at the subsolar point mount until their weight turns them aside above the clouds, and the clouds, heated, turn into vapours that flow toward the warm side, where the agitated vapours yield easily, rather than toward the dense polar air — so in Europe a north wind blows by day, from the clouds down, strong because driven by the strongest heat and made from the matter easiest to dissolve, cold because it brings the very fine matter from the north on which cold chiefly depends, and dry because it carries only the coarser parts of fresh water. South winds blow mostly at night, upward, slow and moist: at night the equatorial land still holds heat and gives off vapours, while the air above has cooled more (coarse, heavy and hard bodies keep heat longer than light, fine and liquid ones), so the vapours above thicken into clouds and force those rising from the land to flow sideways. In March and all spring winds are drier and weather changes more sudden and frequent than in any other season, because the hemisphere in spring, warmed less in the preceding months than the one in autumn, is more covered with snow and cloud, so the day dilates and the night condenses more vapours, making the dry east and north winds stronger; and when the regular winds are advanced, delayed or deflected by local causes they meet and produce rains and storms, soon ended because east and north winds prevail. He believes these are the Greeks' Ornithiae, which brought back the spring birds, while the Etesians after the summer solstice probably come from vapours the sun raises from northern lands and waters after lingering near the tropic of Cancer, its long six-month polar day being then just past noon. The general circulation is supposed, not derived, and the deferral to "the whole structure of the universe" points to the vortex cosmology withheld since the condemnation of Galileo (Discourse AT 60). The rest is deduced from one mechanism, dilation and condensation driven by the sun's daily and seasonal course, applied to observed regularities of direction, time, temperature and moisture. The use of the Ornithiae and Etesians is typical: ancient observations are kept as data, while their ancient explanations are replaced.

(AT 274–279) These general and regular winds would always be as described if the earth's surface were all water or all land, with no other cause dilating or condensing vapours than the sun. But the sun draws more vapours from seas than from dry lands by day, and at night the stored heat draws more from lands, so on coasts the wind often comes from the sea by day and from the land by night, which is also why the will-o'-the-wisps lead night travellers toward water, following the air drawn there. The air touching water follows its course, so coastal winds change with the tides and gentle winds follow great rivers in calm weather. Vapours from water are moister and thicker than those from land, which carry more air and exhalations, so storms are more violent at sea and the same wind can be dry in one country and moist in another, as south winds, moist nearly everywhere, are dry in Egypt, where only the scorched African lands feed them, which is no doubt why it hardly ever rains there. The moon's light, very unequal with its phases, also dilates vapours, as do the stars', but only in proportion to what our eyes feel of it, our eyes being the surest judges of light's force, so starlight counts for almost nothing against moonlight, nor moonlight against sunlight. Vapours rise very unevenly from mountains and plains, forests and meadows, fields and deserts, and from soils warmer in themselves; clouds formed unevenly are carried by slight winds, held at various heights, often several layers above one another, on which the stars act differently, and differently again on the ground beneath them according to cover and recent rain or snow. So it is almost impossible to predict the particular winds each day in each place, and several contrary winds often pass above one another; but one can determine in general which winds will be most frequent and strong, where and in what seasons, and better still on the open sea, where the surface's evenness produces fewer irregular winds and coastal winds rarely reach — witness the sailors who named the widest ocean the Pacific. Nearly all sudden changes of weather — hotter, rarer or moister than the season requires — depend on winds, both local and neighbouring: a weak local south wind from a nearby lake may be moist, and heavier than one caused by northern condensation, while a north wind in neighbouring lands may bring its very fine matter and an extraordinary cold. Adding the fine matter and vapours moving in the earth's pores like winds and bringing exhalations of all kinds, and the winds that descending clouds cause, he thinks all the causes of weather changes have been given. The discourse ends with a statement on the limits of prediction: the mechanism is fully known, but the particular outcome depends on so many local conditions that only general regularities can be forecast, with better results where conditions are uniform. This is the underdetermination of Part Six (AT 64) recast as complexity — not many possible causes, but too many interacting circumstances. The rule that the eye is the surest judge of light's force makes a sensation the measure of a physical quantity, consistent with Optics VI's per-fibre force (AT 132–133). The enumeration of causes closes with the claim to have them "all," the fourth precept applied once more.

### Fifth Discourse: On Clouds

(AT 279–282) Having seen how vapours cause winds by dilating, we must see how by condensing they form clouds and fogs: once they become notably less transparent than pure air, they are called fogs if they reach the ground and clouds if they stay suspended higher. They lose transparency because, as their motion slows and their parts come close enough to touch, they join into small heaps, which are drops of water or particles of ice; while separate and floating they hardly hinder light, but once gathered, though the drops or particles are transparent, each surface reflects part of the rays, as the Optics said of all transparent surfaces (AT 196–197), and there are soon enough surfaces to reflect nearly all of them. Drops form when the fine matter around the vapour's parts no longer has force to keep them stretched and pushing one another apart, but still enough to bend them, so that all those that meet gather into a ball. Its surface at once becomes even and polished, for the reason given for the sea's surface (AT 257), and exactly round, because the fine matter, passing more freely within air or within water than from one to the other, must circulate inside the drop and in the surrounding air at different rates and so arrange its surface in a circle, as rivers swirl where something blocks them. Drops are round in horizontal section, since calm air presses them equally on all sides. Small drops too light to fall are not flattened, since air that will not yield beneath them will not yield at their sides either; falling drops are not elongated, since the air they displace must flow up round them to fill the space they leave, and does so most easily round a sphere, the figure with least surface for its volume. Drop size depends on how close the vapour's parts were when they began to gather, on their later agitation, and on further vapours joining them: first two or three parts, then two or three drops into one, and so on, until they are heavy enough to fall as rain or dew. Opacity is explained by multiplication of reflecting surfaces, the same doctrine that explains whiteness elsewhere: transparent drops make an opaque cloud. The sphericity argument works by symmetry and by minimum: there is no reason for any side to bulge, and the sphere offers the shortest path for displaced air. The growth of drops by successive coalescence is a mechanism of scale that explains why clouds, fogs and rain differ by size of drop rather than by kind.

(AT 282–283) Ice particles form when the cold is so great that the fine matter cannot bend the vapour's parts. If it comes after the drops are formed, it freezes them round, unless a strong wind flattens them on one side; if before they begin to form, the parts join only lengthwise into very fine threads of ice; and if in between, which is commonest, it freezes the parts as they bend and heap together, without letting them unite into drops, making small white knots or pellets of ice. They are white because they consist of many threads with distinct surfaces, though folded together, and hairy because parts that cannot bend and heap so quickly attach themselves straight to them; slower or sudden cold and denser or rarer vapour make the knots larger or smaller and their hairs stronger and shorter or finer and longer. The three outcomes depend on one variable, the moment at which cold arrives relative to the gathering of parts, so ice beads, threads and hairy pellets are three stages of one process interrupted at different times. Whiteness again comes from many distinct surfaces, the explanation of the white colour of salt and snow, so the colour is predicted from the structure. These hairy pellets are the building blocks of the snow stars of Discourse VI.

(AT 283–290) Two things are always needed to turn vapours into water or ice: their parts must be close enough to touch, and cold enough around them to make them stick when they touch. Cold alone would not suffice if they were too scattered, nor nearness if their heat — that is, their agitation — kept them apart. So clouds do not always form high in the air, though it is always cold enough there; a west wind must also gather and condense the vapours where it ends, or several winds must press them together, or one must drive them against an existing cloud, or they must gather under a cloud as they rise. Fogs likewise form only when cold air and abundant vapours coincide, as often in the evening or at night after a warm day, more in spring than in other seasons because of the greater difference between day and night, and more in marshy or coastal places, where water cools the air faster than land and moist warm land supplies vapours. The greatest fogs, like clouds, form where winds meet; their drops must be very small, or they would fall as rain or snow, and any wind soon disperses them, since the slightest agitation makes drops coalesce and fall. Clouds form at various heights, often several layers moved by different winds, especially in mountain country; the highest are almost always of ice, since the air there is at least as cold as on mountain tops, where snow does not melt even in midsummer. So the top of a cloud is usually of fine, widely scattered ice threads, below them small hairy pellets, larger ones further down, and sometimes drops at the very bottom. In calm air, or air moved evenly by a wind, these remain scattered and the cloud is no different from a fog; but winds that do not move all the surrounding air equally flow over and under the cloud and flatten those surfaces. What he especially wants noticed is that the snow pellets in such surfaces arrange themselves so that each has six others around it touching it, or at least equidistant. Suppose a west wind from D meets an east wind from C over the ground AB, they first stop each other around FGP and condense vapours into a confused mass, the air there being calm while their forces balance; but this cannot last, and the stronger finally passes under, over, through or around the cloud, deflecting the other — here the west wind, passing between G and P, drives the east wind below, turns the fog there to dew, and holds the cloud G above, pressed flat between the two. The ice pellets on its upper and lower surfaces, and on the lower surface of cloud P, must arrange themselves each with six neighbours, since nothing prevents it and all equal round bodies moved in one plane by a similar force arrange themselves so — as one can see by scattering a row or two of loose round pearls on a plate and shaking it, or blowing on them gently. He says this only of the upper and lower surfaces, since the sides are irregular, and not of the interior, where it is less evident. Pellets formed afterwards below the cloud from rising vapours, driven by the wind, also arrange themselves each with six around, forming a sheet under the cloud, then another under that, and so on; and since the wind acts more strongly on the lowest sheet than on the next, it can move them separately, polish their surfaces by flattening the hairs on both sides, and even carry part of them beyond the cloud to form a new one. Drops of water can be arranged likewise, provided the wind does not push them together and exhalations or unready vapours keep them apart; otherwise they merge and fall as rain. The two conditions for condensation — contact and cold — replace the single Aristotelian cause of cold acting on moist exhalation, and each case of fog or cloud is checked against them. The hexagonal packing is the discourse's chief hypothesis, supported by the pearls on a plate, an experiment that models the two-dimensional close packing of equal spheres. Descartes limits the claim to where it is evident, the flat faces, and declines it for the interior and the edges. The stacked sheets, moved at different speeds by the wind and polished as they slide, are the intermediate structure from which Discourse VI will derive the six-pointed stars.

(AT 290–291) The irregular outline of clouds applies only to those smaller than the winds around them. Sometimes so many vapours gather where several winds meet that they force the winds to circle round them instead of passing over or under, and form an extraordinarily large cloud, pressed equally on all sides, round and smooth at its edge. If those winds are a little warm, or the sun shines on it, it may acquire a crust of joined ice particles, which can grow quite thick without falling, since the rest of the cloud supports it. The exception tests the rule: the same mechanism that flattens small clouds rounds very large ones, because now the winds press from every side. The crust of ice on the great cloud is prepared here for a later use, since such ice crusts reflecting the sun are the cause Discourse X will give for multiple suns.

### Sixth Discourse: On Snow, Rain and Hail

(AT 291–293) Several things usually keep clouds from falling as soon as they form. Their ice particles or drops are very small and have much surface for their matter, so the air's resistance can outweigh their weight. Winds, usually stronger near the ground, where their body is coarser, than high up, where it is finer, act more from below than above and can hold clouds up or lift them; vapours from the ground swelling the air below, the warmth of that air dilating it, or the coldness of the air above contracting it, can do the same. Ice particles pushed together by winds touch without uniting and form a body so rare, light and spread out that it can hardly reach the ground unless heat melts some parts and so condenses and weighs it down. For as water is in some way dilated by cold when it freezes (AT 238), so heat, which usually rarefies bodies, usually condenses clouds — as snow shows, which is the same matter, only denser: in a warm place it shrinks a great deal before any water leaves it or it loses weight, because the finer tips of its particles melt first and, becoming mobile, attach to neighbouring particles without leaving those they hold, drawing them together. In clouds, whose particles are farther apart, drawing near some means drawing away from others, so they divide into flakes, larger as the cloud was denser and the heat slower; upper flakes falling first join lower ones and enlarge them, and heat, condensing and weighing them more and more, brings them to earth. If they arrive unmelted they are snow; if the air they cross melts them, as always in summer and often in other seasons here, rain; and if a cold wind refreezes them after melting, hail. The paradox that heat condenses clouds is resolved by the structure of the material: in a loose aggregate of hairy pellets, melting first welds the tips together and so contracts the whole, and the experiment with snow shrinking before losing weight checks this. Snow, rain and hail are three outcomes of one process — flakes formed by partial melting — distinguished by the temperature of the air they fall through. Weight is opposed by surface resistance, so the smallness of the particles explains why clouds float.

(AT 293–296) Hail can be of several kinds. A cold wind meeting formed drops makes transparent round grains, sometimes flattened on the windward side. Meeting nearly melted snowflakes not yet rounded, it makes horned hail of irregular shapes, sometimes very large because a wind driving the cloud down pushes many flakes together and freezes them in one mass. When this cold wind approaches melting flakes, the heat of the air around them — the most agitated and least fine matter in it — withdraws into their pores, as a sudden cold wind or rain outside drives more warmth into houses; it gathers toward the flakes' surfaces, melting them further just before they refreeze, while the unmelted parts stay at the centre, so the grains have clear continuous ice outside and a little snow in the middle, as one sees by breaking them. Since such hail falls almost only in summer, this assures us that clouds can then consist of ice particles as well as in winter; in winter not enough heat reaches the clouds unless they are so low that melted matter could not refreeze before reaching the ground. If the snow is only softened, not melted, when the cold wind comes, it stays white like sugar; flakes the size of a pea or smaller become fairly round grains, while larger ones split into several pointed pyramidal grains. The heat retreating into the flake contracts its parts toward the centre, rounding it, and the cold, following and freezing it, makes it much harder than snow; in larger flakes the interior keeps contracting after the outside has frozen, so they crack inward along planes toward the centre and burst into pointed pieces, at least eight, perhaps twelve or twenty-four, more often thirty-two or more, according to their size, the fineness of the snow and the sharpness and suddenness of the cold. He has observed more than once hail shaped like the eighth part of a sphere cut by three perpendicular planes through the centre, others longer and smaller, about a quarter of those, like sugar loaves with blunted edges, and round grains falling before, after or among them. The taxonomy of hail is generated from the state of the material at the moment of freezing — drops, half-melted flakes, softened snow — and confirmed by internal structure visible when a grain is broken. The retreat of heat into the flakes before a cold wind uses the house analogy to make a counterintuitive claim plausible, and the cracking of a contracting interior under a frozen shell derives the segmented hailstones Descartes had observed himself. The mention of personal observation prepares the long report that follows.

(AT 296–298) The various shapes of hail are nothing remarkable compared with those of snow formed from ice pellets arranged by wind in sheets. When heat begins to melt the hairs of these sheets, it first flattens those above and below, as most exposed; the little liquid released spreads over the surfaces, fills their unevenness and makes them as flat and polished as liquid surfaces, though it refreezes at once, since heat just enough to thaw hairs surrounded by air is not enough to keep their matter liquid on a surface of ice. Then the heat softens the hairs around each pellet where it is surrounded by six others: those farthest from the six neighbours bend at random and join those facing the neighbours, which, cooled by the nearness of the neighbouring pellets, cannot melt and instead refreeze the matter of the others as soon as it mingles with theirs. So six points or rays form around each pellet, of various shapes depending on the size and pressure of the pellets, the strength and length of their hairs, the slowness of the heat, and the force of any accompanying wind. The outer face of the cloud, which was like Z or M, becomes like O or Q, and each ice particle has the shape of a small, well-cut rose or star. The six-fold symmetry of snow crystals, which Kepler had puzzled over in his De nive sexangula (1611), is derived from the hexagonal packing of Discourse V: each pellet's rays grow toward its six neighbours because their cold preserves and recruits the hairs facing them. The form arises from the arrangement of neighbours, not from any form in the particle, which is the anti-scholastic thesis made concrete. The variety of stars is referred to the same handful of variables already used for hail and pellets.

(AT 298–308) So that it will not be thought he speaks only from opinion, he reports an observation of the winter of 1635. On 4 February at Amsterdam, after very cold weather, a little freezing rain fell in the evening, then very fine hail whose grains, about the size shown at H, he judged to be drops of the same rain frozen high in the air; but they were notably flatter on one side, almost the shape of the crystalline humour, from which he knew the very strong, cold wind had changed their shape as it froze them. What astonished him most was that some of the last grains had around them six small teeth like those of clock wheels, very white like sugar while the transparent grains looked almost black, so that they were evidently made of very fine snow attached after the grains formed, as hoar frost attaches to plants; one or two at the end had countless little hairs of paler, finer snow. He found it hard to imagine what had formed six teeth so exactly round each grain in open air in a high wind, until he considered that the wind could have carried some grains under or beyond a cloud and held them there, being small; that there they had to arrange themselves each with six others in one plane, "according to the ordinary order of nature"; that the warmth which had caused the rain had also raised vapours which the wind drove against the grains, freezing into fine hairs; and that later warmth melted all the hairs except those facing the six neighbours, whose cold protected them, so the melted matter mingled with and froze among them, making six teeth — while the countless hairs on the last grains had never been reached by that warmth. The next morning at eight he saw a kind of snow he had never heard of: small flat, polished, transparent plates of ice about as thick as heavy paper, perfectly hexagonal, with sides so straight and angles so equal that no human work could be so exact. He saw at once that they had been ice pellets arranged as described, pressed by a strong wind with enough warmth to melt all their hairs and fill their pores with the water, making them transparent, the wind pressing them together and flattening them. The difficulty was that, half-melted and pressed together, they had not stuck to one another — he never found two joined — but he realised that the wind, rippling the cloud's surfaces as it ripples water without roughening it, moves each particle slightly differently from its neighbours and so prevents them from sticking without disarranging them, while polishing their surfaces, as it polishes the waves it makes in a field's dust. Then came little roses or wheels with six teeth rounded in semicircles, transparent and flat, as well cut as could be imagined, some with a small white point in the middle "that one would have said was the mark of the compass foot used to round them" — the heat having been too moderate to reach their centre. Then wheels joined in pairs by an axle, or rather small crystal columns with a six-petalled rose at each end; then thinner columns with unequal stars, then shorter and shorter, until the stars joined completely into double stars with twelve rays, equal or alternately unequal. He explains these as particles from two sheets one above the other, held facing each other by the wind sifting between them, the heat melting only the surrounding hairs, which refroze into the connecting columns; the thick early columns came from four or five sheets whose middle ones melted more, being less exposed; unequal stars from unequal heat or pellets; twelve-rayed doubles from two six-rayed stars welded when the heat between the sheets melted the connecting threads completely. Among thousands of stars that day he found none with more or fewer than six rays, except a few doubles with twelve and four or five with eight, which were oval rather than round, formed where the edges of two sheets met. Countless white stars also fell, some like the transparent ones, most with sharper, finer rays, often divided into three branches like a fleur-de-lis, or into many like feathers or fern leaves, together with shapeless threads of ice: their whiteness came from heat not penetrating their matter, as the very thin ones were transparent; their branching from the heat leaving the hairs as soon as they began to gather, the side branches curling outward because the middle branch cooled them on that side; the shapeless threads showed that not all clouds are made of pellets. They fell because the day's strong wind could disarrange and break the sheets, letting the flat stars tilt and cut through the air; in calm weather they fall when the air below contracts or above dilates, and then more snow follows, which did not happen that day. The next morning flakes fell made of countless tiny stars, less regular inside than outside; then a sudden squall brought long thin white hail like sugar loaves, from the cloud's highest part made of fine threads; and three days later, snow of hairy pellets without star forms confirmed him in all he had imagined. The report is the Essays' most sustained piece of observation, dated and placed, with the instruments of a naturalist: comparison with the crystalline humour, clock wheels, a compass point, the thickness of paper. It has a strict logic. Each new form is first described and then derived from the model of hexagonally packed pellets, heat, cold and wind, and each anomaly — plates that do not stick, eight-rayed oval stars, white versus transparent stars — becomes a test the model passes. The counting ("among thousands … none with more or fewer than six") is a deliberate search for counterexamples. The account of capped columns and twelve-rayed twins describes crystal forms that would be classified only in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. "The ordinary order of nature" names hexagonal packing as a law rather than an accident, the nearest Descartes comes in the Essays to a principle of crystal form.

(AT 308–309) Clouds made only of drops fall as rain by their own weight when the drops are large enough, or because the air below withdraws or the air above presses them down, or by several causes together. The finest rain comes when the air below withdraws, sometimes so fine it is called a falling fog rather than rain; the heaviest when the cloud is pressed by air above, since the highest drops, falling first, meet others and grow. He has sometimes seen in summer, in calm, heavy, stifling weather, a rain begin before any cloud appeared, because the air, full of vapours pressed by winds from elsewhere, as the calm and heaviness showed, turned them into drops that grew large as they fell and fell as fast as they formed. The size of raindrops is derived from the direction of the force that brings the cloud down, an inference from cause to an observable feature. Rain from a clear sky is explained by the two conditions of Discourse V — vapours pressed close and cooled — with the oppressive calm read as evidence of the pressure.

(AT 309) When the earth cools and the air in its pores contracts, letting fogs descend, they turn into dew if made of drops, and into rime or hoar frost if made of vapours already frozen, or freezing as they touch the ground. This happens mostly at night or in the morning, when the earth, turned from the sun, cools. Wind also often brings fogs down, and can carry their matter to make dew or hoar frost where no fog was seen, and then the frost attaches to plants only on the side the wind strikes. Dew and frost are fogs brought down, so their kinds follow from the state of the fog's matter. The observation that frost forms only on the windward side of plants is a small but exact check on the claim that wind transports the matter.

(AT 310) The evening damp (serein), which falls only in the evening and is known by the colds and headaches it causes in some countries, consists of certain fine, penetrating exhalations that, being more fixed than vapours, rise only in warm countries on fine days and fall as soon as the sun's heat leaves them; hence its different qualities in different countries and its being unknown in many, according to the soils that produce it. It is often accompanied by dew, but the dew is not what causes the illnesses blamed on it. Manna and similar juices that fall at night are also exhalations, since vapours can become only water or ice; they differ from country to country, and some attach only to certain bodies because the shape of their parts gives them no hold on others. A popular belief about evening air is kept as a datum but its cause is reassigned, from the dew to exhalations that vary with the soil. The principle that vapours can only become water or ice sorts the phenomena: whatever falls from the air with other properties must come from exhalations. Manna sticking selectively to certain bodies is explained by the shapes of parts, as the Optics explained colours by the manner of reflection.

(AT 310–311) If no dew falls and the fogs rise in the morning leaving the ground dry, rain is coming, because this happens only when the earth, not cooled enough in the night or unusually warmed in the morning, produces vapours that push the fogs up, where their drops meet, grow and soon fall as rain. It is also a sign of rain when, the air being loaded with clouds, the sun nevertheless shines quite brightly early in the morning: no clouds to the east prevent the sun's heat from condensing those above us or raising new vapours to swell them. But since this cause works only in the morning, if it does not rain before noon nothing can be judged about the evening. He says nothing of many other observed signs of rain, which are mostly very uncertain; for the same heat that usually condenses clouds into rain can also dilate them into vapours that vanish insensibly or make winds, according as their parts are a little closer or farther apart, the heat more or less moist, and the surrounding air more or less dilated or condensed — all too variable and uncertain to be predicted with assurance. Weather signs are accepted only where the model explains them, and even then with a stated range: the clear-morning sign is valid only until noon. The refusal of the other signs repeats the conclusion of Discourse IV (AT 277) on the limits of prediction: the causes are known, but the outcome depends on small differences of circumstance, so that the same cause can produce opposite effects. Physics here explains more than it can forecast.

### Seventh Discourse: On Storms, Lightning and All the Other Fires That Kindle in the Air

(AT 312–315) Clouds cause winds not only when they dissolve into vapours but also when they descend so suddenly that they drive out the air beneath them with great violence, making a very strong but short wind — as can be imitated by holding a sail high in the air and letting it fall flat toward the ground. Heavy rains are almost always preceded by such a wind, which plainly blows downward and whose coldness shows it comes from the clouds. This wind is why swallows flying low announce rain, since it brings down the gnats they live on, which fly high in fine weather; and why, even when too weak to be felt in the open air, it can rush down chimneys and stir up ashes and straws in little whirlwinds that astonish those ignorant of the cause and are usually followed by rain. But if the descending cloud is very heavy and wide, as it can more easily be over the open sea, where vapours are evenly spread so that a cloud forming anywhere at once extends everywhere, it infallibly causes a storm, the stronger the larger and heavier the cloud and the longer the higher it falls from. So he imagines the squalls sailors fear beyond the Cape of Good Hope arise: vapours from the wide, sun-heated Ethiopian sea cause a wind that stops those from the Indian Ocean and gathers them into a cloud far larger than ours, which depend on smaller inequalities between plains, lakes and mountains. Since there are hardly ever other clouds there, as soon as sailors see one forming, though it may seem so small that the Flemish compared it to an ox's eye and named it so, and the rest of the air is calm and clear, they strike their sails and prepare for a storm, which never fails to follow; and he judges it must be the stronger the smaller the cloud first appeared, since it could not become dense enough to be visible without being large, so its smallness shows its great height, and a heavy body falls more violently the higher it falls from. The vapours in the air are dilated by the agitation, and the waves send up more, strengthening the wind and prolonging the storm by slowing the cloud's descent. Exhalations mixed with the vapours, less solid and more irregular, are separated by the agitation, as butter from churned cream, gather in heaps that float as high as they can against the cloud, and finally attach to the ropes and masts of ships as the cloud completes its descent, where, kindled by the violent agitation, they make the fires called St Elmo's, which comfort sailors as a sign of fair weather. Storms are often strongest toward the end, and there may be several clouds one above the other with such fires under each, which may be why the ancients thought one fire, Helen's star, an evil omen — the worst still to come — and two, Castor and Pollux, a good one, since that was usually the most they saw, while three, in an extraordinarily great storm, were again an evil omen. Sailors say they sometimes see four or five, perhaps because ships are larger with more masts, or they sail where exhalations are more frequent. For he can say nothing but conjecture about what happens on the great seas, which he has never seen and of which he has only very imperfect reports. The falling sail is the model experiment, as the aeolipile was for winds. The paradox that a smaller first cloud announces a worse storm is derived from the visibility condition and the law that falling bodies gain force with height, which makes a sailors' rule of thumb into a consequence. The classical omens of Helen and the Dioscuri are explained, not dismissed: the ancients read correctly a correlation between the number of fires and the storm's course, and Descartes supplies the cause. The closing admission of conjecture marks the evidential limit of the essay with the same care he used for the snow observations.

(AT 315–321) As for storms with thunder, lightning, whirlwinds and thunderbolts, of which he has seen examples on land, he does not doubt they are caused when, several clouds lying one above another, the higher ones sometimes fall suddenly on the lower. If two clouds A and B are made of very rare, spread-out snow and the air round the upper is warmer, its heat can condense and weigh it down until its highest parts, falling first, bring down many others with them onto the lower cloud with a great noise — just as he remembers seeing in the Alps around May that snow warmed and weighed down by the sun would fall in great masses at the slightest disturbance of the air, called avalanches, whose echo in the valleys imitated thunder quite well. So it thunders less often here in winter, when not enough heat reaches the highest clouds; and when, during great heat, a short north wind is followed by moist, stifling heat, thunder will follow soon, because the north wind, passing along the ground, has driven the heat toward the upper clouds, and then, driven back by the dilation of the lower air, makes the upper clouds condense and fall while the lower ones, still rare and even lifted, resist. The noise is louder than avalanches because of the air's resonance and the falling snow, and all the different sounds of thunder come from the upper clouds falling all at once or piecemeal, faster or slower, and the lower ones being larger or smaller, thicker and more resistant. The differences between lightning, whirlwinds and thunderbolts depend only on the exhalations between the clouds and on how the upper one falls. After great heat and drought, with many fine, inflammable exhalations, even a small, slowly falling upper cloud drives out the air between with a flash of light flame that vanishes at once, so lightning can be seen without thunder, even without visible clouds, and thunder heard without lightning if there are no inflammable exhalations. If the upper cloud falls piecemeal it causes hardly more than lightning and thunder; if it falls whole and fast, it can cause whirlwinds and thunderbolts as well: its edges come down a little faster than its middle, since the air beneath them has less distance to escape, and so touch the lower cloud first, enclosing much air, which, pressed by the still-descending middle, must break through the lower cloud, or open one of its edges, and rushes down to the ground, then rises spinning because it meets resistance on all sides. This is a whirlwind, without lightning or thunderbolt if there are no inflammable exhalations; if there are, they gather into one mass and, driven violently to the ground with the air, make the thunderbolt. It can burn clothes and shave hair without harming the body if its exhalations, which usually smell of sulphur, are only fatty and oily, a light flame catching only on easily burned bodies; conversely it can break bones without harming flesh, or melt a sword without damaging the scabbard, if they are very fine and penetrating, of the nature of volatile salts or strong waters, which do nothing to bodies that yield but shatter and dissolve those that resist much, as aqua fortis dissolves the hardest metals and leaves wax alone. A thunderbolt can even turn into a very hard stone that shatters what it strikes, if among the penetrating exhalations there are many fatty sulphurous ones and coarser earthy ones, as a mixture of that earth, saltpetre and sulphur, set alight, suddenly forms a stone. If the cloud opens at the side, the bolt is thrown sideways and strikes tower and rock tops rather than low places; and even when it breaks below, the bolt strikes high places rather than others, since, the cloud being equally disposed to break anywhere, it breaks above a steeple because of the steeple's resistance. Each thunderclap is usually followed by a shower, and when the rain becomes heavy there is little more thunder, because if the upper cloud's blow is strong enough to bring the whole lower one down, the thunder must stop, and if weaker it can still dislodge flakes that melt into rain. Nor is it without reason that loud noise, as of bells or cannon, is held to lessen the thunderbolt's effect, since it helps dissipate and bring down the lower cloud by shaking its snow, as those who travel in avalanche valleys know, who refrain even from speaking or coughing for fear of starting the snow. The avalanche is the master analogy, an observed terrestrial phenomenon whose sound and mechanism are transferred to the clouds, and it is the only part of the discourse grounded in Descartes's own sight. Every feature of thunderstorms — season, signs, the varied sounds of thunder, lightning without thunder and thunder without lightning, whirlwinds, the selective damage of bolts, thunderstones, the attraction to steeples, the rain after the clap, the effect of bells — is derived from one scenario plus the chemistry of exhalations from Discourse II. The selective damage, a standard marvel since Seneca's Natural Questions, is explained by the analogy with aqua fortis, which acts only against resistance, making the marvel an instance of a known chemical behaviour. Thunder is placed in falling snow rather than in fire bursting from a cloud, which displaces Aristotle's account by an explanation that fits the sequence of observed effects.

(AT 321–323) As lightning sometimes occurs without thunder, so in places with many exhalations and few vapours clouds can form so thin and light that, falling from a height on one another, they make no thunder and raise no storm, yet wrap and join exhalations into the small flames that look like stars falling or crossing the sky, and even into fairly large balls of fire that reach us, "diminutives of the thunderbolt." Since exhalations are of many natures, clouds pressing them may sometimes form a matter that, by its colour and consistency, looks like milk, blood or flesh, or that, burned, is taken for iron or stone, or that, decaying, quickly breeds small animals — as one often reads among prodigies that it has rained iron, blood, locusts and the like. Exhalations can also be heaped and kindled without any cloud, by winds alone, especially contrary ones meeting; and without winds or clouds, a fine penetrating exhalation of the nature of salts insinuating itself into the pores of a fatty sulphurous one can form light flames high or low in the air — shooting stars above, and below the will-o'-the-wisps, or the fires that settle on children's hair, horses' manes or the points of pikes rubbed with oil — since not only violent agitation but often the mere mixture of two bodies suffices to kindle them, as water poured on lime, damp hay stored, and countless chemical examples show. All these fires are weak compared with the thunderbolt because they consist of the softest, stickiest parts of oils, while the more active, penetrating salt parts that help kindle them scatter at once into the open air; the bolt is made chiefly of the latter, violently pressed and driven by the clouds, carrying the others with them to the ground — as anyone who knows the force and speed of saltpetre and sulphur mixed, compared with the separated fatty part of sulphur, will not doubt. The series runs from the thunderbolt down to its diminutives — fireballs, shooting stars, will-o'-the-wisps, St Elmo's fire — each with fewer of the conditions present. The prodigies of rains of blood, iron or locusts are naturalised as possible products of exhalations, with spontaneous generation in decaying matter taken from the common belief of the time. Gunpowder, the mixture of saltpetre and sulphur, serves as the laboratory model of the bolt's violence, in the way the Optics used the camera obscura.

(AT 323) The duration of fires that settle or flit around us can be longer or shorter according as their flame is slower and their matter denser, but fires seen only high in the air must be very brief, since if their matter were not very rare their weight would bring them down. The philosophers were right to compare them to the flame that runs along the smoke of a just-extinguished torch when brought near another flame. But he is astonished that they could then imagine comets and the columns or beams of fire sometimes seen in the sky to be made of exhalations, since these last incomparably longer. The criterion of duration, derived from weight and rarity, separates true meteors from celestial phenomena. Aristotle's Meteorologica (I.4–7) placed comets among sublunary exhalations, and Descartes rejects this on a ground internal to the theory: a burning exhalation could not stay aloft that long. Comets are thereby moved out of meteorology into the cosmology of The World.

(AT 323–324) Since he has tried to explain comets carefully in another treatise and does not think they belong to meteors, any more than earthquakes and minerals, which many writers heap in, he will speak only of certain lights that appear at night in calm, clear weather and lead idle people to imagine squadrons of phantoms fighting in the air, foretelling defeat or victory for their side as fear or hope prevails in their imagination. Having never seen such spectacles and knowing how reports of them are falsified and exaggerated by superstition and ignorance, he will just touch on the causes that could produce them. First, several small clouds, small enough to be taken for soldiers, falling on one another and wrapping enough exhalations to cause many small flashes, little fires and perhaps little noises, so that the soldiers seem to fight. Second, such clouds lit not by their own collisions but by the fires and lightning of a great storm so far off that it cannot be seen. Third, these clouds, or others more northerly that light them, so high that the sun's rays reach them; for, considering the refractions and reflections two or three such clouds can cause, they need not be very high to make such lights appear in the north after twilight, and sometimes even the sun itself when it should have set. But this belongs rather to the following discourses, where he will speak of all the things that can be seen in the air without being there, having finished explaining all those seen there as they are. The other treatise is The World (chapter 9), withheld from print, so the reference is a rare public allusion to it. The excluded topics — earthquakes, minerals — redraw the boundaries of the discipline according to cause rather than location. The phantom armies, a staple of portents, receive three possible causes offered without commitment, which is the right epistemic posture for unwitnessed and distorted reports. The final sentence divides the essay: Discourses I–VII treated things in the air as they are, VIII–X will treat things that appear there without being there — the rainbow, coronas, parhelia — so optics now enters meteorology.

### Eighth Discourse: On the Rainbow

(AT 325–329) The rainbow is so remarkable a marvel of nature, its cause so eagerly sought by good minds in every age and so little known, that he could choose no better subject to show how, by his method, one can reach knowledge that the authors whose writings we have did not possess. First, since the bow appears not only in the sky but also in the air near us whenever there are many sunlit drops, as in some fountains, he judged that it arises only from the way rays of light act on these drops and from there reach our eyes. Knowing that drops are round, as shown above, and that their size does not change the bow, he made a very large one to examine it better: a large, perfectly round, transparent glass flask filled with water. With the sun in the part of the sky AFZ and his eye at E, placing the flask at BCD, he found the part D appeared all red and far brighter than the rest; and whether he brought it nearer or moved it away, to right or left, or even turned it round his head, so long as DE made an angle of about 42 degrees with EM — the line from the centre of the eye toward the sun's centre — D always looked equally red; but as soon as he made the angle the least bit larger the red vanished, and if a little smaller it did not vanish at once but first divided into two less brilliant parts showing yellow, blue and other colours. Looking at the part K, he saw that at an angle of about 52 degrees K too appeared red, though less brilliant, with weaker colours at a slightly larger angle and none at a slightly smaller or much larger one. So if all the air toward M were full of such globes, or drops, a very bright red point must appear in each drop whose line to the eye makes about 42 degrees with EM, and these points, seen together and located only by the angle under which they are seen, must look like a continuous red circle, with circles of weaker colours in drops at slightly smaller angles: this is the primary rainbow. Likewise a red circle at 52 degrees and weaker colours beyond it make the secondary bow, and no colours appear in other drops. Then, examining what made D red, he found it was rays from A bending at B on entering the water, going to C, reflecting to D and bending again on leaving toward E, for if he put an opaque body anywhere on AB, BC, CD or DE the red vanished, while if he covered the whole flask except B and D, provided nothing blocked ABCDE, it still appeared. The red at K came from rays entering at G, bending toward H, reflecting to I and again to K, and bending out toward E. So the primary bow is caused by rays reaching the eye after two refractions and one reflection, the secondary by rays after two refractions and two reflections, which is why it is weaker. The discourse opens as a demonstration of the method: the subject is chosen because it defeated predecessors. The reasoning follows the precepts in order. The bow seen in fountains shows the phenomenon depends only on sunlit drops, which removes the cloud from the problem; the drop is enlarged to a flask, a model that preserves the relevant property (roundness) while making the paths of light observable; and the paths are isolated by occluding each segment in turn, a controlled experiment in the strict sense. The insight that the circle is the locus of drops seen at a fixed angle from the anti-solar line explains at once why the bow is circular, centred on the shadow of the observer's head, and different for each observer.

(AT 329–331) The main difficulty remained: why, when many other rays can reach the eye after two refractions and one or two reflections with the flask in other positions, only those described produce colours. To resolve it he looked for another subject where colours appear in the same way, to judge their cause by comparison, and remembered the crystal prism. With a prism MNP whose flat faces MN and NP are inclined at about thirty or forty degrees, sunlight ABC crossing MN at right angles suffers no sensible refraction there but a large one leaving through NP. Covering NP with an opaque body having a fairly narrow opening DE, he saw the rays passing through it paint all the rainbow's colours on a white cloth or paper FGH, always red toward F and blue or violet toward H. He learned first that the curvature of the drops' surfaces is not needed for these colours, since the prism's faces are flat; nor the size of the angle under which they appear, since it can be changed here without changing them, and though one can make the rays toward F bend more or less than those toward H, they always paint red and blue respectively; nor reflection, since there is none here; nor several refractions, since there is only one. But at least one refraction is needed, and one whose effect is not undone by a contrary one, since experience shows that with parallel faces, the rays straightening in one as much as they bent in the other, no colours appear. Light is needed, since without it nothing is seen. And shadow, or a limit to the light, is needed: if the opaque body on NP is removed the colours FGH disappear, and if the opening DE is made large enough the red, orange and yellow toward F extend no further, nor the green, blue and violet toward H, but all the space between toward G stays white. Next he tried to understand why the colours differ at H and F though refraction, shadow and light concur in the same way at both. The prism is chosen as a second instance of the same effect, and comparison between the two cases eliminates conditions one by one: curvature, angle, reflection and number of refractions are shown inessential, while one uncompensated refraction, light and a boundary of shadow are shown necessary. This is the fourth precept used as a method of discovery, close to what Bacon called tables of presence and absence. The finding that colour requires a shadow boundary is the key observation, since a wide aperture leaves white in the middle and colours only at the edges.

(AT 331–337) Conceiving light as described in the Optics, as the action or motion of a very fine matter whose parts are like small balls rolling in the pores of terrestrial bodies, he saw that these balls can roll in different ways according to the causes that determine them, that all refractions toward one side make them turn in the same sense, and that where they have no neighbours moving notably faster or slower, their rotation is about equal to their straight motion; but where they have, on one side, neighbours moving more slowly and on the other side neighbours moving faster or equally fast, as at the boundaries of light and shadow, they rotate less fast than they move straight if they meet the slower ones on the side toward which they roll, as in the ray EH, and faster if they meet them on the other side, as in the ray DF. To understand this: a ball 1234 pushed from V to X in a straight line, its sides 1 and 3 descending equally to the water's surface YY, has its side 3, meeting the surface first, retarded while side 1 continues, so it necessarily begins to turn in the order 1 2 3. If it is surrounded by four others, Q and R tending toward X more strongly than it, S and T less strongly, then Q pressing its part 1 and S holding back its part 3 increase its rotation, while R and T do not hinder it — this explains the ray DF; conversely, if Q and R tend more slowly and S and T more strongly, R hinders the rotation of part 1 and T that of part 3 — this explains the ray EH. But the ball, being very round, may, when pressed rather hard by R and T, turn about the axis 4 2 instead of stopping its rotation, and, changing position in a moment, then turn in the order 3 2 1, since R and T, having started to turn it, oblige it to complete a half-turn, after which they increase its rotation instead of retarding it. This resolved the chief of all his difficulties. It seems to him very evidently demonstrated that the colours toward F consist only in the parts of the fine matter tending to rotate more strongly than they move in a straight line — much more strongly giving red, a little more yellow — and those toward H in their rotating less fast than usual — a little less giving green, much less blue — with a crimson mixed at the edges of the blue, making violet or purple, because the cause that usually retards rotation, strong enough then to turn some parts over, increases it in them while diminishing it in others. Reason agrees so perfectly with experience here that he thinks no one who knows both well can doubt it. For if the sensation of light is caused by the motion or inclination to motion of some matter touching our eyes, its different motions must cause different sensations, and since there can be no other diversity in these motions than the one described, so we find no other diversity in our sensations of them than that of colours. Nothing in the prism can produce colours except the way it sends the parts of the fine matter to the cloth and thence to our eyes, so one should look for nothing else in the colours other objects show, since light or white, shadow or black, and the rainbow's colours suffice to compose all others. He cannot accept the philosophers' distinction between true colours and false or apparent ones: since their whole true nature is only to appear, it seems a contradiction to call them false and say they appear. He grants that shadow and refraction are not always needed to produce them, since the size, shape, position and motion of the parts of so-called coloured bodies can combine variously with light to increase or diminish the rotation of the fine matter's parts. Even in the rainbow he at first doubted whether the colours arose just as in the prism, since he saw no shadow limiting the light and did not yet know why they appeared only at certain angles, until, taking up his pen and calculating in detail all the rays falling on the various points of a drop, to see under what angles they could reach the eye after two refractions and one or two reflections, he found that after one reflection and two refractions far more can be seen at 41 to 42 degrees than at any smaller angle and none at a larger; and after two reflections and two refractions far more at 51 to 52 degrees than at any larger angle and none at a smaller. So there is shadow on both sides limiting the light, for not to receive rays, or notably fewer from one object than from one beside it, is to see shadow. This shows the bow's colours have the same cause as the prism's, that the inner bow's radius cannot exceed 42 degrees nor the outer's be less than 51, and that the first must be more sharply bounded on its outside than its inside, the second the reverse, as experience shows. The theory of colour is the discourse's most ambitious claim and its least successful part. Colour is identified with the ratio of rotational to rectilinear speed of the globules, a mechanical variable for which the boundary of light and shadow supplies the cause, and the argument that our sensations of colour vary along one dimension because the motions vary along one is a correspondence between two orderings, consistent with the non-resemblance doctrine of Optics IV and VI. Newton's prism experiments of 1666–72 would show that white light is a mixture and that refrangibility differs by colour, which the globule theory cannot accommodate. The rejection of "apparent" colours applies the definition of colour as an appearance: if colour simply is how bodies affect sight, the scholastic contrast between real colours in bodies and apparent ones in rainbows collapses. The decisive move is the calculation: the shadow the prism needed is supplied in the rainbow by the concentration of rays at an extreme angle, which explains both why the bow appears only at about 42 and 52 degrees and why there is a dark band between the bows, the band later named after Alexander of Aphrodisias.

(AT 337–338) So that mathematicians can judge whether his calculation is correct enough, he explains it. Let AFD be a drop whose radius CD or AB is divided into as many equal parts as rays to be calculated, so as to give each the same amount of light. Take one ray EF, which instead of going straight to G turns toward K, reflects from K to N and goes to the eye P, or reflects again from N to Q and turns toward the eye R. Drawing CI perpendicular to FK, by the Optics AE or HF and CI stand in the ratio that measures water's refraction, so if HF is 8000 parts of which AB is 10000, CI is about 5984, since water's refraction is a little greater than three to four and, as exactly as he could measure it, as 187 to 250. Knowing HF and CI, he easily finds the arcs FG, 73 degrees 44 minutes, and FK, 106 degrees 30 minutes. Subtracting twice FK from FG plus 180 degrees gives 40 degrees 44 minutes for the angle ONP, ON being parallel to EF; subtracting 40.44 from FK gives 65 degrees 46 minutes for SQR, SQ also being parallel to EF. Calculating all the other rays parallel to EF that pass through the divisions of AB in the same way, he composes the table that follows, from HF 1000 to 10000. The calculation is an early piece of numerical ray-tracing: equally spaced parallel rays model uniform illumination, the sine law gives each ray's internal path, and simple angle arithmetic gives the emergent deviation. The refractive index 250/187, about 1.337, is a good value for water. By publishing the procedure and the numbers, Descartes submits the result to the checking he invited in Part Six of the Discourse (AT 75).

(AT 338–340) The table shows that far more rays make the angle ONP about 40 degrees than make it smaller, and SQR about 54 than make it larger. To make it more precise he computes a second table for HF from 8000 to 9800, from which the largest ONP is 41 degrees 30 minutes and the smallest SQR 51 degrees 54 minutes; adding or subtracting about 17 minutes for the sun's semi-diameter gives 41 degrees 47 minutes for the greatest radius of the inner bow and 51 degrees 37 minutes for the least of the outer. The refinement of the table near the turning value is the procedure of finding an extremum by tabulation, which concentrates computation where the function changes direction. The rays pile up at the minimum deviation, so the extremum is what makes the colours appear: the bow is a caustic, a concentration of light at the stationary angle. Allowing for the sun's apparent size turns a value for point sources into an observable width.

(AT 340–341) When water is warm its refraction is slightly smaller than when cold, which can change the calculation somewhat; yet it cannot increase the inner bow's radius by more than a degree or two at most, and then the outer's will be smaller by nearly twice as much. This is worth noting, because it shows that water's refraction can be neither much smaller nor much greater than he supposes: a little greater would make the inner bow's radius less than 41 degrees, whereas common opinion gives it 45; and if refraction is made small enough to make it truly 45, the outer bow's will be hardly more than 45 too, whereas it looks much larger to the eye than the inner. Maurolycus, he believes the first to fix the inner bow at 45 degrees, puts the outer at about 56, which shows how little trust observations deserve when not accompanied by the true reason. As to why red is outside in the inner bow and inside in the outer, the cause that puts red toward F rather than H in the prism means that, looking at the prism from the cloth's position, one sees red toward its thicker part MP and blue toward N, since the red ray going to F comes from C, the part of the sun nearest MP; and since the drops' centres, their thickest part, lie outside the coloured points of the inner bow, red must appear outside there, and inside in the outer bow, where the centres lie inside. The theory constrains a physical constant: the observed bow fixes water's refractive index within narrow bounds, which inverts the usual direction of inference. The correction of Maurolycus's 45 degrees, which had become the accepted value, is the method's point stated outright: observation without the reason gives authority to errors. The colour order of the two bows follows from the prism analogy and the geometry of the paths.

(AT 341–342) He thinks no difficulty remains, unless perhaps the irregularities: an arc not exactly round, or its centre not on the line through eye and sun, which can happen if winds change the drops' shape, since the slightest loss of roundness makes a notable difference in the angle. He has been told of a rainbow seen inverted, its horns turned upward, which he can attribute only to sunlight reflected from the sea or a lake: rays from SS falling on the water DAE and reflected toward the rain CF make the eye B see the arc FF centred at C, where CB extended to A, AS passing through the sun's centre, makes the angles SAD and BAE equal, and the angle CBF is about 42 degrees. This also requires no wind ruffling the water toward E, and perhaps a cloud G preventing direct sunlight from effacing the light the water sends to the rain, which is why it is rare. The eye may also be so placed that one sees the lower part completing the bow's circle without the upper, and takes it for an inverted bow though seen toward the water or ground. Irregular bows are explained by a sensitivity the calculation makes precise: small departures from sphericity shift the angle. The inverted bow, known only by report, receives a conditional explanation — a reflected sun as source — together with the conditions that make it rare, which is the right posture for hearsay. The second explanation, a lower arc mistaken for an inverted one, shows how the geometry of the cone of 42 degrees can make one phenomenon look like another.

(AT 342–343) He has also been told of a third rainbow above the two ordinary ones, much weaker and about as far from the second as the second from the first. He can attribute it only to very round, transparent hailstones mixed with the rain, in which refraction being notably greater than in water, the outer bow would be much larger and appear above the other; their inner bow, smaller than the rain's, might have gone unnoticed beside its brightness, or their ends might have joined so that they were counted as one, with colours arranged unusually. The explanation is derived, not guessed: a higher refractive index enlarges the secondary bow, as the calculation implies. The explanation was wrong, since hail is not transparent enough or spherical enough, and the supernumerary arcs actually observed near the bows require the wave theory of light (Young, 1804). The argument still shows the theory being used to predict from a change of parameter.

(AT 343–344) This reminds him of a way to make signs appear in the sky that would cause great wonder in those ignorant of the reasons. One already knows how to make a rainbow with a fountain: water from small holes ABC rising high and spreading toward R, with the sun at Z so that, ZEM being straight, the angle MER is about 42 degrees, the eye E will see the iris at R just like the sky's. There are oils, spirits and other liquids in which refraction is notably greater or smaller than in water and which are no less clear, so several fountains of such liquids arranged in order — those of greatest refraction nearest the spectators and not rising so high as to block the view of those behind — would fill a large part of the sky with the iris's colours. Since closing some holes makes any part of the iris disappear, opening and closing the holes of these fountains at the right times could make the coloured region take the shape of a cross, a column or anything else that causes wonder. It would need skill and expense to proportion the fountains and make the liquids rise high enough for the figures to be seen far off by a whole people without the artifice being discovered. The essay began by promising to remove wonder (AT 231) and closes its central discourse by showing how knowledge could manufacture it. A cross appearing in the sky is the most famous of all signs, so the remark has a sharp edge: a well-understood cause can counterfeit a portent, which is a reason to examine portents rather than believe them. The fountain display is also a genuine application, an artificial meteor built from the theory, in the spirit of the practical philosophy of Part Six.

### Ninth Discourse: On the Colour of Clouds and the Circles or Coronas Sometimes Seen Around the Stars

(AT 345–348) After what has been said of colours, little remains about those of clouds. Their whiteness and darkness come only from their being more or less exposed to the light of the stars, or to shadow, their own or their neighbours'. Two points only. First, the surfaces of transparent bodies reflect part of the rays falling on them (AT 196–197), which is why light passes better through three pikes' depth of water than through a little foam, which is only water but with many surfaces, each reflecting part of the light until almost none remains; so neither crushed glass, nor snow, nor thick clouds can be transparent. Second, although luminous bodies only push the fine matter in straight lines, the ordinary motion of its parts, at least of those in the air around us, is to roll as a ball rolls on the ground though pushed straight, and bodies that make them roll so are called white, as all those must be that lack transparency only because of their many surfaces — foam, crushed glass, snow and clouds. So the clear sky looks blue: of itself it gives no light and would look extremely black without exhalations or vapours above us, but there are always some that reflect rays toward us, and when there are many, the fine matter reflected by the first meets others that make its parts roll before reaching us, so the sky looks white, while if there are not enough to make them rotate, it looks only blue, as was said of blue. The same cause makes very pure, deep sea water look blue, since few rays are reflected from its surface and none of those entering return. At sunset or sunrise the whole side of the sky toward the sun often looks red, when there are not so many fogs between it and us that its light cannot pass, but it passes less easily near the ground than a little higher, and less there than much higher; refracted in the fogs, it determines the fine matter's parts to rotate as a ball rolling from that side, so the rotation of the lower ones is always increased by the higher, which act more strongly, and this suffices to make red, which reflected in the clouds can spread across the sky. This red in the morning foretells winds or rain, since it shows there are few clouds to the east, so the sun can raise many vapours before noon, and that the fogs causing it are beginning to rise; in the evening it foretells fine weather, since there are few or no clouds to the west, the east winds must prevail, and the fogs descend in the night. The colour theory of Discourse VIII is applied to the sky. Whiteness from many surfaces is explained as before, while the blue of the sky is derived from a deficit of rotation in light scattered once from sparse vapours, a mechanism for what Rayleigh scattering would later explain. Morning and evening red are given a physical cause and connected to the weather proverb ("red sky at morning…") through the wind theory of Discourse IV, so the proverb gets a reason.

(AT 348–351) He will not dwell on other colours seen in clouds, whose causes are all sufficiently contained in what he has said. But certain circles sometimes appear around the stars, which must be explained. They resemble the rainbow in being round, or nearly so, and always surrounding the sun or another star, which shows they are caused by some reflection or refraction whose angles are nearly all equal; and in being coloured, which shows refraction and shadow limiting the light. They differ in that the rainbow is seen only where it is actually raining, though often not where the observer is, whereas these are never seen where it rains, which shows they are caused not by refraction in drops or hail but in the small transparent ice stars described above, since no other cause capable of the effect can be imagined in clouds; and though such stars are seen to fall only in cold weather, reason assures us they form in all seasons, and since some warmth is needed to make them transparent instead of white, summer is perhaps more suitable than winter. Although most look perfectly flat, they are all slightly thicker in the middle than at the edges, as some visibly are, and according as they are more or less so, the circles are larger or smaller, for there are no doubt several sizes; if those most often observed have a diameter of about 45 degrees, as some have written, he supposes the ice particles causing them have their most ordinary convexity, perhaps the greatest they usually acquire without melting completely. With the sun ABC, the eye D, and transparent ice particles E, F, G side by side as they form, so convex that a ray from A on the edge of G and from C on the edge of F returns toward D, with many others through the particles toward E but none through those beyond the circle GG, the rays AD, CD passing straight show the sun at its usual size, while the others, refracted toward EE, make the whole area within FF fairly bright and its circumference between FF and GG a crown painted with the rainbow's colours, red inside toward F and blue outside toward G, as is usually observed. With two or more layers of ice particles, rays crossing two by their edges, bending almost twice as much, produce another coloured circle much larger but less visible, so that two crowns appear one inside the other, the inner better painted, as has sometimes been observed.
