Crowns do not usually form around stars low on the horizon, since the rays meet the particles too obliquely to cross them; their colours are less vivid than the rainbow's, being caused by much smaller refractions; and they appear more often around the moon, even around stars, since they depend on fewer reflections and refractions and need less strong light; and they are often only white, not for lack of light but because their matter is not wholly transparent. The coronas are distinguished from the rainbow by a single observed difference — they never appear where it rains — which eliminates drops and points to the ice stars of Discourse VI, an inference by elimination tied back to Descartes's own observations. The halo is modelled with ice particles as weak lenses, and the ordinary 45-degree diameter is explained by their typical convexity, while a second outer ring is predicted from double passage. Ice prisms of hexagonal form, not lenticular plates, were later recognised as the true cause of the 22-degree halo, though the basic idea that halos come from refraction in ice crystals is correct. The colour order, red inside, opposite to the primary bow, follows from the model and matches observation.

(AT 351) One could imagine others formed in imitation of the rainbow in drops: first by two refractions without reflection, but then nothing fixes their diameter and the light is not limited by shadow, as colour requires; then by two refractions and three or four reflections, but their light, being very weak, is easily effaced by that reflected from the drops' surfaces, which makes him doubt they ever appear, and the calculation shows their diameter would be much larger than that of those usually observed. Possible alternatives are examined and excluded by two conditions — a determinate angle and a shadow boundary — and by intensity and calculated size. The higher-order rainbows dismissed here were later observed in the laboratory and, rarely, in nature, but the reasoning that weakness after many reflections makes them practically invisible is sound.

(AT 351–354) Those seen round lamps and torches must be sought not in the air but in the eye. Travelling by ship at night last summer, having kept his head all evening on a hand that closed his right eye while he looked at the sky with the other, when a candle was brought and he opened both eyes, he saw two crowns round the flame with colours as vivid as he had ever seen in a rainbow: the larger AB red toward A and blue toward B, the smaller CD red toward C but white toward D and extending to the flame. Closing the right eye again, the crowns disappeared; opening and closing the left, they remained, which assured him they came from some disposition his right eye had acquired while closed, making some of the flame's rays deviate so as to spread over the spaces where they painted the two crowns. He does not determine that disposition, since several could cause the same effect: one or two small wrinkles in one of the eye's surfaces, spreading circularly because of the eye's shape, as straight ones crossing on the axis make us see long rays round torches; something opaque spread circularly between the cornea and the lens or to one side; or some change of temper or shape of the humours or skins, since people with sore eyes commonly see such crowns, and they do not look alike to all. Only, their outer part is usually red, contrary to those round the stars, because here the crystalline humour plays the role of the prism and the back of the eye that of the white cloth. One may wonder why the crystalline does not colour all objects so; but the rays from each point of an object to each point of the back of the eye pass some through one side of it and some through the other, and their actions, being contrary, cancel as regards colour, whereas here the rays going to the crown pass only through one side. All this agrees so well with what he said of the nature of colours that it can, he thinks, do much to confirm its truth. The experiment is accidental but controlled: switching between eyes isolates the cause in the right eye, which locates the phenomenon in the observer rather than the medium, a case of what Discourse VII called things that appear without being there. Several mechanisms are left open where the evidence does not decide, in keeping with Part Six on underdetermination. The colour order reversed relative to atmospheric coronas is predicted by identifying the eye's parts with the prism apparatus, and the objection that the lens should colour everything is met by the cancellation of opposite actions, the condition of AT 330 that refraction must not be undone by a contrary one. The confirmation by an unplanned observation that fits the theory is the kind of evidence Part Six valued most.

### Last Discourse: On the Appearance of Several Suns

(AT 354–358) Other circles are sometimes seen in clouds, always white, which instead of having a star at their centre usually pass through the sun or moon and seem parallel or nearly parallel to the horizon. Since they appear only in the great round clouds described above (AT 290–291), and several suns or moons are sometimes seen in the same clouds, both must be explained together. Let A be the south, where the sun is, with a warm wind toward B, and C the north, with a cold wind also toward B; these winds meet or gather a cloud of snow so deep and wide that they cannot pass over, under or between, as usual, but must flow round it. So they round it, and the warm south wind melts a little of the snow at its edge, which, refrozen at once by the north wind and by the unmelted snow within, can form a great ring of continuous transparent ice, its surface well polished because the winds rounding it are very uniform; the ice is thicker on the side DEF, exposed to the warm wind and the sun, than on GHI, where the snow could not melt so easily. In such conditions, and without storm, there cannot be enough warmth round the cloud to form this ice without enough in the ground beneath to raise vapours that hold it up. So the sun's light, high toward the south, striking the ice ring all round and reflecting onto the white snow beside it, must make the snow look like a great white circle to those below; it suffices that the cloud be round and a little denser at its edge than in the middle, even without the ice ring. When the ring is formed, one below at K can see up to six suns set in the white circle like diamonds in a ring: the first at E, by direct rays; two at D and F, by rays refracted through the ice where its thickness diminishes, bending inward on both sides as through the prism, so these suns have red edges on the side toward E, where the ice is thickest, and blue on the other; the fourth at H by reflection; and the last two, also by reflection, at G and I, on a circle centred at K through B, the cloud's centre, so that the angles KGB and KBG, or BGA, are equal, and likewise KIB and KBI, or BIA, since reflection is always at equal angles and the polished ice must show the sun wherever its rays can reflect to the eye. Direct rays being brighter than refracted, and these than reflected, the sun at E must look brighter than at D or F, and these than G, H or I, which have no coloured edges but are only white. Observers nearer B see only four suns; those far back toward H or C see only D, E, F, G and I; farther still, only D, E, F, no longer in a white circle but crossed by a white bar; and with the sun too low to light GHI, or the ring not yet formed, only the three D, E, F. The ice ring is a hypothesis built from earlier materials — the large round cloud pressed by surrounding winds (AT 290–291), melting and refreezing of snow, the prism and the law of reflection — and from it a whole family of appearances follows, each tied to the observer's position. The parhelic circle, the colours of the refracted parhelia and the whiteness of the reflected ones are all predicted by the type of path, which makes them the strongest evidence for the model. The number of suns visible from different positions is derived as a function of the observer's place, as the rainbow was a function of angle.

(AT 358–360) So far he has considered only the plane of the cloud; its profile shows more. Though the sun is not on the straight line from E to the eye K but higher or lower, it should still appear there, especially if the ice does not extend too far in height or depth, since its surface will then be so curved that wherever the sun is, it can almost always send its rays toward K: if the ice's thickness has the figure between the lines 1 2 3 and 4 5 6, the rays can reach K whether the sun is on the line A 2, much lower on S 1 or much higher on T 3, and so always appear as if at E, the ring being not very wide. This can make the sun appear even after it has set, and advance or retard the shadow of sundials so that they mark quite another hour. If the sun is much lower than it appears at E, so that its rays also pass straight beneath the ice to K, a seventh sun appears below the six, and, having most light, effaces the shadow the others might cast on sundials; likewise a seventh above the six if its rays pass straight over the ice and the cloud is not too opaque. If the ice extends higher and lower, three suns can appear one above another at E, three at D and three at F, up to twelve set in the white circle; with the sun a little lower or higher, three at E again, two in the circle and one below or above, and two at D and F. But he does not know that so many have ever been observed at once, nor, when three have been seen one above another, as has happened several times, others beside them, nor, when three have been seen side by side, as has also happened, others above or below. The reason is no doubt that the width of the ice usually bears no proportion to the size of the whole cloud's circuit, so the eye must be very close to E to distinguish three suns one above another there, and very far away for the rays bending toward D and F, where the ice thins most, to reach it. The profile adds a vertical dimension to the model and yields predictions about sundials and suns seen after sunset, practical consequences of an optical illusion. The model generates more configurations (up to twelve suns) than have been reported, and Descartes explains the gap by a geometrical incompatibility of observer positions, so that the observed combinations are exactly those the theory allows together. This reasoning from what has not been observed, as well as from what has, is a test of the theory's consistency with negative evidence.

(AT 361–362) It is rare for a cloud to be whole enough to show more than three at once. Yet the king of Poland is said to have seen six in 1625, and three years ago the mathematician of Tübingen observed the four marked D, E, F and H, noting especially in his account that D and F were red toward the middle one E, which he calls the true sun, and blue on the other side, and that the fourth, H, was very pale and hardly visible — which strongly confirms what Descartes has said. But the finest and most remarkable observation he has seen on this subject is of the five suns that appeared at Rome on 20 March 1629, around two or three in the afternoon, and so that the reader can judge whether it agrees with his account, he gives it in the very terms in which it was then published. The Latin report describes, for the observer at A with the zenith B and the true sun C, two incomplete concentric coloured irises round the sun — the inner DEF fuller and more perfect but open from D to F and constantly trying to close, the outer GHI weak and unstable though coloured — and a third, very large, all-white circle KLMN, eccentric, passing through the sun's middle, complete at first but toward the end weak and broken from M toward N; at the intersections of this circle with the outer iris appeared two parhelia N and K, not very perfect, N the stronger, their middles rivalling the sun but their sides painted with rainbow colours, irregular in outline; N, an unquiet apparition, threw out a thick, fiery tail NOP with alternating motion; L and M beyond the zenith were less vivid but rounder and white like the circle, like milk or pure silver, M having nearly vanished by the middle of the third hour; N disappeared before K, which grew stronger as N faded and was the last to disappear. The Tübingen report, usually ascribed to Wilhelm Schickard, supplies independent confirmation of a detail — the colour order of the refracted parhelia — that the model predicts. The Roman parhelia of 1629, observed by Christoph Scheiner and circulated through Gassendi and Reneri, were the occasion that turned Descartes to meteorology in 1629, as his letters to Mersenne that autumn show, so the essay closes by returning to its own starting point. Quoting the report verbatim, rather than paraphrasing it into agreement, lets the reader check the fit, the publication of evidence that Part Six demanded.

(AT 362–364) CKLMN was a white circle in which five suns were seen; one must imagine the observer at A with this circle hanging above him, so that B answered to the top of his head and the two suns L and M were behind his shoulders when he faced the other three, K, C and N. K and N were coloured at their edges and neither as round nor as bright as C, which shows they were caused by refraction; L and M were fairly round but less bright, and all white, without any other colour at their edges, which shows they were caused by reflection. Several things may have prevented a sixth sun at V, the likeliest being that the eye was so close, given the cloud's height, that all the rays falling on the ice there reflected beyond A. Though B is not shown as close to L and M as to the cloud's centre, this does not prevent the rule about where they must appear from being observed, since the observer, nearer the arc LVM than the rest of the circle, must have judged it larger by comparison than it was, besides which these clouds are never perfectly round, though they look so. The Roman report is read through the model point by point. Coloured edges and irregular shape mark the refracted suns, white roundness the reflected ones, and the missing sixth sun is explained by the observer's position, as at AT 357–358. The adjustment for perceived size uses Optics VI's doctrine that size is judged by estimated distance (AT 140). The admission that real clouds are never perfectly round marks how much the geometry idealises.

(AT 364) Two more things are notable. First, the sun N, toward the west, with a changing, uncertain shape, threw out a thick tail of fire NOP, now longer, now shorter, which was no doubt only the sun's image distorted and irregular there, as one often sees it floating in slightly trembling water or through a window pane with uneven surfaces. For the ice was probably a little agitated there and its surfaces not so regular, because it was beginning to dissolve, as is proved by the white circle being broken, almost absent, between M and N, and by the sun N vanishing before K, which seemed to strengthen as the other faded. The "fiery tail," the most portentous detail in the report, is reduced to an image in an imperfect mirror, with ordinary analogies — trembling water, uneven glass. The inference that the ice was melting on that side links three separate observations (the tail, the broken circle, the order of disappearance) to one cause, which is the strongest kind of confirmation the essay offers.

(AT 364–366) Second, there were two crowns round the sun C, painted with the rainbow's colours, the inner DEF much more vivid than the outer GHI, so that he does not doubt they were caused, as he explained, by refraction not in the continuous ice where K and N were seen, but in other ice divided into many small particles above and below it. It is very likely that the same cause that formed a whole ring of ice from some outer parts of the cloud disposed others nearby to show these crowns; so if they are not always seen with several suns, it is because the cloud's thickness does not always extend beyond its ring of ice, or is too opaque to see them through. They are always seen round the true sun and have no connection with the apparent ones; though K and N here lie at the intersection of the outer crown with the white circle, that was by chance, and he is sure it was not seen so in places a little away from Rome where the same phenomenon was observed. Yet he does not think their centre is always exactly on the line from the eye to the sun, as the rainbow's is: water drops, being round, always refract alike in whatever position, whereas flat ice particles refract more the more obliquely they are viewed, and since those formed by a wind circling a cloud's edge must lie differently from those formed above or below, two crowns may be seen one within the other, nearly the same size and not exactly concentric. The coronas of the Roman report are referred to the halo theory of Discourse IX, and their coincidence with the parhelia K and N is declared accidental, with a prediction: observers elsewhere will not have seen the coincidence, since the positions of parhelia depend on the observer. The difference between round drops and flat particles explains a fine detail, non-concentric double halos, which shows the model's resolution.

(AT 366) Besides the winds round such a cloud, another may pass over or under it and form another ice surface there, causing further variations in this phenomenon, as can the surrounding clouds or rain if it falls there, since rays reflected from one cloud's ice onto the drops will show parts of rainbows in very various positions; and spectators not beneath such a cloud but beside it, among several, can see other circles and other suns. He thinks he need say no more, for he hopes that those who have understood all that this treatise has said will see nothing in the clouds in future whose cause they cannot easily understand, or that gives them occasion for wonder. The open-ended variations are left to the reader, who now has the principles to generate them, the pedagogy of Discourse Part Six (AT 72) applied to physics. The last sentence closes the circle opened at AT 231: the essay promised to remove wonder from the heavens, and it ends by claiming to have done so. This is the Discourse's programme carried through on the subject where wonder, signs and divine agency had the strongest hold.

## Geometry

(AT 368) In the advertisement Descartes says that until now he has tried to make himself intelligible to everyone, but he fears this treatise can be read only by those who already know what is in the books of geometry; since those books contain many well-demonstrated truths, he thought it superfluous to repeat them, though he has not failed to use them. The Geometry reverses the Discourse's address to natural reason in the vernacular (AT 77): it presupposes Euclid and Apollonius and proceeds without the steps a beginner needs. The terseness was deliberate as well as economical. The text says so (AT 374), and the letters of 1637–39 add that omissions were left to keep others from claiming they had known everything already. Its difficulty created a commentary tradition at once, from Florimond de Beaune's notes to Frans van Schooten's Latin editions (1649, 1659), whose comments the asterisks in AT's text mark. The French Geometry has no Latin counterpart in this volume, so it is read on its own terms.

### First Book: On Problems That Can Be Constructed Using Only Circles and Straight Lines

(AT 369) All problems of geometry can easily be reduced to such terms that one then need know only the lengths of certain straight lines to construct them. The opening sentence states the programme of the whole treatise. Every geometrical problem is to be converted into the determination of line segments, so that a single kind of magnitude carries all the content. This is the Regulae's mathesis universalis in practice: Rule XVIII reduced all operations to the comparison of lines, and the Discourse spoke of representing proportions by lines (AT 20). Once the unknown is a segment, the problem has the form of a quantity sought among known quantities, which algebra can handle.

(AT 369–370; margin: "How the calculation of arithmetic relates to the operations of geometry") As all arithmetic consists of four or five operations — addition, subtraction, multiplication, division and extraction of roots, which may be taken as a kind of division — so in geometry, to prepare the lines sought to be known, one need only add or subtract other lines; or, having one line, called unity to relate it better to numbers, which can usually be chosen at will, and two others, find a fourth that is to one of them as the other is to unity, which is multiplication; or a fourth that is to one of them as unity is to the other, which is division; or one, two or more mean proportionals between unity and another line, which is extracting a square, cube or other root. He will not hesitate to bring these arithmetical terms into geometry, to make himself more intelligible. The arbitrary unit segment is the decisive innovation. In Greek geometry the product of two lines is a rectangle, an area, and products of more than three lines have no meaning; with a unit, the product is a fourth proportional and therefore another line. Segments thus form a system closed under the arithmetical operations, so algebraic expressions of any degree can denote lines. The phrase "to make myself more intelligible" understates what is a conceptual change in what a geometrical magnitude is, the change Jacob Klein analysed in Greek Mathematical Thought and the Origin of Algebra as the passage from determinate multitudes to symbolic quantity.

(AT 370; margin: "Multiplication") If AB is the unit and BD is to be multiplied by BC, one need only join A and C and draw DE parallel to CA, and BE is the product. The construction is the intercept theorem: from the similar triangles, BE is to BD as BC is to AB, so BE equals BD times BC when AB is one. The product is obtained by ruler alone, given a way to draw parallels, so the arithmetical operation is legitimated as a construction in the classical sense.

(AT 370; margin: "Division") To divide BE by BD, join E and D and draw AC parallel to DE, and BC is the quotient. Division is the same figure read backwards. The pairing shows that the inverse operations correspond to one configuration traversed in two directions, the way analysis and synthesis traverse one chain of reasons.

(AT 370–371; margin: "Extraction of the square root") To extract the square root of GH, add to it in a straight line FG, the unit, bisect FH at K, describe from K the circle FIH, and raise from G a perpendicular to meet the circle at I: GI is the root. Nothing is said here of cube and other roots, to be treated more conveniently later. The construction is Euclid VI.13, the mean proportional between the unit and GH. That the square root needs a circle while the other operations need only straight lines, and that the cube root is deferred, already marks the boundary that organises the treatise: square roots are constructible with ruler and compass, cube roots require curves beyond the circle (Book III).

(AT 371; margin: "How one can use letters in geometry") Often one need not draw these lines on paper; it suffices to designate them by letters, one letter each. To add BD to GH, call one a and the other b and write a + b; a − b to subtract; ab to multiply; a/b to divide; aa or a² to multiply a by itself, a³ to multiply it again by a, and so on to infinity; √(a² + b²) for the square root of a² + b², and √C.(a³ − b³ + abb) for the cube root of a³ − b³ + abb, and so on. The notation that became standard is introduced here: single letters for lines, exponents for powers, the radical for roots. It fulfils the Regulae's advice (Rule XVI) to designate magnitudes by the briefest possible signs so that memory is relieved, and the Discourse's resolve to express proportions "by the shortest possible symbols" (AT 20). The letters designate lines, not numbers or Viète's species of magnitude, but since lines now form an arithmetic the distinction ceases to matter in calculation.

(AT 371) By a², b³ and the like he usually conceives only simple lines, though he calls them squares or cubes to use the names customary in algebra. The traditional names are kept while their meaning changes: a "cube" is a line obtained by two multiplications with respect to the unit, not a solid. This one sentence removes the dimensional ceiling that, as the Pappus quotation will show (AT 378), stopped the ancients at products of three lines.

(AT 371–372) All the parts of one line should usually be expressed by as many dimensions one as another when the unit is not determined in the question — as here a³ has as many as abb or b³ in the line called √C.(a³ − b³ + abb) — but not when the unit is determined, since it can be understood wherever there are too many or too few dimensions: to extract the cube root of aabb − b, one must think of aabb as divided once by the unit and b as multiplied twice by it. The law of homogeneity, which Viète had made a rule of algebra, becomes a convention that can be restored at will by inserting powers of the unit. Dimension is therefore relative to the choice of unit rather than a property of the magnitude, which is what allows terms of different degree to be added.

(AT 372) So as not to forget the names of the lines, one should always keep a separate register as they are introduced or changed, writing for example AB ∞ 1, that is, AB equal to 1; GH ∞ a; BD ∞ b; and so on. The symbol ∞ is Descartes's sign of equality, which was later displaced by Recorde's =. The register is an external memory, the paper aid that Regulae XVI recommended for whatever the mind need not hold before it while it reasons, and it applies the fourth precept's concern with omitting nothing.

(AT 372–373; margin: "How one must come to the equations that serve to solve problems") To solve a problem one must first consider it as already done and give names to all the lines that seem needed to construct it, unknown as well as known. Then, making no distinction between known and unknown, one must go through the difficulty in the order that shows most naturally how they depend on one another, until one finds a way to express one quantity in two ways: this is called an equation, since the terms of one way are equal to those of the other. One must find as many such equations as one has supposed unknown lines; if there are fewer, and nothing in the question has been omitted, the question is not entirely determined, and one can then take known lines at will for all the unknowns to which no equation corresponds. This is the method of the treatise in one paragraph. "Considering it as already done" is the analysis of the ancients as Pappus describes it in Book VII of the Collection, now made mechanical by naming every line with a letter. Treating known and unknown alike is the decisive step, since the unknown can then enter calculations as if it were given. The order "that shows most naturally how they depend on one another" is the third precept of the Discourse (AT 18–19). The count of equations against unknowns gives a criterion of determinacy, and the indeterminate case is not discarded: with one equation short, an unknown can be chosen freely, and this is exactly the situation of the Pappus problem, where the solutions form a curve.

(AT 373–374) If several equations remain, each must be used in order, alone or compared with the others, to express each unknown line, and so, by disentangling them, reduce them until only one unknown remains, equal to some known line, or whose square, cube, square of square, sursolid, square of cube and so on equals what is produced by adding or subtracting two or more other quantities, one known and the others composed of mean proportionals between unity and that square or cube, multiplied by other known quantities. He writes this z ∞ b, or z² ∞ −az + bb, or z³ ∞ +az² + bbz − c³, or z⁴ ∞ az³ − c³z + d⁴, and so on: z, the unknown, equals b; or its square equals b's square minus a times z; and so forth. All unknowns can always be reduced to one when the problem can be constructed by circles and straight lines, or by conic sections, or even by some other line only one or two degrees more composite. Elimination brings any determinate problem to a single polynomial equation in one unknown, written in a normal form with the highest power alone on one side. The "mean proportionals between unity and the square" are the lower powers of z, described geometrically to keep the link with the constructions. The classification of problems by the curves needed to construct them is announced here as a correlate of the equation's degree.

(AT 374) He does not dwell on this in more detail, since he would take away the pleasure of learning it oneself and the usefulness of cultivating one's mind by exercise, which is in his opinion the principal benefit to be drawn from this science; nor does he find anything here so difficult that those somewhat versed in ordinary geometry and algebra, attending to everything in the treatise, could not find it. The omission is justified by the Discourse's pedagogy of invention (AT 71–72) and its view of mathematics as an exercise that accustoms the mind to truth (AT 19). Readers found the gaps a real obstacle, and the Geometry's history of commentary shows the cost of the principle.

(AT 374) He will only point out that, provided one does not fail in disentangling the equations to use every division possible, one will infallibly reach the simplest terms to which the question can be reduced. "Every division possible" means removing common factors so that the final equation is of least degree, which Book III makes systematic by dividing by binomials such as z − a. The point matters for classification: a problem must be ranked by the degree of its irreducible equation, or a plane problem might be mistaken for a solid one.

(AT 374; margin: "Which problems are plane") If the problem can be solved by ordinary geometry, using only straight and circular lines traced on a plane surface, then when the last equation has been entirely disentangled there will remain at most an unknown square equal to the product of its root by some known quantity, plus or minus some other known quantity. This is the first statement that constructibility by ruler and compass corresponds to equations of the second degree. Descartes shows only one direction, that quadratics can be so constructed; the other — that a problem whose irreducible equation is of higher degree cannot — was proved only by Wantzel in 1837. The claim nevertheless supplies the frame within which the classical problems of cube duplication and angle trisection will be placed in Book III.

(AT 374–375; margin: "How they are solved") The root, or unknown line, is then easily found. If z² ∞ az + bb, make the right triangle NLM with side LM equal to b, the square root of the known quantity bb, and the other side LN equal to ½a, half the other known quantity multiplied by z; extending MN, the base, to O so that NO equals NL, the whole OM is z, the line sought, expressed as z ∞ ½a + √(¼aa + bb). The construction proves the formula geometrically: by the power of the point M with respect to the circle of radius NL, MO times (MO − a) equals LM squared. Only the positive root is constructed; the AT editors note that Descartes does not acknowledge negative roots here, which Book III will call "false roots."

(AT 375) If yy ∞ −ay + bb, with y the quantity to be found, make the same right triangle NLM and from its base MN take NP equal to NL, and the remainder PM is the root y, so that y ∞ −½a + √(¼aa + bb). Likewise if x⁴ ∞ −ax² + b², PM would be x², and x ∞ √(−½a + √(¼aa + b²)); and so for others. The same figure yields the second form by subtraction instead of addition. The biquadratic is reduced to a quadratic in x² followed by one more square root, so degree alone does not settle constructibility: the structure of the equation counts, which is why Descartes insisted on reducing to the simplest terms.

(AT 376) Finally, if z² ∞ az − bb, make NL equal to ½a and LM equal to b as before; then, instead of joining M and N, draw MQR parallel to LN, and with the circle from centre N through L cutting it at Q and R, the line sought z is MQ or MR, since in this case it is expressed in two ways: z ∞ ½a + √(¼aa − bb) and z ∞ ½a − √(¼aa − bb). Two positive roots appear as two intersections of a line with a circle. The correspondence between roots and intersection points is the geometrical intuition behind the whole treatise, and Book II's method of normals depends on its limiting case, where two intersections merge into one and the equation has a double root.

(AT 376) If the circle with centre N through L neither cuts nor touches the line MQR, there is no root of the equation, and one can be sure the construction of the proposed problem is impossible. Non-intersection signals the absence of real roots, so impossibility becomes something one can decide rather than merely fail to achieve. Book III will name such roots "imaginary," and the touching case, mentioned here in passing, corresponds to the double root.

(AT 376) These roots can be found by infinitely many other means; he has given these very simple ones to show that all problems of ordinary geometry can be constructed by doing no more than the little contained in the four figures explained. He does not think the ancients noticed this, or they would not have taken the trouble to write so many large books, in which the very order of their propositions shows that they lacked the true method of finding them all and merely collected those they came across. The polemic is the Regulae's (Rule IV): the ancients knew a method but either lacked it in general or concealed it, leaving collections of results. The claim of generality is the method's mark: all plane problems reduce to four constructions, so the multitude of particular theorems becomes unnecessary for solving problems. The judgment on Euclid and Apollonius is harsh and historically unfair, but it expresses exactly the contrast between a collection and an ordered method.

(AT 377; margin: "Example taken from Pappus") This can be seen very clearly from what Pappus put at the beginning of his seventh book, where, after listing what his predecessors had written in geometry, he speaks of a question that he says neither Euclid nor Apollonius nor anyone else had been able to solve completely, in these words — cited, the margin says, from the Latin version rather than the Greek so that everyone may understand it more easily: "The locus to three and four lines of which Apollonius speaks in his third book was not completed by Euclid, nor could Apollonius complete it, nor anyone else; nor could he add anything to what Euclid wrote, using only the conics demonstrated up to Euclid's time." The test case is chosen as the rainbow was in the Meteorology (AT 325), a famous problem the ancients left unsolved, which lets the method prove itself against the tradition. The citation is from Commandino's Latin translation (1588), whose occasional inaccuracies the AT editors note, and the preference for Latin over Greek fits the advertisement's assumption of learned readers while explaining the choice by accessibility.

(AT 377–378) A little later Pappus explains the question: "The locus to three and four lines, of which Apollonius boasts so much without acknowledging his predecessor, is this. If, three straight lines being given in position, straight lines are drawn from one and the same point to the three at given angles, and the ratio of the rectangle contained by two of them to the square of the third is given, the point lies on a solid locus given in position, that is, on one of the three conic sections. And if lines are drawn at given angles to four straight lines given in position, and the ratio of the rectangle contained by two to that contained by the other two is given, the point likewise lies on a conic given in position. If to two lines only, the locus has been shown to be plane. But if to more than four, the point lies on loci not yet known, called simply 'lines,' whose nature and properties are not established; they have composed one of them, not the first, though it seems the most manifest, showing it to be useful. The propositions are these: if from a point lines are drawn at given angles to five lines given in position, and the ratio of the rectangular parallelepiped contained by three of them to that contained by the other two and some given line is given, the point lies on a line given in position; if to six, and the ratio of the solid contained by three to that contained by the other three is given, again the point lies on a line given in position. If to more than six, they cannot yet say whether the ratio of something contained by four lines to what is contained by the others is given, since nothing is contained by more than three dimensions." The final clause is the evidence Descartes needs. The Greek treatment stops at six lines not because the problem changes but because a product of four lines has no spatial meaning, which is precisely the barrier removed by making a³ and its successors simple lines (AT 371). The admission that loci beyond four lines were unknown curves, named only "lines," is the gap Book II will fill with a classification of curves.

(AT 378–379) He asks the reader to note in passing that the ancients' scruple about using arithmetical terms in geometry, which could only come from their not seeing the relation clearly enough, caused much obscurity and difficulty in their explanations, for Pappus continues: "Those who have recently interpreted such things agree, though they signify nothing comprehensible by what is contained by these. But it is possible to state and demonstrate these things generally by means of compound ratios, in this way: if from a point lines are drawn at given angles to lines given in position, and the ratio compounded of that which one drawn line has to another, a second to a second, a third to a third, and the last to a given line, if there are seven — or, if eight, the last to the last — is given, the point lies on lines given in position. And similarly for any odd or even number, since these correspond, as I said, to the locus to four lines; they have therefore set none such that the line is known." Pappus's own escape — compound ratios instead of products — is read by Descartes as arithmetic disguised by scruple, a reading that treats Greek proportion theory as a concealed algebra, as the Regulae had suggested (Rule IV). Historians have contested the reading, Klein most fundamentally, but the passage shows that the generalisation to any number of lines was available to Pappus in principle and that what was missing was a way to handle the resulting loci.

(AT 379–380) The question begun by Euclid, pursued by Apollonius and finished by no one was therefore this. Given three, four or more straight lines in position, one seeks a point from which as many other lines can be drawn, one to each of the given lines, making given angles with them, such that the rectangle of two of them has a given ratio to the square of the third if there are three, or to the rectangle of the other two if there are four; or, if five, that the parallelepiped of three has a given ratio to the parallelepiped of the other two and another given line; or, if six, to the parallelepiped of the other three; or, if seven, that the product of four has the given ratio to the product of the other three and another given line; or, if eight, to the product of the other four; and so on for any number of lines. And since infinitely many points always satisfy the conditions, one must also know and trace the line on which they all lie. Pappus says that for three or four lines it is a conic section, but he does not determine or describe it, nor those for more lines, only adding that the ancients imagined one curve they showed useful, which seemed the most manifest but was not the first. This gave Descartes occasion to see whether his method could go as far as they had. Descartes's restatement already embodies his reform: "the product of four" is said without hesitation, and the extension "to any other number of lines" is uniform. The problem is defined as a locus problem, an indeterminate question with one condition and two unknowns, so its answer is a curve, which is how curves enter the Geometry as solution sets of equations.

(AT 380–381; margin: "Answer to Pappus's question") First, he found that when the question is proposed in only three, four or five lines, the points sought can always be found by simple geometry, with ruler and compass only, except when five given lines are all parallel. In that case, and when the question is proposed in six, seven, eight or nine lines, the points can always be found by the geometry of solids, that is, using one of the three conic sections, except when nine lines are all parallel. In that case, and for ten, eleven, twelve or thirteen lines, they can be found by a curve one degree more composite than the conics, except when thirteen are all parallel, in which case, and for fourteen to seventeen, a curve one degree more composite still is needed, and so on to infinity. The result classifies the problem by the number of lines, two lines raising the degree by one step. What is classified here is the construction of individual points: for a chosen value of one unknown the other is found by a quadratic, cubic or quartic equation, so the tools needed for a point follow the degree. The parallel cases are exceptions because then one unknown drops out and the other must be found from the full equation.

(AT 381) He also found that, with only three or four lines given, the points all lie not only on one of the three conic sections but sometimes on a circle or a straight line; with five, six, seven or eight, they all lie on one of the lines one degree more composite than the conics, and it is impossible to imagine one of these that is not useful for this question, though they may also lie on a conic, a circle or a straight line; with nine to twelve, on a line that can be only one degree more composite than the preceding, but all of that degree can serve; and so on to infinity. The second result classifies the loci themselves, as distinct from the construction of their points. The claim that every curve of the corresponding degree arises as a Pappus locus is too strong — later analysis, notably Henk Bos's in Redefining Geometrical Exactness, shows that not every cubic can be so obtained — but the aim is clear: the Pappus problem is to generate the whole hierarchy of algebraic curves, making it the bridge to Book II's classification.

(AT 381–382) The first and simplest of all after the conic sections is the one that can be described by the intersection of a parabola and a straight line in the manner explained later. So he thinks he has entirely satisfied what Pappus says the ancients sought, and he will try to put the demonstration in a few words, "for it already wearies me to write so much about it." The curve "most manifest but not first" in Pappus is answered by naming the first: the cubic later called the Cartesian parabola or trident, traced in Book II by a moving parabola and ruler. The weariness, half rhetorical, signals the treatise's preference for showing the way over working out every case.

(AT 382) Let AB, AD, EF, GH and so on be several lines given in position, and let a point C be required from which other lines CB, CD, CF, CH can be drawn to the given ones so that the angles CBA, CDA, CFE, CHG are given, and the product of some of these lines is equal to the product of the others, or has some other given ratio to it, which makes the question no harder. The general statement is made with equality of products, ratios being absorbed into a constant. The figure, with its lines drawn to given lines at given angles, is the data from which the coordinate frame will be built.

(AT 382–384; margin: "How the terms should be set out to arrive at the equation in this example") First he supposes the thing already done and, to escape the confusion of all these lines, considers one of the given lines and one of those to be found, say AB and CB, as the principal ones, to which he tries to relate all the others. Let the segment of AB between A and B be called x, and BC be called y; let all the other given lines be extended until they cut these two, also extended if need be and if not parallel, as here they cut AB at A, E, G and BC at R, S, T. Since all the angles of triangle ARB are given, the ratio of AB to BR is given; set it as z to b, so that with AB equal to x, RB is bx/z and the whole CR is y + bx/z, B falling between C and R — if R fell between C and B, CR would be y − bx/z, and if C between B and R, −y + bx/z. Likewise the three angles of triangle DRC are given, and so the ratio of CR to CD, set as z to c, gives CD ∞ cy/z + bcx/zz. Then, since AB, AD and EF are given in position, the distance between A and E is given; calling it k, EB is k + x (or k − x, or −k + x, according to where B falls); the triangle ESB being given, the ratio of BE to BS, set as z to d, gives BS ∞ (dk + dx)/z and CS ∞ (zy + dk + dx)/z (with other signs if S falls elsewhere); the triangle FSC gives CS to CF as z to e, so CF ∞ (ezy + dek + dex)/zz. Likewise AG, called l, is given, BG is l − x, the triangle BGT gives BG to BT as z to f, so BT ∞ (fl − fx)/z and CT ∞ (zy + fl − fx)/z; and the triangle TCH gives TC to CH as z to g, so CH ∞ (gzy + fgl − fgx)/zz. This is the birth of coordinate geometry, and it does not look like the textbook version. The two reference quantities are taken from the problem itself — x measured along a given line from a given point, y the length of one of the drawn lines — so the axes are oblique and asymmetric, adapted to the data rather than imposed on the plane. Negative coordinates are not used; each possible order of points gets its own sign pattern, which is why the text keeps listing alternatives. The constant z is a common denominator chosen to keep the expressions homogeneous.

(AT 384–385) So, however many lines are given in position, all those drawn from C at the given angles can always be expressed by three terms each: one composed of the unknown y multiplied or divided by a known quantity, another of the unknown x likewise multiplied or divided, and the third of a wholly known quantity — except when the lines are parallel to AB, in which case the term in x is zero, or to CB, in which case the term in y is zero, which is too obvious to explain. The signs + and − joined to these terms can be changed in every imaginable way. Each distance from C to a given line is a linear expression in x and y, which is the structural fact from which everything follows. The degree of the final equation will simply count how many such linear factors are multiplied together, so the complexity of the locus is read off from the number of lines.

(AT 385) Multiplying several of these lines together, the quantities x and y in the product can have only as many dimensions as there were lines multiplied, so never more than two in what is produced by multiplying two lines, nor more than three in what is produced by three, and so to infinity. Degree is bounded by the number of linear factors. This observation turns the number of lines in the Pappus problem into the degree of the locus and so into the basis for classifying curves.

(AT 385–386; margin: "How one finds that this problem is plane when it is not proposed in more than five lines") Since only one condition is required to determine C — that the product of some lines be equal, or (no harder) in given ratio, to the product of the others — one can take either unknown, x or y, at will and find the other by this equation, in which, if the question has no more than five lines, x, which does not enter the expression of the first line, can always have no more than two dimensions. So, taking a known quantity for y, there remains only xx ∞ +or− ax +or− bb, and x can be found with ruler and compass as explained. And taking successively infinitely many different values for y, one finds infinitely many for x, and so infinitely many points like C, by which the curve required is described. The indeterminacy that one condition leaves between two unknowns is turned into a method: fix one unknown, solve for the other, and the curve appears as the set of all points so obtained. Here the equation generates the curve point by point. The converse — taking a given curve and finding its equation, so as to study its properties — is Book II's task. That x is absent from the first line's expression (CB is y itself) is what keeps it of second degree for up to five lines.

(AT 386–387) When the question is proposed in six or more lines, if some of the given lines are parallel to BA or BC, one of the two quantities x or y may have only two dimensions in the equation, and C can then be found with ruler and compass. Conversely, if they are all parallel, even with only five lines, C cannot be found that way, since x does not appear in the equation at all, so a known quantity can no longer be taken for y; y itself must be sought, and having three dimensions it can be found only by extracting the root of a cubic equation, which cannot generally be done without at least one conic section. And with up to nine lines, provided they are not all parallel, the equation can always be made to rise no higher than the square of square, so it can always be solved by conic sections as will be explained; with up to thirteen, no higher than the square of cube, so it can be solved by a line only one degree more composite than the conics, as will also be explained. This is the first part of what he had to demonstrate; before passing to the second, he must say something in general about the nature of curved lines. The exceptions and the general bounds give the constructional side of the Pappus answer: the degrees of the equations to be solved for single points rise in steps of two, which corresponds to the grouping of curves in pairs of degrees that Book II calls genres. The transition states the problem the next book must solve: before curves of higher degree can be used to construct solutions, it must be settled which curves geometry may legitimately admit, the question of geometrical exactness.

### Second Book: On the Nature of Curved Lines

(AT 388–390; margin: "Which curved lines can be received in geometry") The ancients rightly noted that geometrical problems are plane, solid or linear — constructible with straight lines and circles, requiring at least a conic section, or requiring some more composite line. But Descartes is surprised that they did not distinguish further degrees among these more composite lines, and cannot understand why they called them mechanical rather than geometrical. If it is because a machine is needed to describe them, circles and straight lines would have to be rejected too, since they are drawn on paper only with compass and ruler, which may also be called machines. If it is because the instruments are more complex and so less accurate, that would exclude them from mechanics, where accuracy of handiwork matters, rather than from geometry, where only accuracy of reasoning is sought, which can be as perfect for these lines as for others. Nor can it be that the ancients wanted no more postulates than joining two points by a line and describing a circle, since they assumed besides, to treat the conics, that any cone can be cut by any plane; and to trace all the curves he wants to introduce nothing more is needed than that two or more lines can be moved one by another and that their intersections mark others. Taking "geometrical" for what is precise and exact and "mechanical" for what is not, and geometry for the science that teaches in general the measures of all bodies, one should no more exclude the most composite lines than the simplest, provided they can be imagined as described by one continuous motion, or by several successive ones of which the later are entirely regulated by the earlier, for then one can always know their measure exactly. Perhaps the ancients were put off because the first they considered happened to be the spiral, the quadratrix and the like, which truly belong only to mechanics, being imagined as described by two separate motions with no exactly measurable relation between them; or because, still knowing little of the conics, and even of what ruler and compass can do, they thought they should not tackle harder matter. Since he hopes those who master the geometrical calculation proposed here will no longer find enough to occupy them in plane and solid problems, he invites them to other researches where they will never lack exercise. This is the treatise's criterion of geometrical exactness, the subject of Henk Bos's Redefining Geometrical Exactness. The ancients' line between geometrical and mechanical curves is moved: instruments are not the issue, the ruler and compass being machines too, and the test is whether the generating motions are linked by an exactly determined relation. The spiral and quadratrix fail because they couple a rectilinear and a circular motion whose ratio cannot be exactly known, which anticipates the thesis on the unknowability of the ratio of straight to curved (AT 412). Geometry is redefined as the science of the measures of all bodies, the mathesis universalis of Regulae IV.

(AT 391–392) Consider the lines AB, AD, AF and the like, described by an instrument YZ composed of several rulers so joined that, YZ being fixed on the line AN, the angle XYZ can be opened and closed; when it is closed the points B, C, D, E, F, G, H all meet at A, but as it opens, the ruler BC, fixed at right angles to XY at B, pushes CD toward Z, which slides along YZ always at right angles to it; CD pushes DE, which slides along YX parallel to BC; DE pushes EF, EF pushes FG, FG pushes GH, and infinitely many others can be conceived pushing one another in the same way, some always making the same angles with YX, the others with YZ. As the angle opens, B describes AB, a circle, and the other points D, F, H, where the other rulers intersect, describe other curves AD, AF, AH, each more composite than the one before, and the first more composite than the circle. He sees nothing that prevents conceiving the description of the first as clearly and distinctly as that of the circle, or at least of the conics, nor the second, third and all the others as well as the first, nor therefore receiving them all alike for the speculations of geometry. The instrument is the mesolabe that appears in Descartes's notes of 1619, a machine for finding mean proportionals, which Book III uses to construct roots of equations. Each point's motion is wholly determined by the angle's opening, so the device satisfies the criterion of linked motions. The appeal to conceiving the description "clearly and distinctly" carries the criterion of evidence from the Discourse (AT 18, 33) into the admission of curves.

(AT 392–393; margin: "The way to distinguish all curved lines into certain genres, and to know the relation that all their points have to those of straight lines") He could give many other ways of tracing curves more composite by degrees to infinity, but to comprehend all those in nature and distinguish them in order into genres, he knows nothing better than to say that all the points of those that can be called geometrical, that is, that fall under some precise and exact measure, necessarily have some relation to all the points of a straight line that can be expressed by an equation, the same for all. When the equation rises only to the rectangle of two indeterminate quantities, or the square of one, the curve is of the first and simplest genre, which contains only the circle, parabola, hyperbola and ellipse; when it rises to the third or fourth dimension of the two indeterminate quantities, or of one — two are needed to express the relation of one point to another — it is of the second; when to the fifth or sixth, of the third; and so on to infinity. The kinematic criterion of AT 390 is replaced by an algebraic one: a curve is geometrical exactly when its points satisfy a polynomial equation in two indeterminates. Descartes treats the two criteria as coextensive without proof, which is the central difficulty Bos analyses. The two indeterminates are the coordinates, so the remark that "two are needed" states the dimension of the plane in algebraic terms. Grouping degrees in pairs into genres is a distinctive choice, justified at AT 395–396.

(AT 393–394) To know of what genre the line EC is, imagined as described by the intersection of the ruler GL and the rectilinear plane figure CNKL, whose side KN is extended indefinitely toward C, and which, moved on the plane beneath in a straight line so that its diameter KL is always applied to some part of BA extended either way, makes the ruler GL turn round the point G, since it is joined so as always to pass through L: he chooses a straight line such as AB to relate all the curve's points to, and a point A on it to begin the calculation. He says he chooses both because they may be taken freely: though choice can make the equation shorter and easier, however they are taken the line can always be shown to be of the same genre, as is easy to prove. Then, taking any point C on the curve where the instrument is applied, he draws CB parallel to GA and, CB and BA being two indeterminate unknown quantities, calls one y and the other x. To find their relation he considers also the known quantities determining the curve's description: GA, called a; KL, called b; and NL, parallel to GA, called c. As NL is to LK, or c to b, so CB, or y, is to BK, which is therefore (b/c)y; BL is (b/c)y − b, and AL is x + (b/c)y − b. Further, as CB is to LB, or y to (b/c)y − b, so GA, or a, is to LA, or x + (b/c)y − b. Multiplying the second by the third gives (ab/c)y − ab, equal to xy + (b/c)yy − by, the product of the first and last; so the required equation is yy ∞ cy − (c/b)xy + ay − ac, from which the line EC is seen to be of the first genre, as indeed it is a hyperbola. This is the first derivation in print of an equation from a curve-tracing mechanism, the converse of Book I's procedure. The remark on the free choice of reference line and origin asserts that degree is invariant under change of coordinates, which is true and is what makes the classification well defined, though Descartes leaves the proof to the reader. Similar triangles carry the whole calculation, so the algebra stays close to the figure.

(AT 394–395) If in the instrument a hyperbola, or another curve of the first genre, bounds the plane CNKL instead of the straight line CNK, its intersection with the ruler GL will describe another curve of the second genre. If CNK is a circle with centre L, one describes the first conchoid of the ancients; if a parabola with diameter KB, the curve he called the first and simplest for Pappus's question in five lines. If a curve of the second genre bounds the plane, one of the third is described, from the third one of the fourth, and so to infinity, as calculation easily shows. And however one imagines the description of a curve, provided it is geometrical, an equation determining all its points can always be found in this way. The machine raises the genre by one at each stage, so the hierarchy of curves is generated by iterating a single device. The final sentence states universality — every geometrical description yields an equation — as a claim, not a theorem. The conchoid of Nicomedes and the Cartesian parabola, or trident, are placed in the second genre, the curves needed for problems of the third and fourth degree.

(AT 395–396) He puts curves whose equation rises to the square of square in the same genre as those whose equation rises only to the cube, and those whose equation rises to the square of cube with those rising only to the sursolid, and so on, because there is a general rule for reducing to the cube all difficulties that go to the square of square, and to the sursolid all those that go to the square of cube, so they should not be thought more composite. The pairing of degrees rests on the reduction of the quartic to a cubic, which Book III gives, and on its supposed extension to higher degrees. Genre is therefore defined by the tools needed to solve, not by degree alone. The extension from the quartic case to sixth-degree equations does not hold in general, and later classification (Newton's of the cubics) proceeded by degree.

(AT 396) Among the lines of each genre, though most are equally composite, so that they can determine the same points and construct the same problems, some are simpler and have less extent in their power. In the first genre, besides the ellipse, hyperbola and parabola, which are equally composite, the circle is included, which is plainly simpler; in the second, there is the common conchoid, which arises from the circle, and some others which, though they have less extent than most of their genre, cannot be put in the first. The genres are not a complete measure of complexity, and Descartes admits finer gradations. "Power" here means the range of problems a curve can construct, so a curve is ranked by what it can do as an instrument of construction, the viewpoint of Book III.

(AT 396–397; margin: "Continuation of the explanation of Pappus's question set out in the preceding book") Having reduced all curves to certain genres, he can continue the demonstration of his answer to Pappus. With only three or four lines the equation determining the points rises only to the square, so the curve is necessarily of the first genre, since that equation expresses the relation of all points of first-genre curves to a straight line; with no more than eight lines the equation rises to the square of square at most, so the curve is of the second genre or below; with no more than twelve, to the square of cube, so of the third genre or below; and so on. And since the positions of the given lines can vary in every way, changing the known quantities and the signs + and − of the equation in every imaginable way, there is no first-genre curve that is not useful for the question in four lines, no second-genre curve that is not useful in eight, nor third in twelve, and so on, so that there is not one curve that falls under calculation and can be received in geometry that is not useful for some number of lines. The completeness claim of Book I (AT 381) is restated as a consequence of the free variation of positions. It holds for the conics, every one of which is a four-line locus, but in general the Pappus loci for 2n lines do not exhaust the curves of degree n, because the lines' positions supply fewer independent parameters than a general equation has coefficients. The intended conclusion — that the Pappus problem and the algebraic curves correspond exactly — would have unified the treatise's two halves; it is not true as stated.

(AT 397; margin: "Solution of this question when it is proposed in only three or four lines") He must now determine more particularly and give the way of finding the line sought in each case with only three or four lines; this will also show that the first genre contains no other curves than the three conic sections and the circle. The promised result is a classification of all second-degree equations. It will be obtained by solving the general four-line case, which produces every second-degree equation, and reading off the type of conic from its coefficients.

(AT 397–399) Taking up the four lines AB, AD, EF and GH given earlier, one must find a line on which there are infinitely many points like C such that, the lines CB, CD, CF and CH being drawn at given angles to the given ones, CB multiplied by CF equals CD multiplied by CH. With CB ∞ y, CD ∞ (czy + bcx)/zz, CF ∞ (ezy + dek + dex)/zz and CH ∞ (gzy + fgl − fgx)/zz, the equation is a quadratic in y whose coefficients involve x — at least supposing ez greater than cg, for if less all the signs + and − must be changed. If y were zero or less than nothing in this equation, with C supposed in the angle DAG, C must be supposed in the angle DAE, EAR or RAG, changing the signs as required; and if in all four positions y were zero, the question would be impossible in the case proposed. Supposing it possible, and abbreviating by writing 2m, 2n/z, o and p/m for the various combinations of given quantities — which, being all given, can be named as one likes — the length of BC with AB or x indeterminate is y ∞ m − (n/z)x + √(mm + ox − (p/m)xx). With three or four lines one can always have such terms, except that some may be zero and the signs may be changed. The four-line equation, solved for y, is brought to a canonical form: a linear part m − (n/z)x plus the square root of a quadratic in x. Negative values are handled by moving the point into another angle of the figure rather than admitting negative coordinates, and the case of no admissible position at all is identified as impossibility. Renaming combinations of constants as single letters is a point of method: what matters is the structure of the expression, not the provenance of its coefficients.

(AT 400) He then makes KI equal and parallel to BA, cutting from BC the part BK equal to m, since there is +m here — he would have added it by drawing IK on the other side if there were −m, and would not have drawn it at all if m were zero. He also draws IL so that IK is to KL as z to n, that is, with IK equal to x, KL is (n/z)x; and he knows likewise the ratio of KL to IL, set as n to a, so that IL is (a/z)x. He makes K fall between L and C because there is −(n/z)x, whereas he would have put L between K and C with +(n/z)x, and would not have drawn IL at all if (n/z)x were zero. The algebraic expression is turned back into a figure term by term: the constant m becomes the segment BK, the linear term becomes the line IL through I, and the sign of each term decides on which side the segment is laid off. The new line IL will serve as the diameter of the conic, so the construction is an affine change of reference adapted to the equation.

(AT 400–402) There remain for the line LC only the terms LC ∞ √(mm + ox − (p/m)xx). If they were zero, C would lie on the straight line IL; if they were such that the root could be extracted — mm and (p/m)xx having the same sign and oo equal to 4pm, or the terms mm and ox, or ox and (p/m)xx, being zero — C would lie on another straight line no harder to find than IL. Otherwise C is always on one of the three conic sections or a circle, one of whose diameters lies in the line IL, with LC one of the lines applied in order to this diameter, or conversely LC parallel to the diameter to which the line in IL is applied. If the term (p/m)xx is zero, the conic is a parabola; if it has the sign +, a hyperbola; if −, an ellipse — except when aam equals pzz and the angle ILC is right, in which case it is a circle instead of an ellipse. For a parabola, its latus rectum is oz/a, its diameter always in the line IL, and its vertex N found by making IN equal to amm/oz, with I between L and N if the terms are +mm + ox, L between I and N if +mm − ox, or N between I and L if −mm + ox, though −mm cannot occur as the terms have been set; N coincides with I if mm is zero. The parabola is then easily found by the first problem of Apollonius's first book. The type of conic is read off the sign of the squared term, an algebraic classification of second-degree curves equivalent to the later discriminant test. The degenerate cases — one line, two lines — are listed so that the enumeration is complete. The synthesis is handed over to Apollonius: Descartes computes the parameters (diameter, vertex, latus rectum) that Apollonius's construction problems require, so analytic geometry here completes classical geometry rather than replacing it.

(AT 402–403) If the line sought is a circle, ellipse or hyperbola, one must first find its centre M, always on IL, taking IM equal to aom/2pz, so that if o is zero the centre is exactly at I; for a circle or ellipse M is on the same side of I as L when there is +ox and on the other side when −ox, while for the hyperbola the reverse. The latus rectum is then √(oozz/aa + 4mpzz/aa) when there is +mm and the curve is a circle or ellipse, or −mm and it is a hyperbola, and √(oozz/aa − 4mpzz/aa) when, for a circle or ellipse, there is −mm, or for a hyperbola with oo greater than 4mp there is +mm; if mm is zero it is oz/a, and if ox is zero it is √(4mpzz/aa). For the transverse side, find a line that is to the latus rectum as aam is to pzz. In all these cases the diameter lies in IM and LC is applied in order to it, so that, making MN equal to half the transverse side on the same side of M as L, N is the vertex of this diameter, and the section is easily found by the second and third problems of Apollonius's first book. The central conics need a centre, a latus rectum and a transverse side, and each is given as an explicit expression in the abbreviated constants. The case distinctions by sign are exhaustive, which is the fourth precept's work in a calculation, and the result again feeds Apollonius's constructions (Conics I.55–56).

(AT 403–404) When the section is a hyperbola with +mm and oo zero or less than 4pm, one must draw from the centre M the line MOP parallel to LC and CP parallel to LM, make MO equal to √(mm − oom/4p), or equal to m if ox is zero, and take O as the vertex of this hyperbola, whose diameter is OP and to which CP is applied in order; its latus rectum is √(4a⁴m⁴/ppz⁴ − a⁴oom³/p³z⁴) and its transverse side √(4mm − oom/p), except when ox is zero, when the latus rectum is 2aamm/pzz and the transverse side 2m. So it is easily found by the third problem of Apollonius's first book. The remaining case is the hyperbola whose relevant diameter is the conjugate one, where IL does not meet the curve. The AT editors explain the second alternative of AT 401 by this case: the line in IL is then not a diameter in Apollonius's sense, so the construction must pass to the conjugate diameter OP.

(AT 404–405; margin: "Demonstration of all that has just been explained") The demonstrations of all this are evident. Composing an area from the quantities assigned to the latus rectum, the transverse side and the segment of the diameter NL or OP, according to the eleventh, twelfth and thirteenth theorems of Apollonius's first book, one finds all the same terms that make up the square of CP or CL, the line applied in order to this diameter. For example, subtracting IM from NM gives IN, adding IL gives NL, which multiplied by the latus rectum gives the rectangle, from which must be subtracted an area that is to the square of NL as the latus rectum is to the transverse side; the result is mm + ox − (p/m)xx for the square of CL, which is therefore a line applied in order to the segment NL of the diameter in an ellipse or circle. Verification is by synthesis: the Apollonian "symptom" of each conic — the relation between ordinate squared, latus rectum and abscissa (Conics I.11–13) — is shown to reproduce exactly the radicand of the equation. Analytic geometry thus appears as a translation of the Apollonian symptoms into equations, and the demonstration shows the two descriptions coincide.

(AT 405–406) If one wants to express all the given quantities in numbers — say EA ∞ 3, AG ∞ 5, AB ∞ BR, BS ∞ ½BE, GB ∞ BT, CD ∞ ½CR, CF ∞ 2CS, CH ∞ ⅔CT, the angle ABR 60 degrees, and the rectangle of CB and CF equal to that of CD and CH, all of which must be given for the question to be entirely determined — then, with AB ∞ x and CB ∞ y, one finds by the method above yy ∞ 2y − xy + 5x − xx and y ∞ 1 − ½x + √(1 + 4x − ¾xx). So BK must be 1 and KL half of KI; and since the angle IKL or ABR is 60 degrees and KIL, half of KIB or IKL, is 30, ILK is right. With IK or AB named x, KL is ½x, IL is x√¾, z is 1, a is √¾, m is 1, o is 4, and p is ¾, so that IM is √(16/3) and NM √(19/3); and since aam, which is ¾, here equals pzz and the angle ILC is right, the curve NC is a circle. All other cases can be examined in the same way. A numerical example shows the abstract scheme producing a definite answer, a circle, and the parenthesis on what must be given for the question to be "entirely determined" restates Book I's count of data. The example works as a check the reader can carry out by hand, in keeping with the treatise's habit of leaving the general proofs to the reader.

(AT 406–407; margin: "Which loci are plane and solid, and the way to find them") Since equations rising only to the square are all included in what he has just explained, not only is the ancients' problem in three and four lines entirely completed here, but also everything belonging to what they called the composition of solid loci, and consequently of plane loci, which are included in the solid. These loci are nothing but this: when a point is to be found that lacks one condition to be entirely determined, as in this example, all the points of one line can be taken for it. If the line is straight or circular it is called a plane locus; if a parabola, hyperbola or ellipse, a solid locus; and whenever this happens one arrives at an equation with two unknowns like one of those just solved. If the line so determining the point is one degree more composite than the conics, it can likewise be called a sursolid locus, and so on; and if the point lacks two conditions, its locus is a surface, which may be plane, spherical or more composite. The ancients' highest aim in this matter was to reach the composition of solid loci, and all that Apollonius wrote on the conics seems to have been written only to seek it. The classical doctrine of loci is redefined by counting missing conditions: one missing condition gives a curve, two give a surface, which is the notion of degrees of freedom in a locus. The extension to surfaces anticipates the final section on curves in space (AT 440). Reading Apollonius's whole work as aimed at the locus problem is a characteristic Cartesian reinterpretation, in which earlier geometry is understood as an incomplete attempt at what the method now does in general.

(AT 407) One also sees that what he took for the first genre of curves can include no others than the circle, parabola, hyperbola and ellipse, which is all he had undertaken to prove. The claim of AT 392 is now established: since the four-line case produces every second-degree equation, and each has been shown to give one of these curves (or a degenerate line), the first genre consists of the conics. This is the first general classification of plane curves of the second degree by algebraic means.

(AT 407; margin: "Which is the first and simplest of all the curves that serve in the ancients' question when it is proposed in five lines") If the ancients' question is proposed in five lines all parallel, the point sought will always be on a straight line. The degenerate case is dismissed first: with all lines parallel, only one coordinate enters, and the locus collapses to a line. This clears the way for the case that yields the first curve beyond the conics.

(AT 408) But if it is proposed in five lines of which four are parallel and the fifth cuts them at right angles, all the lines drawn from the point sought also meeting them at right angles, and the parallelepiped of three of the lines so drawn to three of the parallels is equal to the parallelepiped of the two lines drawn, one to the fourth parallel and the other to the perpendicular, and of a third given line — which seems to him the simplest case imaginable after the preceding — the point sought will be on the curve described by the motion of a parabola in the manner explained above. The simplest five-line case is chosen so as to produce the curve of AT 395, the trident. "Simplest" is a judgment Descartes makes and later qualifies (AT 411), so the ranking of curves within the second genre is partly a matter of convenience.

(AT 408–410) Let the given lines be AB, IH, ED, GF and GA, and let the point C be required such that, drawing CB, CF, CD, CH and CM at right angles to the given lines, the parallelepiped of the three CF, CD and CH equals that of the other two, CB and CM, and a third line AI. Set CB ∞ y, CM ∞ x, AI or AE or GE ∞ a, so that, C being between AB and DE, CF ∞ 2a − y, CD ∞ a − y and CH ∞ y + a; multiplying these three gives y³ − 2ayy − aay + 2a³, equal to the product of the other three, axy. Then consider the curve CEG, imagined as described by the intersection of the parabola CKN, moved so that its diameter KL is always on the line AB, and the ruler GL, turning meanwhile about G so as always to pass through L in the parabola's plane. Make KL ∞ a, the principal latus rectum (relating to the parabola's axis) also ∞ a, GA ∞ 2a, CB or MA ∞ y, and CM or AB ∞ x. By the similar triangles GMC and CBL, GM, which is 2a − y, is to MC, which is x, as CB, y, is to BL, which is therefore xy/(2a − y); since LK is a, BK is a − xy/(2a − y), or (2aa − ay − xy)/(2a − y); and since BK, a segment of the parabola's diameter, is to BC, applied in order to it, as BC is to the latus rectum a, the calculation shows that y³ − 2ayy − aay + 2a³ equals axy, and so C is the point required. It can be taken anywhere on the line CEG, or on its adjoint cEGc, described in the same way with the parabola's vertex turned the other way, or on their counterparts NIo and nIO, described by the intersection of GL with the other side of the parabola KN. The cubic locus is identified with the curve traced by the parabola-and-ruler machine, which joins the kinematic and algebraic definitions in one example. The four branches are listed so that the whole locus is accounted for, since the equation includes all of them. The same parabola symptom (BK times latus rectum equals BC squared) that served for conics now generates a curve of the second genre, showing how the machine of AT 393–395 raises the degree.

(AT 410–411) Even if the given parallels AB, IH, ED and GF were not equally spaced and GA did not cut them at right angles, nor the lines from C meet them so, C would still lie on a curve of the same nature; and sometimes even when none of the given lines is parallel. But if, with four parallels and a fifth crossing them, the parallelepiped of three of the lines drawn from the point — one to the fifth line and two to two of the parallels — equals that of the two drawn to the other two parallels and another given line, the point is on a curve of another nature: one such that, all the lines applied in order to its diameter being equal to those of a conic section, the segments of this diameter between the vertex and these lines have the same ratio to a given line that this given line has to the segments of the conic's diameter to which the like lines are applied. He cannot truly say this line is less simple than the previous one, which he chose as first because its description and calculation are somewhat easier. The second curve is defined by inverting abscissas with respect to a conic, a transformation-based definition rather than a mechanical one. The admission that the "first" curve was chosen for convenience shows that the hierarchy within a genre is not fixed by a single criterion.
