(AT 411) He will not stop to distinguish by species the lines that serve the other cases, for he has not undertaken to say everything; having explained how to find infinitely many points through which they pass, he thinks he has given enough means to describe them. The refusal to classify further is principled: the method supplies the means to describe any such curve point by point, so a catalogue would add nothing. The next paragraph explains why pointwise description is legitimate here and not for the spiral.

(AT 411–412; margin: "Which curves described by finding several of their points can be received in geometry") There is a great difference between this way of finding several points to trace a curve and the one used for the spiral and the like. By the latter one does not find indifferently all the points of the curve sought, but only those determined by some measure simpler than the one required to compose it, so that, properly speaking, one does not find a single one of its points — none of those so proper to it that they can be found only by it. Whereas there is no point on the curves serving the proposed question that cannot be among those determined in the way explained. And since this way of finding a curve by finding indifferently several of its points extends only to curves that can also be described by a regular and continuous motion, it should not be entirely rejected from geometry. The distinction is subtle and important. For algebraic curves any point can be constructed, while for the spiral only special points can be, those corresponding to rational divisions of the circle, which are fixed by a simpler measure than the curve's own. Descartes thus separates what would later be called algebraic from transcendental curves by the constructibility of an arbitrary point, and links pointwise construction back to the kinematic criterion.

(AT 412; margin: "Which curves described with a cord can also be received") Nor should one reject the way of using a thread or a folded cord to determine the equality of the sum or difference of two or more straight lines drawn from each point of the curve sought to certain other points, or to certain other lines at certain angles, as was done in the Optics to explain the ellipse and hyperbola. Although no lines like cords — sometimes straight, sometimes curved — can be received, because the ratio between straight and curved lines is not known, and even, he believes, cannot be known by men, so nothing exact and certain could be concluded from it, yet since cords are used in these constructions only to determine straight lines whose length is perfectly known, they should not be rejected. The gardener's constructions of the Optics (AT 166, 176) are admitted because the cord only carries straight lengths. The general thesis that the ratio of straight to curved is unknowable excludes rectification from geometry. It was refuted within two decades, when Neil, van Heuraet and Wren rectified algebraic curves (1657–59), and van Heuraet's result appeared in Schooten's own Latin edition of the Geometry.

(AT 412–413; margin: "That to find all the properties of curved lines it suffices to know the relation their points have to those of straight lines, and the way to draw lines that cut them at right angles at all their points") From knowing only the relation all points of a curve have to all those of a straight line, as explained, one can easily find their relation to all other given points and lines, and then the diameters, axes, centres and other lines or points to which each curve has some more particular or simpler relation, and so imagine various ways of describing them and choose the easiest. Almost everything that can be determined about the size of the areas they enclose can also be found from this alone, without his needing to say more. All other properties attributable to curves depend only on the size of the angles they make with other lines; but when one can draw straight lines cutting them at right angles at the points where they meet the lines whose angles are to be measured — or, what he takes here as the same, cutting their tangents — those angles are no harder to find than between straight lines. So he will think he has given all that is required for the elements of curves once he has given a general way of drawing straight lines perpendicular to them at any chosen point; and he dares say this is the most useful and general problem not only that he knows, but that he has ever wished to know in geometry. The equation is presented as containing all the curve's information, which is the founding idea of analytic geometry. Angles between curves are reduced to angles between their normals, so the normal problem becomes the key to the "elements" of curved lines. The claim about areas is overconfident, since quadrature requires methods of integration that Descartes did not have and that Fermat, Roberval and later Leibniz and Newton developed. The pride in the normal problem was tested at once, in the dispute with Fermat over tangents in 1637–38.

(AT 413–414; margin: "General way of finding straight lines that cut given curves, or their tangents, at right angles") Let CE be the curve, and let a straight line through C be required that makes right angles with it. Supposing it done, let the line sought be CP, extended to P on the straight line GA to whose points all those of CE are referred; with MA or CB ∞ y and CM or BA ∞ x, there is an equation expressing the relation between x and y. Then making PC ∞ s and PA ∞ v, or PM ∞ v − y, the right triangle PMC gives ss, the square of the base, equal to xx + vv − 2vy + yy, the squares of the two sides: so x ∞ √(ss − vv + 2vy − yy), or y ∞ v + √(ss − xx). By this equation one removes one of the two indeterminates, x or y, from the curve's equation, putting √(ss − vv + 2vy − yy) for x and its square for xx, its cube for x³, and so on, or ss − xx and its powers for y; so that there always remains an equation with only one indeterminate, x or y. The method rests on the circle with centre P on the reference line through C. Substituting the circle's equation into the curve's gives an equation whose roots are the intersections of circle and curve, and the normal is found by requiring that these intersections merge at C (AT 417–418). Finding a tangent becomes a problem about the roots of an equation.

(AT 414–415) If CE is an ellipse, MA the segment of its diameter to which CM is applied in order, r its latus rectum and q its transverse side, by the thirteenth theorem of Apollonius's first book xx ∞ ry − (r/q)yy, so removing xx there remains ss − vv + 2vy − yy ∞ ry − (r/q)yy, or yy + (qry − 2qvy + qvv − qss)/(q − r) equal to nothing — "for it is better here to consider the whole sum together than to make one part equal to the other." The Apollonian symptom serves as the ellipse's equation, which shows how naturally the classical properties pass into equations. The remark about setting the whole equal to zero introduces a convention of great consequence: written as a polynomial equal to nothing, an equation can be compared term by term with another of the same form, which is the technique the method of normals needs.

(AT 415; margin: "Example of this operation in the parabola of the second genre") Likewise, if CE is the curve described by the motion of a parabola as explained above, with b for GA, c for KL and d for the latus rectum of the parabola's diameter KL, the equation relating x and y is y³ − byy − cdy + bcd + dxy ∞ 0; removing x gives y³ − byy − cdy + bcd + dy√(ss − vv + 2vy − yy) ∞ 0, and, rearranging by multiplication, an equation of the sixth degree in y. And so for others. The method applies unchanged to a curve of the second genre, only with a higher-degree result. That the same procedure works for the trident as for the ellipse is the point: generality is shown by applying the identical steps to a harder case.

(AT 416–417; margin: "Example of this operation in an oval") Even if the points of the curve were not related to a straight line in the way described but in any other imaginable way, such an equation could always be found. If CE is a line related to three points F, G and A so that the lines drawn from each of its points, such as C, to F exceed FA by a quantity having a given ratio to the quantity by which GA exceeds the lines drawn from the same points to G: let GA ∞ b, AF ∞ c, and, taking any point C on the curve, let the excess of CF over FA be to that of GA over GC as d to e, so that, calling this indeterminate quantity z, FC is c + z and GC is b − (e/d)z. With MA ∞ y, GM is b − y and FM c + y, and the right triangle CMG gives the square of CM by subtracting GM² from GC²; subtracting FM² from FC² gives it again in other terms; equating them gives y, or MA, as a fraction in z, and substituting it in the square of CM expresses CM² in z alone. A curve defined by distances to fixed points — a bipolar definition — is converted into the coordinate scheme, showing that the method does not depend on how a curve is first given. This curve is one of the ovals introduced at AT 424, so the example prepares their optical use, for which the normal is essential.

(AT 417) Then, supposing the straight line PC meets the curve at right angles at C, and making PC ∞ s and PA ∞ v as before, PM is v − y, and the right triangle PCM gives ss − vv + 2vy − yy for the square of CM; substituting for y the expression equal to it gives the equation sought, an equation in z. The same circle device is applied in the new variable z. The procedure is general because it requires only that every coordinate quantity be expressible through one parameter.

(AT 417–418) Having found such an equation, instead of using it to know the quantities x, y or z, which are already given since C is given, one must use it to find v or s, which determine the point P sought. For this, consider that if P is as required, the circle with centre P passing through C will touch the curve CE there without cutting it; but if P is the least bit nearer to or farther from A than it should be, the circle will cut the curve not only at C but necessarily also at another point. Also, when the circle cuts the curve, the equation by which x or y or some other quantity is sought, supposing PA and PC known, necessarily contains two unequal roots: if the circle cuts the curve at C and E, drawing EQ parallel to CM, the names x and y suit EQ and QA as well as CM and MA, and PE equals PC, so the same equation arises, and the value of x or y is double — two unequal roots, one CM and the other EQ, or one MA and the other QA. True, if E is not on the same side of the curve as C, only one of the roots is true and the other is reversed or less than nothing; but the closer C and E are, the less the roots differ, and they become entirely equal if both points merge into one, that is, if the circle through C touches the curve without cutting it. This is the central idea of the method: tangency is the coincidence of two intersections, which corresponds algebraically to a double root. Negative roots are recognised here as "reversed or less than nothing," the false roots of Book III. The idea parallels Fermat's method of adequality and the later calculus, but Descartes's version stays within algebra, requiring no limits or infinitesimals, at the cost of heavy computation and of applying only to algebraic curves.

(AT 418–419) When an equation has two equal roots, it necessarily has the same form as the product of the unknown minus the known quantity equal to it, multiplied by itself, and — if this does not have as many dimensions as the equation — multiplied further by another sum having as many as are lacking, so that there can be separate equality between each term of the one and each term of the other. The double-root condition is turned into identities between coefficients: the equation is set equal to (y − e)² times an auxiliary polynomial with unknown coefficients. This is the method of undetermined coefficients, which Descartes calls at AT 423 one of the most important parts of his method.

(AT 419) For example, the first equation found above, yy + (qry − 2qvy + qvv − qss)/(q − r) ∞ 0, must have the same form as that produced by making e equal to y and multiplying y − e by itself, which gives yy − 2ey + ee; so each term can be compared separately: the first, yy, is the same in both, and the second, (qr − 2qv)/(q − r) times y in one, equals −2ey in the other. Hence, seeking v, the line PA, one has v ∞ e − (r/q)e + ½r, or, since e was supposed equal to y, v ∞ y − (r/q)y + ½r. One could find s by the third term, but since v suffices to determine P, which is all we seek, there is no need to go further. For the ellipse the result is the classical subnormal, PM ∞ ½r − (r/q)y, recovered by the new method. Only the quantity needed is computed, the economy the Discourse's order requires, since s would add nothing to the construction of P.

(AT 420) Likewise the second equation, of the sixth degree, must have the same form as the product of yy − 2ey + ee by y⁴ + fy³ + ggyy + h³y + k⁴; so from the two equations six others are drawn, which serve to know the six quantities f, g, h, k, v and s. It is easy to see from this that, whatever genre the proposed curve is, this procedure always gives as many equations as unknowns one must suppose. To disentangle them in order and finally find v, the only one needed and for whose sake the others are sought, one must first find by the second term f, the first unknown of the auxiliary sum: f ∞ 2e − b. Then by the last term one must find k, the last unknown of the same sum: k⁴ ∞ bbccdd/ee. The count of equations against unknowns from Book I (AT 372) assures that the problem is determined for any genre. The order of elimination — first coefficient from the second term, last from the last — is chosen so that each equation gives one new unknown, the third precept applied to a system of equations.

(AT 421) Then by the third term one must find g, the second quantity: gg ∞ 3ee − 4be − 2cd + bb + dd. Each step introduces only one unknown, so the system solves itself from the ends inward. The procedure is mechanical once the order is fixed.

(AT 421) Then by the penultimate term one must find h, the penultimate quantity: h³ ∞ 2bbccdd/e³ − 2bccdd/ee. The alternation between the second and the penultimate terms works inward from both ends of the polynomial, keeping each equation solvable for one new unknown.

(AT 421) One would have to continue in the same order to the last, if there were more in this sum, since this can always be done in the same way. The general claim is that the system obtained by comparing coefficients is always solvable in this order, a triangular structure that makes the method an algorithm for any degree.

(AT 421) Then by the next term in this order, here the fourth, one must find v, obtaining an expression in e, b, c and d; putting y for e, which equals it, one has the line AP as a rational expression in y. The normal to the trident is thus constructed at any point. The result is lengthy but explicit, which demonstrates that the method yields an answer even for a curve beyond the conics.

(AT 421–422) The third equation, for the oval, has the same form as zz − 2fz + ff, supposing f equal to z, so there is again an equation between −2f, or −2z, and the coefficient of z; from which v is known as (bcdd − bcde + bddz + ceez − cddz)/(bdd + cee + eez − ddz). For the oval a quadratic in z suffices, so the double-root condition gives v at once. The normal to the oval is what the optical theory of AT 424–440 needs, since refraction at a surface is governed by the angle with the normal.

(AT 422) So, composing the line AP of this sum equal to v, all of whose quantities are known, and drawing from the point P so found a straight line to C, it cuts the curve CE at right angles, which was to be done. He sees nothing to prevent extending the problem in the same way to all curves that fall under any geometrical calculation. The construction closes the analysis: P is found, and PC is the normal. The extension to "all curves under geometrical calculation" is correct for algebraic curves, and only for them, which is consistent with the exclusion of the spiral and quadratrix. It was this limit that Roberval and Fermat exploited in 1638 by proposing curves such as the cycloid.

(AT 422–423) As for the last sum, taken at will to make up the dimensions of the other when they fall short, as y⁴ + fy³ + ggyy + h³y + k⁴ was taken above, the signs + and − can be supposed as one likes without the line v, or AP, coming out different, "as you can easily see by experience," for if he had to stop to demonstrate all the theorems he mentions he would have to write a much larger volume than he wishes. But he wants to point out in passing that the invention of supposing two equations of the same form, so as to compare all the terms of one separately with those of the other and so make several arise from one, can serve for infinitely many other problems and is not the least part of the method he uses. The appeal to "experience" in mathematics — trying cases — is notable: the claim is checked rather than proved, which shows Descartes's practice to be less purely deductive than his rhetoric. Naming the comparison of coefficients as a central part of the method identifies a technique that Book III uses again for the reduction of equations.

(AT 423) He does not add the constructions by which the tangents or perpendiculars sought can be described after this calculation, since they are always easy to find, though skill is often needed to make them short and simple. The analysis yields an expression, and turning it into an elegant construction is left as a matter of skill. The method guarantees a solution but not a good one, so geometrical art keeps a place beside the calculation.

(AT 423–424; margin: "Example of the construction of this problem in the conchoid") For example, if DC is the first conchoid of the ancients, with pole A and rule BH, so that all lines drawn toward A and lying between the curve and the rule, such as DB and CE, are equal, and the line CG cutting it at right angles at C is required, one might, seeking the point of BH through which CG must pass by the method explained here, engage in a calculation as long as or longer than any of the preceding; and yet the construction that should be deduced from it is very simple. One need only take CF on the line CA equal to CH, the perpendicular to HB, then from F draw FG parallel to BA and equal to EA, which gives the point G through which CG, the line sought, must pass. Descartes gives the elegant synthetic construction without the long calculation behind it, both as a demonstration of skill and as a challenge to readers. The conchoid of Nicomedes, used since antiquity for trisection and cube duplication, here receives its normal by a ruler-and-compass construction. The contrast between the length of the analysis and the brevity of the synthesis concedes the cost of the algebraic method while showing that its results can be put in classical form.

(AT 424; margin: "Explanation of four new kinds of ovals that serve in optics") So that the reader may know that the consideration of curves proposed here is not without use, and that they have various properties in no way inferior to those of the conic sections, he will add the explanation of certain ovals that will be found very useful for the theory of catoptrics and dioptrics. The ovals are offered as a demonstration of the new curves' worth on the ground where the conics had been praised, in optics. They also fulfil the promise of Optics VIII (AT 185) to treat "more conveniently in the Geometry" the lenses that turn rays from a point exactly to another point. This is the point where the Geometry and the Optics meet most directly.

(AT 424–425) First, having drawn two straight lines FA and AR intersecting at A, at any angle, he takes on one the point F at will, nearer to or farther from A according as he wants the ovals larger or smaller, and from F as centre describes a circle passing a little beyond A, through the point 5. From 5 he draws the line 56 cutting the other at 6, so that A6 is less than A5 in any given ratio — the ratio that measures refractions, if it is to serve for dioptrics. Then he takes the point G on the line FA, on the side of 5, at will, making AF and GA have any given ratio. Then he makes RA equal to GA on the line A6 and, describing from centre G a circle with radius equal to R6, it cuts the first circle on either side at the point 1, one of those through which the first oval must pass. Again, from F he describes a circle passing a little short of or beyond 5, through 7, draws 78 parallel to 56, and from G describes another circle with radius equal to R8, cutting the circle through 7 at another point 1 of the same oval; and so on, as many as desired, by drawing more parallels to 78 and more circles from F and G. The construction is pointwise with ruler and compass, which by AT 411 is legitimate since any point can be found. The construction ensures that the changes in distance to the two foci F and G stand in the fixed refraction ratio, so the oval is the curve whose bipolar equation is linear: distance to F plus a constant multiple of distance to G is constant. This is what later geometry calls the Cartesian oval, of the fourth degree, and so of the second genre.

(AT 425–426) For the second oval there is no difference except that, instead of AR, one takes on the other side of A the line AS equal to AG, and the radius of the circle described from G, to cut the one from F through 5, is equal to S6, or to S8 for the one through 7, and so on, so that these circles intersect at the points marked 2, which belong to the second oval, A2X. The AT editors note that this second oval is geometrically identical with the third, as the first is with the fourth, which reduces the four kinds to two curves read in different ways. Changing the side on which the segment is taken changes the sign in the bipolar relation, from a sum to a difference.

(AT 426–427) For the third and fourth, instead of AG one takes AH on the other side of A, the same side as F, and AH must be greater than AF, which may even be zero, so that F falls at A, in describing all these ovals. Then, with AR and AS equal to AH, to describe the third oval A3Y he makes a circle from centre H with radius equal to S6 cutting at 3 the circle from F through 5, and another with radius S8 cutting the one through 7 at another point 3, and so on; and for the last oval, circles from H with radii equal to R6, R8 and the like, cutting the other circles at the points marked 4. The family is completed by placing the second focus on the same side as the first. With F allowed to coincide with A, the construction covers limiting cases that will matter for the lens problems at AT 434–439.

(AT 427–428) Infinitely many other ways of describing the same ovals could be found. For example, the first, AV, when FA and AG are supposed equal, can be traced by dividing the whole FG at L so that FL is to LG as A5 is to A6, that is, in the ratio measuring refractions; then, bisecting AL at K, one turns a ruler such as FE about F, pressing with a finger at C the cord EC, which, attached to the ruler's end at E, folds from C to K, then back from K to C, and from C to G, where its other end is attached, so that the cord's length is made of GA plus AL plus FE minus AF. The motion of C will describe this oval, in imitation of what was said in the Optics of the ellipse and hyperbola, but he will not dwell on it longer. The cord construction is the one AT 412 admitted, since the cord only carries straight lengths, and it ties the ovals to the gardener's constructions of Optics VIII (AT 166, 176). The doubled cord between C and K produces the weighted sum of distances the refraction ratio requires, so a mechanical device embodies the optical law.

(AT 428–429) Although all these ovals seem almost of the same nature, they are of different genres, each containing infinitely many subordinate genres, each of which again contains as many species as the genre of ellipses or of hyperbolas. According as the ratio between A5 and A6 differs, the subordinate genre differs; according as the ratio between AF and AG or AH changes, the ovals of each subordinate genre change species; and according as AG or AH is greater or smaller, they differ in size. If A5 and A6 are equal, instead of ovals of the first or third genre one describes only straight lines, while instead of those of the second one gets all possible hyperbolas, and instead of those of the last all ellipses. "Genre" here is used loosely, not in the technical sense of AT 392, since all these ovals are of the fourth degree. The taxonomy by three parameters — refraction ratio, focal ratio, scale — mirrors the classification of conics by eccentricity and size. That the ovals reduce to ellipses and hyperbolas when the ratio is one connects them to the foci-based definitions of the Optics.

(AT 429; margin: "The properties of these ovals regarding reflections and refractions") In each of these ovals two parts must be considered, which have different properties. In the first, the part toward A makes rays that, being in air, come from F all turn toward G when they meet the convex surface of a glass whose surface is A1, and in which the refractions are such that, as the Optics said, they can all be measured by the ratio between A5 and A6, or the like, by means of which the oval was described. The first oval solves the problem the ellipse could not: it brings rays from a point at finite distance exactly to another point by one refraction, where the ellipse of Optics VIII handled only parallel rays. The proof will use the normal method of AT 413–422 and the sine law of Optics II.

(AT 430) The part toward V makes rays coming from G all reflect toward F if they meet the concave surface of a mirror whose figure is 1V1 and whose matter diminishes the force of these rays in the ratio between A5 and A6. For, from what was demonstrated in the Optics, it is evident that the angles of reflection would then be unequal, as are those of refraction, and could be measured in the same way. The hypothesis of a mirror that alters the ray's force so that reflection obeys a sine ratio instead of equal angles is purely theoretical. It shows the curve's second branch solving a counterfactual reflection problem, which completes the symmetry of the theory more than it serves practice.

(AT 430) In the second oval, the part 2A2 also serves for reflections whose angles are supposed unequal: on the surface of a mirror of the same matter as before, it would reflect all rays from G so that after reflection they seemed to come from F. And with AG made much larger than AF, this mirror would be convex in the middle, toward A, and concave at the ends, since such is the figure of this line, which in this respect represents a heart rather than an oval. The heart shape is a genuine feature of the quartic oval for some parameters, an inflection that conics cannot have. Descartes notes the shape because it contradicts the name, a sign that he examined the curves themselves rather than only their equations.

(AT 430) Its other part, 2X2, serves for refractions, and makes rays that, being in air, tend toward F turn toward G on crossing the surface of a glass having its figure. This branch handles converging rays, the case where a lens receives a pencil already aimed at a point. Rays converging on F, diverging from F and parallel are the three states of Optics VIII (AT 175), so the branches of the ovals map onto that enumeration.

(AT 430–431) The third oval serves entirely for refractions, and makes rays that, being in air, tend toward F go toward H in the glass after crossing its surface, whose figure is A3Y3, convex everywhere except toward A, where it is slightly concave, so that it too has the figure of a heart. The difference between its two parts lies in F being nearer one of them than H is and farther from the other than H is. Here both branches are refracting, and the relative positions of F and H distinguish them. The repeated heart shape shows that these quartics can have points of inflection.

(AT 431) Likewise the last oval serves entirely for reflections, and makes rays coming from H, if they meet the concave surface of a mirror of the same matter as the previous ones with figure A4Z4, all reflect toward F. Each oval has now been assigned its optical function, two for refraction and two for reflection with unequal angles. The enumeration is complete in the sense the fourth precept demands, since every branch has a use.

(AT 431) So the points F and G or H can be called the burning points of these ovals, after the example of those of the ellipses and hyperbolas, so named in the Optics. The name of Optics VIII (AT 167) passes to the ovals, so that the term "focus" is defined by optical behaviour rather than by a construction. This unifies the conics and ovals as one family of optical curves.

(AT 431–433; margin: "Demonstration of the properties of these ovals regarding reflections and refractions") He omits many other refractions and reflections governed by these ovals, which, being only the converses or contraries of these, can easily be deduced. But he must not omit the demonstration of what he has said. Take, for example, any point C on the first part of the first oval, and draw CP cutting the curve at right angles at C, which is easy by the preceding problem. Taking b for AG, c for AF, c + z for FC, and supposing the ratio between d and e — which he will always take here for the one measuring the refractions of the glass — to designate also the ratio between A5 and A6 used to describe the oval, which gives b − (e/d)z for GC, one finds that AP is (bcdd − bcde + bddz + ceez)/(bde + cdd + ddz − eez), as shown above. Further, drawing from P the line PQ perpendicular to FC and PN perpendicular to GC, if PQ is to PN as d is to e — the lines measuring the refractions of the convex glass AC — the ray from F to C must bend on entering the glass so as to go to G, as is evident from the Optics. Finally, let us see by calculation whether PQ is to PN as d is to e. The right triangles PQF and CMF are similar, so CF is to CM as FP is to PQ, and FP multiplied by CM and divided by CF equals PQ; likewise the right triangles PNG and CMG are similar, so GP multiplied by CM and divided by CG equals PN. Since multiplying or dividing two quantities by the same one does not change their ratio, if FP times CM divided by CF is to GP times CM divided by CG as d is to e, then, dividing both by CM and multiplying both by CF and again by CG, FP times CG must be to GP times CF as d to e. By the construction, FP is c + AP and CG is b − (e/d)z. The demonstration joins the two halves of the Essays: the normal comes from Geometry II's method (AT 421–422) and the refraction condition from Optics II's sine law (AT 100–101), with PQ and PN as the perpendiculars that measure the sines of incidence and refraction relative to the normal. The ovals are thus shown to be the curves the sine law requires. The sentence about omitting the converse cases relies on the reciprocity of refraction (AT 104).

(AT 433) So, multiplying FP by CG, there comes an expression over the common denominator bde + cdd + ddz − eez. The calculation proceeds in the steps announced. The displayed products are the evidence, and the AT editors print them as given.

(AT 433) Then GP is b minus AP, which reduces to a fraction over the same denominator, and CF is c + z. The second product is prepared in the same way. Both quantities are expressed in the oval's parameters and the free variable z.

(AT 433) So, multiplying GP by CF, another expression results over the same denominator. With both products written out, the comparison of their ratio with d to e can be made term by term.

(AT 433) And since the first of these sums divided by d is the same as the second divided by e, it is manifest that FP times CG is to GP times CF — that is, PQ is to PN — as d is to e, which is all that had to be proved. The verification reduces to the identity that one numerator is d times a common factor and the other e times it. The proof is a check by direct computation, the analysis having produced AP and synthesis now confirming that the sine condition holds at every point.

(AT 433–434) The same demonstration extends to everything said of the other refractions or reflections in the proposed ovals, with no change but the signs + and − in the calculation, so everyone can easily examine them for himself without his dwelling on it. The extension by changing signs reflects the uniform treatment of cases that the coordinate method allows, which the case analysis of Book I (AT 383) had prepared. The reader is again left to verify the remaining cases.

(AT 434) He must now make up for what he omitted in the Optics. After noting that there can be glasses of several figures that equally make rays from one point of the object gather at another point after crossing them, and that among these glasses those very convex on one side and concave on the other have more power to burn than those equally convex on both sides, while the latter are best for telescopes, he contented himself with explaining those he thought best in practice, given the difficulty craftsmen may have in cutting them. So that nothing remains to be desired in the theory of this science, he must explain here the figure of glasses that, having one surface as convex or concave as desired, still make all rays coming toward them from one point, or parallel, gather afterwards at one point; and of glasses that do the same while being equally convex on both sides, or with the convexity of one surface in a given ratio to that of the other. The unfinished business of Optics VIII (AT 185, 189–194) is taken up: the Optics chose hyperbolic lenses for ease of cutting, and the Geometry now gives the theoretically complete solution with a free choice of curvature. "So that nothing remains to be desired in the theory" marks the difference between the two essays: practice is constrained by craft, theory only by geometry.

(AT 434–437; margin: "How to make a glass as convex or concave as desired on one of its surfaces, which gathers at a given point all the rays coming from another given point") For the first case, suppose the points G, Y, C and F given, and that the rays from G, or parallel to GA, must gather at F after crossing a glass so concave that, Y being the middle of its inner surface, its edge is at C, so that the chord CMC and the arrow YM of the arc CYC are given. First one must consider which of the ovals the surface YC should have, to make all rays inside it that tend toward one point, such as H, not yet known, go toward another, F, after leaving. For there is no effect regarding rays changed by reflection or refraction from one point to another that cannot be caused by one of these ovals; and this one can be caused by the part 3A3 of the third oval, by its part 3Y3, or by the part 2X2 of the second. Since these three fall under the same calculation, one takes Y as their vertex, C as a point of their circumference and F as one of their burning points, after which only H, the other burning point, remains to be found. It is found by considering that the difference between FY and FC must be to that between HY and HC as d to e — the greater of the lines measuring the refractions to the lesser — as is evident from the description of the ovals. Since FY and FC are given, so is their difference, and so the difference between HY and HC; and since YM is given, so is the difference between MH and HC; and since CM is given, it remains only to find MH, a side of the right triangle CMH, whose other side CM is known together with the difference between the base CH and the side MH, which is easy: taking k for the excess of CH over MH and n for CM, one has nn/2k − ½k for MH. Having found H, if it is farther from Y than F is, CY must be the first part of the oval of the third genre, 3A3; but if HY is less than FY, either it exceeds HF by so much that their difference, in relation to the whole FY, is greater than e, the lesser of the lines measuring refraction, compared with d, the greater — that is, with HF ∞ c and HY ∞ c + h, dh is greater than 2ce + eh — and then CY must be the second part of the same oval, 3Y3; or dh is equal to or less than 2ce + eh, and then CY must be the second part of the oval of the second genre, 2X2. Finally, if H is the same as F, which happens only when FY and FC are equal, the line YC is a circle. The claim that every point-to-point transformation by reflection or refraction can be achieved by one of these ovals is the completeness theorem of the ovals, the analogue of AT 175 for the Optics' conics. The problem is solved by locating the unknown focus H from the given chord, sagitta and focus, through a right-triangle calculation, and the conditions decide which branch applies. The case analysis by inequalities is complete, and the degenerate circle is identified, so nothing is left to chance.

(AT 437) Then one must find CAC, the glass's other surface, which must be an ellipse with H as burning point if the rays falling on it are supposed parallel, and is then easy to find. But if they come from G, it must be the first part of an oval of the first genre whose burning points are G and H and which passes through C; from which the vertex A of this oval is found by considering that GC must exceed GA by a quantity that is to the excess of HA over HC as d to e. Taking k for the difference between CH and HM and supposing x for AM, x − k is the difference between AH and CH; then, taking g for the difference between GC and GM, which are given, g + x is the difference between GC and GA; and since this last is to the other, x − k, as d to e, one has ge + ex ∞ dx − dk, or (ge + dk)/(d − e) for the line x or AM, which determines the point A. The second surface converts the incoming pencil into one aimed at the intermediate focus H, so the lens works in two stages: the front surface sends rays toward H, the rear surface takes them from H to F. For parallel rays the ellipse of Optics VIII serves, which shows the ovals extending, not replacing, the earlier results.

(AT 437–438; margin: "How to make a glass having the same effect as the preceding, with the convexity of one of its surfaces in a given ratio to that of the other") Suppose now, for the other case, that only the points G, C and F are given, with the ratio between AM and YM, and that the figure of the glass ACY is required which makes all rays from G gather at F. The second problem fixes the ratio of the two sagittas instead of one surface, the case of a lens with surfaces of chosen relative curvature. This covers the equi-convex lens the Optics preferred for telescopes, now in exact form.

(AT 438–439) Two ovals can again serve, one, AC, with G and H as burning points, the other, CY, with F and H. To find them, supposing first that H, common to both, is known, he seeks AM by the three points G, C, H in the way just explained, taking k for the difference between CH and HM and g for that between GC and GM, and, AC being the first part of the oval of the first genre, gets (ge + dk)/(d − e) for AM. Then he seeks MY by the three points F, C, H, so that CY is the first part of an oval of the third genre: taking y for MY and f for the difference between CF and FM, he has f + y for that between CF and FY; and, having already k for that between CH and HM, he has k + y for that between CH and HY, which must be to f + y as e is to d because of the oval of the third genre; whence MY is (fe − dk)/(d − e). Joining the two quantities found for AM and MY, he finds (ge + fe)/(d − e) for the whole AY. So, wherever H is supposed, AY is always composed of a quantity that is to the excess of GC and CF together over the whole GF as e, the lesser of the lines measuring refraction, is to d − e, their difference — "a rather fine theorem." Having the whole AY, one must divide it in the ratio its parts AM and MY must have, and since M is already known, A and Y are found, and then H by the preceding problem. But first one must check whether AM so found is greater than, less than or equal to ge/(d − e): if greater, AC must be the first part of an oval of the first genre and CY the first part of one of the third, as supposed here; if less, CY must be the first part of an oval of the first genre and AC the first of one of the third; and if equal, AC and CY must both be hyperbolas. The unknown H drops out of the total thickness AY, which yields a theorem: the axial thickness of such a lens depends only on the excess of the broken path GC + CF over the straight path GF, scaled by the refraction ratio. Descartes's pleasure in the result ("assés beau") is that of a regularity found by calculation that no construction would have suggested. The case analysis again settles which ovals the surfaces must be, with hyperbolas as the boundary case.

(AT 439) These two problems could be extended to infinitely many other cases, which he does not stop to deduce, since they have had no use in dioptrics. The criterion for stopping is use, the practical end of the Optics, so the geometry of lenses is developed exactly as far as optics needs it. The remark is consistent with the Discourse's ordering of theory to practical philosophy.

(AT 439–440) One could also go further and say, when one surface of the glass is given, provided it is entirely flat or composed of conic sections or circles, how its other surface must be made to transmit all rays from one given point to another given point. This is no harder than what he has just explained, or rather much easier, the way being open; but he prefers that others look for it, so that, if they still have some trouble finding it, they will esteem the more the invention of the things demonstrated here. The problem of the second surface given the first is left as an exercise, with the stated motive that difficulty teaches the value of what is given. The general case requires curves beyond the ovals, so the claim that it is "much easier" is optimistic; the remark is typical of the Geometry's habit of withholding.

(AT 440–441; margin: "How what has been said here of curves described on a plane surface can be applied to those described in a space of three dimensions") He has spoken in all this only of curves that can be described on a plane surface, but what he has said can easily be applied to all those that can be imagined formed by the regular motion of the points of some body in a space of three dimensions: draw from each point of the curve two perpendiculars to two planes intersecting at right angles, one to each; their ends describe two other curves, one on each plane, all of whose points can be determined in the way explained and related to those of the straight line common to the two planes, by which the points of the three-dimensional curve are entirely determined. Even to draw a straight line cutting this curve at right angles at a given point, one need only draw in the two planes two other lines, one in each, cutting at right angles the two curves there at the points where the perpendiculars from the given point fall; and, raising on each of these lines a plane cutting at right angles the plane it is in, the intersection of these two planes will be the line sought. So he thinks he has omitted none of the elements needed for knowing curved lines. The extension to space curves by two projections is the only place where the Geometry treats three dimensions, and it amounts to a sketch of coordinates in space. The AT editors point out that the normal construction is wrong: at a point of a space curve there are infinitely many normals, all in one plane, and the line Descartes constructs is a normal only in special cases, as a straight line in space and its two projections show at once. None of his contemporaries noticed the error. The confident close, "I think I have omitted none of the elements," is therefore undercut in its last step, though the idea of describing a space curve by two plane projections was sound and fruitful.

### Third Book: On the Construction of Problems That Are Solid or More Than Solid

(AT 442; margin: "Which curved lines may be used in the construction of each problem") Although all curves that can be described by some regular motion must be received in geometry, it does not follow that one may use indifferently the first one met for constructing each problem; one must always take care to choose the simplest by which it can be solved. And by the simplest one must understand not only those most easily described, nor those that make the construction or demonstration easiest, but chiefly those of the simplest genre that can serve to determine the quantity sought. The admission of curves in Book II is now regulated by a norm of economy: a problem must be constructed by the curve of lowest genre adequate to it. Simplicity is defined by genre, that is, by the degree of the equation, and explicitly not by ease of tracing. Newton objected to exactly this in the Arithmetica universalis (1707), arguing that a curve's simplicity should be judged by how easily it is described, which shows the choice was contestable from the start.

(AT 442–443; margin: "Example concerning the finding of several mean proportionals") He believes there is no easier way to find as many mean proportionals as one wants, nor one whose demonstration is more evident, than to use the curves described by the instrument XYZ explained above (AT 391). To find two mean proportionals between YA and YE, one need only describe a circle with diameter YE; since it cuts the curve AD at D, YD is one of the means sought, and the demonstration is visible to the eye from the instrument applied to YD: as YA, or YB which equals it, is to YC, so YC is to YD and YD to YE. The mesolabe gives a construction whose evidence is immediate, since the similar right triangles formed by the rulers display the continued proportion. It is introduced as the obvious solution so that the next paragraphs can show it is the wrong one by the standard of AT 442.

(AT 443) Likewise, to find four mean proportionals between YA and YG, or six between YA and YN, one need only trace the circle YFG, which, cutting AF at F, determines YF, one of the four, or YHN, which, cutting AH at H, determines YH, one of the six; and so on. The instrument extends uniformly to any number of means, each additional pair of rulers producing a curve of higher degree. The uniformity is elegant, and Descartes's point is that elegance of this kind is not the criterion.

(AT 443–444) But since the curve AD is of the second genre, and two mean proportionals can be found by the conic sections, which are of the first, and since four or six can be found by lines of genres less composite than AF and AH, it would be a fault in geometry to use them. It is also a fault, on the other side, to labour uselessly at constructing a problem by a simpler genre of line than its nature permits. Two opposite faults are named: using a curve more complex than needed, and trying to do with a simpler curve what cannot be done with it. The second fault turns the classical problems of cube duplication and angle trisection into questions of proof: to stop trying to solve them by ruler and compass one must know that it is impossible, which requires a theory of equations.

(AT 444; margin: "On the nature of equations") To give some rules for avoiding both faults, he must say something in general about the nature of equations, that is, of sums composed of several terms, partly known and partly unknown, some equal to the others, or rather all together equal to nothing, for it will often be best to consider them so. The treatise turns from geometry to algebra because the classification of problems depends on properties of equations. The convention of writing every equation as a polynomial equal to zero, introduced at AT 415, is now adopted as the standard form, since the properties of roots are read most easily from it.

(AT 444–445; margin: "How many roots there can be in each equation") Know then that in each equation there can be as many different roots, that is, values of the unknown, as the unknown has dimensions. If x is supposed equal to 2, or x − 2 equal to nothing, and again x ∞ 3, or x − 3 ∞ 0, multiplying these two equations gives xx − 5x + 6 ∞ 0, in which x is worth 2 and also 3. And if x − 4 ∞ 0 is multiplied by this sum, there comes x³ − 9xx + 26x − 24 ∞ 0, in which x, having three dimensions, has three values, 2, 3 and 4. The bound on the number of roots is shown constructively, by building equations as products of binomials, which makes the link between roots and factors visible. Descartes says "can be" (peut), an upper bound. The claim that one can always "imagine" as many roots as the degree (AT 453) is an early statement of what became the fundamental theorem of algebra, proved only in the nineteenth century; Albert Girard had stated a version in 1629.

(AT 445; margin: "Which are the false roots") Often some of these roots are false, or less than nothing: if x also designates the defect of a quantity, say 5, one has x + 5 ∞ 0, which multiplied by x³ − 9xx + 26x − 24 ∞ 0 gives x⁴ − 4x³ − 19xx + 106x − 120 ∞ 0, an equation with four roots, three true, 2, 3, 4, and one false, 5. Negative roots are admitted as roots, though called "false" and described as the "defect" of a quantity, so that their magnitude is given and their sign is treated as a kind of lack. Including them is what makes the count of roots and the rule of signs work, so the theory forces Descartes beyond his geometrical habit of considering only lengths.

(AT 445; margin: "How to diminish the number of dimensions of an equation when one knows some of its roots") It is evident from this that the sum of an equation containing several roots can always be divided by a binomial composed of the unknown minus the value of any one of the true roots, or plus the value of one of the false ones, by which its dimensions are diminished by as much. This is the factor theorem: a known root yields a linear factor, and division lowers the degree by one. It is the algebraic counterpart of the "use every division possible" of Book I (AT 374), now with a criterion for when division is possible.

(AT 445–446; margin: "How one can know whether some given quantity is the value of a root") Conversely, if the sum of an equation cannot be divided by a binomial composed of the unknown plus or minus some other quantity, this shows that this quantity is not the value of any of its roots. So x⁴ − 4x³ − 19xx + 106x − 120 ∞ 0 can be divided by x − 2, x − 3, x − 4 and x + 5, but not by x plus or minus any other quantity, which shows it can have only the four roots 2, 3, 4 and 5. The converse turns divisibility into a test for roots. That the four binomials exhaust the roots of a quartic follows from the bound on their number, so the example also illustrates that bound.

(AT 446; margin: "How many true roots there can be in each equation") One also knows how many true and how many false roots there can be in each equation: as many true as the signs + and − change, and as many false as there are pairs of successive like signs. In the last equation, since after +x⁴ comes −4x³, a change of sign, and after −19xx comes +106x, and after +106x comes −120, two more changes, there are three true roots; and one false, since the two signs − of 4x³ and 19xx follow each other. This is Descartes's rule of signs, stated without proof. As an upper bound it is correct, and the number of positive roots differs from the number of sign changes by an even number, a refinement and proof that came only in the eighteenth century (Segner, 1728; Gauss, 1828). The rule's formulation for false roots holds only when all places are filled and the roots are real, which is why the next paragraphs give ways to fill the places and to make all roots true.

(AT 446–447; margin: "How one makes the false roots of an equation become true, and the true false") It is easy to make all the false roots of an equation become true and all the true become false, by changing all the signs + or − in the second, fourth, sixth and other even places, without changing those in the first, third, fifth and other odd places. So instead of +x⁴ − 4x³ − 19xx + 106x − 120 ∞ 0 one writes +x⁴ + 4x³ − 19xx − 106x − 120 ∞ 0, with one true root, 5, and three false, 2, 3 and 4. The transformation is the substitution of −x for x, described as an operation on signs. It makes the true and false roots symmetric, which supports treating them as two kinds of one thing rather than real and fictitious solutions.

(AT 447; margin: "How one can increase or diminish the roots of an equation without knowing them") If, without knowing the roots of an equation, one wants to increase or diminish them by some known quantity, one need only suppose another unknown greater or less by that quantity and substitute it everywhere for the first. To increase by 3 the root of x⁴ + 4x³ − 19xx − 106x − 120 ∞ 0, take y instead of x, with y − 3 equal to x, and put for xx the square of y − 3, for x³ its cube, for x⁴ its square of square; so the sum becomes y⁴ − 8y³ − yy + 8y ∞ 0, or y³ − 8yy − y + 8 ∞ 0, where the true root, which was 5, is now 8, because of the three added. The translation of roots by substitution is a general tool that operates on an equation whose roots are unknown, which is what makes it useful for analysis. The example also shows a root becoming zero, so that the degree drops, which the next paragraphs explain.

(AT 448) If instead one wants to diminish the root of the same equation by three, one must make y + 3 ∞ x and yy + 6y + 9 ∞ xx, and so on, so that instead of x⁴ + 4x³ − 19xx − 106x − 120 ∞ 0 one writes y⁴ + 16y³ + 71yy − 4y − 420 ∞ 0. The same operation in the other direction shows that translation works both ways. The worked arithmetic lets the reader check the substitution term by term.

(AT 448–449; margin: "That by increasing the true roots one diminishes the false, and conversely") Diminishing the true roots of an equation increases the false ones by the same quantity, and conversely; and if either is diminished by a quantity equal to it, it becomes zero, and if by a greater quantity, true roots become false or false true. So, by increasing the true root 5 by 3, each false root has been diminished by 3, so that 4 is now only 1, 3 is zero, and 2 has become true and is 1, since −2 + 3 makes +1; which is why y³ − 8yy − y + 8 ∞ 0 has only three roots, two true, 1 and 8, and one false, also 1. In the other, y⁴ + 16y³ + 71yy − 4y − 420 ∞ 0, there is only one true root, 2, since 5 − 3 makes 2, and three false, 5, 6 and 7. The behaviour of true and false roots under translation treats them as positions on one line, with zero between them, which is the conception of signed numbers that Descartes's terms "true" and "false" otherwise obscure. The passage from true to false as a root crosses zero is described in words, without a number line, but with exactly that structure.

(AT 449–450; margin: "How one can remove the second term of an equation") By this way of changing the roots without knowing them, two things can be done that will be useful later. The first is that one can always remove the second term of the equation under examination, by diminishing the true roots by the known quantity of the second term divided by the number of dimensions of the first, if one of these terms has + and the other −, or increasing them by the same if both have + or both −. To remove the second term of y⁴ + 16y³ + 71yy − 4y − 420 ∞ 0, dividing 16 by 4 gives 4, so he writes z − 4 ∞ y and obtains z⁴ − 25zz − 60z − 36 ∞ 0, where the true root, which was 2, is 6, increased by 4, and the false ones, 5, 6 and 7, are only 1, 2 and 3, diminished by 4. Removing the second term, a technique going back to Cardano and Viète, reduces every cubic and quartic to a depressed form with fewer coefficients. The constructions of AT 464–470 require this form, which is why the operation is introduced here.

(AT 450) Likewise, to remove the second term of x⁴ − 2ax³ + (2aa − cc)xx − 2a³x + a⁴ ∞ 0, dividing 2a by 4 gives ½a, so one must make z + ½a ∞ x, write the substituted sum, and, having found the value of z, add ½a to it to get x. A literal example shows that the procedure does not depend on numerical coefficients. The equation is the one that will arise from the Pappus problem at AT 462, so the example is prepared for later use.

(AT 450–451; margin: "How one can make all the false roots of an equation become true without the true becoming false") The second useful thing is that one can always, by increasing the true roots by a quantity greater than any of the false, make them all true, so that no two signs + or − follow each other, and moreover make the known quantity of the third term greater than the square of half that of the second. Though this is done when the false roots are unknown, it is easy to judge roughly their size and take a quantity exceeding them by as much as needed. For example, with x⁶ + nx⁵ − 6nnx⁴ + 36n³x³ − 216n⁴xx + 1296n⁵x − 7776n⁶ ∞ 0, making y − 6n ∞ x gives an equation in which 504nn, the known quantity of the third term, is clearly greater than the square of 35n/2, half that of the second; and there is no case where the quantity added needs to be greater, in proportion to the given ones, than in this one. The condition on the third term is exactly what the sextic construction of AT 477 needs (q greater than (½p)²). The claim that a shift of six times n always suffices in proportion is asserted from the example rather than proved, a bound found by inspection.

(AT 451–452; margin: "How one makes all the places of an equation filled") Since the last term here comes out zero, if one does not want that, the roots must be increased a little more, and it cannot be by so little that it is not enough; just as when one wants to increase the number of dimensions of an equation and fill all the places of its terms. If instead of x⁵ − b ∞ 0 one wants an equation of six dimensions with no term missing, one must first write x⁶ − bx ∞ 0, then, making y − a ∞ x, one has y⁶ − 6ay⁵ + 15aay⁴ − 20a³y³ + 15a⁴yy − (6a⁵ + b)y + a⁶ + ab ∞ 0, where, however small a is supposed, all the places of the equation are filled. Filling every place matters because the rule of signs and the sextic construction both presuppose a complete equation. Raising the degree by multiplying by x is a deliberate complication in the service of a uniform construction, which shows that the method prefers one general procedure to many special ones even at the cost of a higher degree.

(AT 452; margin: "How one can multiply or divide the roots without knowing them") Moreover, without knowing the true roots of an equation, one can multiply or divide them all by any known quantity, by supposing that the unknown multiplied or divided by that quantity equals another, then multiplying or dividing the known quantity of the second term by it, that of the third by its square, that of the fourth by its cube, and so to the last. Scaling roots by substitution completes the set of transformations: translation, reflection and scaling. The rule for the coefficients is stated directly, so the reader can apply it without carrying out the substitution.

(AT 452–453; margin: "How one reduces the fractions in an equation to whole numbers") This can serve to reduce to whole and rational numbers the fractions, and often also the surds, found in the terms of equations. If one has x³ − √3 xx + (26/27)x − 8/(27√3) ∞ 0 and wants another in its place with all terms rational, one must suppose y ∞ x√3 and multiply by √3 the known quantity of the second term, by its square, 3, that of the third, and by its cube, 3√3, that of the last, which gives y³ − 3yy + (26/9)y − 8/9 ∞ 0; then, for one with only whole numbers, suppose z ∞ 3y and, multiplying 3 by 3, 26/9 by 9 and 8/9 by 27, one finds z³ − 9zz + 26z − 24 ∞ 0, whose roots being 2, 3 and 4, those of the preceding were 2/3, 1 and 4/3, and those of the first 2/(3√3), 1/√3 and 4/(3√3). Clearing fractions and surds prepares the search for rational roots among divisors of the last term (AT 454). The example returns to the cubic of AT 445, so the reader sees a familiar equation disguised and recovered.

(AT 453; margin: "How one makes the known quantity of one of the terms of an equation equal to any other one wants") This operation can also serve to make the known quantity of one of the terms equal to some other given one: if, having x³ − bbx + c³ ∞ 0, one wants an equation in which the known quantity of the third term, bb here, is 3aa, one must suppose y ∞ x√(3aa/bb) and write y³ − 3aay + (3a³c³/b³)√3 ∞ 0. Normalising a coefficient fits an equation to a construction whose parameters are fixed, such as the trisection form z³ ∞ 3z − q of AT 470. The transformations thus serve as adaptors between arbitrary equations and standard constructions.

(AT 453–454; margin: "That roots, both true and false, can be real or imaginary") Moreover, neither true nor false roots are always real; sometimes they are only imaginary, that is, one can always imagine as many in each equation as he said, but sometimes no quantity corresponds to those imagined. So, though one can imagine three in x³ − 6xx + 13x − 10 ∞ 0, there is only one real, 2, and the other two, however increased, diminished or multiplied in the ways explained, can only be made imaginary. Here the word "imaginary" enters mathematics in this sense. The roots are "imagined" to make up the count the degree requires, and the test of their unreality is that none of the transformations can make them real, a definition by invariance. The phrasing keeps the fundamental theorem as a claim about what can be imagined, without asserting that the imagined roots are quantities.

(AT 454; margin: "The reduction of cubic equations when the problem is plane") When, to find the construction of some problem, one reaches an equation in which the unknown has three dimensions, one must first reduce any fractions among its known quantities to whole numbers by the multiplication explained, and any surds to rational numbers as far as possible, by the same multiplication or by various other easily found means. Then, examining in order all the quantities that divide the last term without remainder, one must see whether any of them, joined to the unknown by + or −, makes a binomial dividing the whole sum. If so, the problem is plane, that is, it can be constructed with ruler and compass, since either the known quantity of the binomial is the root sought, or the equation, divided by it, is reduced to two dimensions, so its root can be found by what was said in the first book. This is the rational root test, applied as a criterion of constructibility. If a cubic has a rational root, the problem reduces to a quadratic and is plane; if not, it is solid, as AT 456–457 asserts. The criterion is correct for cubics with rational coefficients, though Descartes does not prove that a cubic without a rational root cannot be constructed with ruler and compass, which Wantzel showed in 1837.

(AT 454–455) For example, with y⁶ − 8y⁴ − 124yy − 64 ∞ 0, the last term, 64, can be divided without remainder by 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64. So one must examine in order whether the equation can be divided by one of the binomials yy − 1 or yy + 1, yy − 2 or yy + 2, yy − 4, and so on, and one finds it can by yy − 16. The equation is a cubic in yy, so the test is applied to binomials in yy. The example is the resolvent cubic of a quartic that will appear at AT 458, so the method is shown on material it will soon need.

(AT 455; margin: "The way to divide an equation by a binomial containing its root") He begins with the last term and divides −64 by −16, which gives +4, written in the quotient; then multiplies +4 by +yy, giving +4yy, so he writes −4yy in the sum to be divided, since the sign opposite to that produced by the multiplication must always be written there; joining −124yy with −4yy gives −128yy, which divided by −16 gives +8yy for the quotient; multiplying it by yy gives −8y⁴ to join with the term to be divided, also −8y⁴, which together make −16y⁴, divided by −16 to give +y⁴ for the quotient, and −y⁶ to join with +y⁶, which makes 0 and shows the division complete. Had any quantity remained, or had one of the preceding terms not been divisible without fraction, it would have been known that the division could not be done. Polynomial division is worked from the lowest term upward, the reverse of the usual order, which suits the search for divisors of the constant term. The quotient y⁴ + 8yy + 4 shows the equation reduced, and the criterion of failure — a remainder or a fraction — makes the test decidable.

(AT 456) Likewise, with y⁶ + (aa − 2cc)y⁴ − (a⁴ − c⁴)yy − a⁶ − 2a⁴cc − aac⁴ ∞ 0, the last term can be divided without fraction by a, aa, aa + cc, a³ + acc and the like, but only two need be considered, aa and aa + cc, since the others, giving more or fewer dimensions in the quotient than in the known quantity of the penultimate term, would prevent the division from being done there; and note that he counts the dimensions of y⁶ here as only three, since there is no y⁵, y³ or y in the whole sum. Examining the binomial yy − aa − cc ∞ 0, one finds the division can be done by it, which shows the root sought is aa + cc; the proof is easy by multiplication. The literal case shows the test applied with a dimensional pruning of candidates, a use of homogeneity to limit the search. The equation is the resolvent for the Pappus example of AT 462, so this calculation is used again at AT 461.

(AT 456–457; margin: "Which problems are solid when the equation is cubic") But when no binomial is found that divides the whole sum of the proposed equation, the problem depending on it is certainly solid. And after that it is no less a fault to try to construct it using only circles and straight lines than it would be to use conic sections to construct those that need only circles: for, in the end, whatever shows some ignorance is called a fault. The certainty claimed here — that the absence of a rational root makes a cubic problem solid — is asserted, not demonstrated. The last sentence turns a mathematical classification into a norm of competence: using the wrong curve is a "fault" because it betrays ignorance of the problem's nature, the language of Discourse Part Two's rules applied to the craft of geometry.

(AT 457–458; margin: "The reduction of equations of four dimensions when the problem is plane, and which are solid") If the unknown has four dimensions, one must likewise, after removing surds and fractions, see whether some binomial can be found dividing the whole sum, composed with one of the quantities dividing the last term without fraction. If one is found, either its known quantity is the root sought, or after division only three dimensions remain, to be examined again in the same way. But when no such binomial is found, one must remove the second term by increasing or diminishing the root, and then reduce the equation to another with only three dimensions, as follows: instead of +x⁴ * .pxx .qx .r ∞ 0 one must write +y⁶ .2py⁴ +(pp .4r)yy −qq ∞ 0, with +2p if there was +p and −2p if −p; −4r if +r and +4r if −r; and always −qq and +pp, at least supposing x⁴ and y⁶ marked +. This is Descartes's method for the quartic: the depressed quartic is reduced to a cubic in yy, the resolvent. His resolvent differs from Ferrari's but serves the same end. The sign rules are given in words because the symbols for "plus or minus" were not yet standard, and the dots in the formulas mark undetermined signs, as Descartes's asterisks mark missing terms.

(AT 458) For example, for x⁴ * − 4xx − 8x + 35 ∞ 0 one must write y⁶ − 8y⁴ − 124yy − 64 ∞ 0: p being −4, one puts −8y⁴ for 2py⁴; r being 35, −124yy, that is (16 − 140)yy, for (pp − 4r)yy; and q being 8, −64 for −qq. Likewise, instead of x⁴ * − 17xx − 20x − 6 ∞ 0 one writes y⁶ − 34y⁴ + 313yy − 400 ∞ 0, since 34 is double 17, 313 is its square plus four times 6, and 400 is the square of 20. And likewise, for the literal quartic from AT 450, one obtains the resolvent already divided at AT 456, since p is aa − cc, pp is a⁴ − 2aacc + c⁴, 4r is −3a⁴ + 4aacc, and −qq is −a⁶ − 2a⁴cc − aac⁴. Three examples are worked so that the reader can see the coefficient rules applied, including one whose resolvent was solved two pages earlier. The examples are chosen to show different outcomes, as the next paragraphs reveal.

(AT 459) After the equation has been reduced to three dimensions, one must find the value of yy by the method explained; if it cannot be found, one need go no further, for it follows infallibly that the problem is solid. But if it is found, one can divide the preceding equation by its means into two others, each with only two dimensions in the unknown, whose roots are the same as its own: instead of +x⁴ * .pxx .qx .r ∞ 0 one writes the two equations +xx − yx + ½yy .½p .(q/2y) ∞ 0 and +xx + yx + ½yy .½p .(q/2y) ∞ 0, with signs given by rules, after which all the roots of the proposed equation are easily known, and the problem constructed with circles and straight lines only. The quartic is plane exactly when its resolvent has a rational root, and then it factors into two quadratics. The rule decides the constructibility of quartic problems in the same way as the rational root test did for cubics.

(AT 459–460) For example, since, making y⁶ − 34y⁴ + 313yy − 400 ∞ 0 for x⁴ * − 17xx − 20x − 6 ∞ 0, one finds yy is 16, instead of this equation one must write the two others xx − 4x − 3 ∞ 0 and xx + 4x + 2 ∞ 0, since y is 4, ½yy is 8, p is 17 and q is 20, so that ½yy − ½p − q/2y makes −3 and ½yy − ½p + q/2y makes +2. The example's numbers can be checked directly, since the product of the two quadratics restores the quartic. The procedure is now fully algorithmic.

(AT 460) Taking the roots of these two equations, one finds all the same ones as from the equation in x⁴: one true, √7 + 2, and three false, √7 − 2, 2 + √2 and 2 − √2. The roots are listed by kind, the false ones by their magnitudes, consistent with AT 445. The surd roots show why the problem is plane even though its equation is of fourth degree.

(AT 460) So, having x⁴ * − 4xx − 8x + 35 ∞ 0, since the root of y⁶ − 8y⁴ − 124yy − 64 ∞ 0 is again 16, one must write xx − 4x + 5 ∞ 0 and xx + 4x + 7 ∞ 0, since here the terms ½yy, ½p and q/2y, taken with their signs, make 5 in one equation and 7 in the other. The same resolvent root factors a different quartic, preparing the case of entirely imaginary roots. The reader is shown that a successful reduction does not guarantee a real construction.

(AT 461) And since no root, true or false, is found in these last two equations, one knows that the four of the equation they come from are imaginary, and that the problem for which it was found is plane by nature but cannot in any way be constructed, since the given quantities cannot be joined. The distinction between a problem's nature and its possibility is exact: the problem is plane, since the equation factors into quadratics, but impossible, since the quadratics have no real roots. "The given quantities cannot be joined" is the geometrical form of the absence of real intersection (AT 376).

(AT 461) Likewise, having the depressed literal quartic from AT 450, since aa + cc is found for yy, one must write two quadratics in z with coefficients involving √(aa + cc), from which the value of z is known, and since z + ½a ∞ x was made above, x, for which all these operations were done, is ½a + √(¼aa + ¼cc) − √(¼cc − ½aa + ½a√(aa + cc)). The literal case gives the root of the Pappus problem of the next paragraph before the problem itself is stated. The order is analytic: the algebra is completed first, and then its geometrical source is revealed.

(AT 461–462; margin: "Example of the use of these reductions") To show the usefulness of this rule better, he must apply it to some problem. The method is now to be tested on a problem with a known classical solution. Comparing the two routes will show what the algebra contributes and what it costs.

(AT 462–463) If, the square AD and the line BN being given, the side AC must be extended to E so that EF, drawn from E toward B, equals NB, Pappus teaches that, having first extended BD to G so that DG equals DN, and described a circle with diameter BG, the extended line AC will meet its circumference at E, as required. But for those not knowing this construction it would be quite hard to find, and seeking it by the method proposed here, they would never think of taking DG for the unknown, but rather CF or FD, since these lead most easily to the equation; and then they would find one not easy to untangle without the rule just explained. Setting a for BD or CD, c for EF and x for DF, one has CF ∞ a − x, and as CF, or a − x, is to FE, or c, so FD, or x, is to BF, which is therefore cx/(a − x); then, by the right triangle BDF, xx + aa equals the square of the base, ccxx/(xx − 2ax + aa), so, multiplying by xx − 2ax + aa, the equation is x⁴ − 2ax³ + (2aa − cc)xx − 2a³x + a⁴ ∞ 0, and by the preceding rules its root, the length of DF, is ½a + √(¼aa + ¼cc) − √(¼cc − ½aa + ½a√(aa + cc)). The comparison is candid. Pappus's construction is simple and plane, while the method, taking the natural unknown, produces a quartic that only the reduction rules can resolve. The method still succeeds, and it shows the problem is plane, which the classical construction presupposes without explaining.

(AT 463) If one took BF, CE or BE for the unknown, one would again reach an equation of four dimensions, but easier to untangle, and reached easily; whereas taking DG, one would reach the equation much less easily, but it would be very simple. He says this to warn that when a problem is not solid, if one reaches a very composite equation by one path, one can usually reach a simpler one by another. The choice of unknown is a matter of skill not settled by the method, as the choice of reference line was in Book II (AT 393). The lesson qualifies the method's mechanical character: the degree of the equation is fixed by the problem, but its complexity depends on how it is set up.

(AT 463) He could add various rules for untangling equations of the cube or square of square, but they would be superfluous, since when problems are plane their construction can always be found by these. The criterion of sufficiency governs what is included: once plane problems are fully handled, further rules add nothing to the method. This matches the restraint of the Optics, which stopped where use stopped (AT 439).

(AT 463–464; margin: "General rule for reducing equations that go beyond the square of square") He could also add others for equations rising to the sursolid, the square of cube or beyond, but prefers to include them all in one, and to say in general that, when one has tried to reduce them to the same form as those of as many dimensions that come from multiplying two others of fewer, and, having enumerated all the ways this multiplication is possible, it has succeeded by none, one must be sure they cannot be reduced to simpler ones. So if the unknown has three or four dimensions, the problem is solid; if five or six, it is one degree more composite; and so on. The general rule is reducibility by enumeration of possible factorisations, which applies the fourth precept to algebra: if every possible decomposition has been tried and failed, the equation is irreducible and the problem's rank is fixed. The rule is correct in principle, though the enumeration Descartes envisages is not fully specified for higher degrees and its completeness is asserted rather than shown.

(AT 464) He has omitted the demonstrations of most of what he has said, because they seemed so easy that, provided one takes the trouble to examine methodically whether he has erred, they will present themselves, and it will be more useful to learn them this way than by reading them. The pedagogy of the Discourse (AT 72) is restated at the moment when proofs matter most. The reader is asked to verify "methodically," which makes the method itself the guarantee of the omitted proofs.

(AT 464–465; margin: "General way to construct all solid problems reduced to an equation of three or four dimensions") When one is sure the problem is solid, whether the equation rises to the square of square or only to the cube, its root can always be found by any one of the three conic sections, or even by some part of one, however small, using only straight lines and circles besides; but he will be content to give a general rule for finding them all by means of a parabola, since it is in a way the simplest. First one must remove the second term of the equation, if it is not already zero, and so reduce it to the form z³ ∞ * .apz .aaq if the unknown has three dimensions, or z⁴ ∞ * .apzz .aaqz .a³r if it has four, or, taking a for unity, z³ ∞ * .pz .q and z⁴ ∞ * .pzz .qz .r. The parabola is chosen as the simplest conic for the purpose, and the depressed forms are the inputs to a single construction. That any small arc of any conic would suffice is a strong claim of generality, consistent with the view that a conic's construction power belongs to its species, not to a particular piece.

(AT 465–467) Then, supposing the parabola FAG already described, with axis ACDKL, latus rectum a or 1, of which AC is half, C inside the parabola and A its vertex, one must make CD equal to ½p, taken on the same side of C as A if there is +p in the equation, and on the other side if −p. From D, or from C if p is zero, raise a perpendicular to E equal to ½q. Finally, from centre E describe the circle FG with radius AE if the equation is only cubic, r being zero. If there is +r, one must take on AE extended AR equal to r on one side and AS equal to the latus rectum, 1, on the other, describe a circle on RS as diameter, and raise AH perpendicular to AE meeting it at H, the point through which the other circle FHG must pass. If there is −r, having found AH so, one must inscribe AI equal to it in another circle with diameter AE, and the first circle FIG must pass through I. This circle can cut or touch the parabola at one, two, three or four points, from which perpendiculars to the axis give all the roots, true and false. If q is marked +, the true roots are the perpendiculars on the same side of the parabola as E, the circle's centre, such as FL, and the others, such as GK, are false; if q is marked −, the reverse. And if the circle neither cuts nor touches the parabola at any point, there is no root, true or false, and all are imaginary. So this rule is the most general and complete one could wish. The intersection of a circle with a fixed parabola solves every cubic and quartic, the generalisation of Menaechmus's use of conics for cube duplication. True and false roots are read from the side of the axis, and imaginary roots from non-intersection, so the whole theory of AT 444–454 is given a figure. The claim of complete generality is justified, since every depressed cubic and quartic fits the scheme.

(AT 467–468) The demonstration is very easy.
