If GK, found by this construction, is called z, AK is zz, since in the parabola GK must be a mean proportional between AK and the latus rectum, 1; subtracting AC, ½, and CD, ½p, from AK leaves DK or EM as zz − ½p − ½, whose square is z⁴ − pzz − zz + ¼pp + ½p + ¼; and since DE or KM is ½q, the whole GM is z + ½q, whose square is zz + qz + ¼qq; adding these two squares gives z⁴ − pzz + qz + ¼qq + ¼pp + ½p + ¼ for the square of GE, the base of the right triangle EMG. The demonstration expresses the circle's radius in two ways, from the intersection point and from the construction's data. The parabola's symptom (AK equals GK squared) supplies the link between geometry and the equation.

(AT 468–469) But since GE is also the circle's radius, it can be expressed otherwise: ED being ½q and AD ½p + ½, EA is √(¼qq + ¼pp + ½p + ¼), by the right angle ADE; and HA being a mean proportional between AS, 1, and AR, r, it is √r, and by the right angle EAH the square of HE or EG is ¼qq + ¼pp + ½p + ¼ + r; so there is an equation between this and the preceding sum, which is the same as z⁴ ∞ * pzz − qz + r, and therefore GK, named z, is the root of this equation, as was to be shown. Applying the same calculation to all other cases of the rule, changing the signs as needed, one will find the account comes out the same. Equating the two expressions for the radius yields the quartic, so the construction is proved. The proof is a model of the treatise's synthesis: short, verifiable and dependent on only two elementary facts, the parabola's symptom and Pythagoras.

(AT 469–470; margin: "The finding of two mean proportionals") To find two mean proportionals between a and q by this rule, everyone knows that, setting z for one, as a is to z, so z is to zz/a and zz/a to z³/aa, so that q equals z³/aa, that is, z³ ∞ * * aaq. With the parabola FAG described, and AC, part of its axis, equal to ½a, half the latus rectum, one must raise from C the perpendicular CE equal to ½q and, describing from E through A the circle AF, one finds FL and LA for the two means sought. The duplication of the cube, the oldest of the solid problems, is solved as a special case of the general rule, with p and r zero. The ancient problem becomes an exercise in applying a formula, the kind of demotion of famous problems the Discourse promised for the method.

(AT 470–471; margin: "The way to divide an angle into three") Likewise, to divide the angle NOP, or the arc NQTP, into three equal parts, making NO ∞ 1 for the radius, NP ∞ q for the chord of the given arc, and NQ ∞ z for the chord of a third of it, the equation is z³ ∞ * 3z − q. For, drawing NQ, OQ and OT, with QS parallel to TO, as NO is to NQ, so NQ is to QR and QR to RS, so that NO being 1 and NQ z, QR is zz and RS z³; and since NP, which is q, falls short of three times NQ, which is z, only by RS, or z³, one has q ∞ 3z − z³, or z³ ∞ * 3z − q. With the parabola described and CA, half its principal latus rectum, being ½, taking CD ∞ 3/2 and the perpendicular DE ∞ ½q, and describing from E through A the circle FAgG, it cuts the parabola at three points F, g and G besides the vertex A, which shows three roots: GK and gk true, and the third, FL, false. Of the two true, gk, the smaller, is the one to take for NQ, the line sought; the other, GK, equals NV, the chord of a third of the arc NVP that completes the circle with NQP; and the false FL equals the two together, QN and NV, as is easy to see by calculation. Trisection gives a cubic with three real roots, and Descartes interprets all three geometrically: the chord of a third of the given arc, the chord of a third of the complementary arc, and their sum as the false root. That every root has a meaning in the figure strengthens the case for admitting false roots, since the construction produces them and the geometry explains them.

(AT 471–472; margin: "That all solid problems can be reduced to these two constructions") It would be superfluous to give other examples, since all merely solid problems can be reduced so far that this rule is needed only to find two mean proportionals or to divide an angle into three equal parts. For their difficulties can always be contained in equations rising only to the square of square or the cube; all those rising to the square of square reduce to the square by means of others rising only to the cube; and the second term can be removed from these, so that every one reduces to one of three forms: z³ ∞ * − pz + q, z³ ∞ * + pz + q, z³ ∞ * + pz − q. If one has z³ ∞ * − pz + q, the rule whose invention Cardan attributes to one Scipio Ferreus teaches that the root is √C.(½q + √(¼qq + p³/27)) − √C.(−½q + √(¼qq + p³/27)); likewise with z³ ∞ * + pz + q, when the square of half the last term exceeds the cube of a third of the known quantity of the penultimate, a similar rule gives the root √C.(½q + √(¼qq − p³/27)) + √C.(½q − √(¼qq − p³/27)). All solid problems reduce to two primitive operations, cube roots and angle trisection, which is a structural theorem about the cubic: Cardano's formula requires cube roots, and the case where the formula fails requires trisection. The attribution to Scipione del Ferro follows Cardano's own Ars magna (1545), which credited del Ferro and Tartaglia.

(AT 472) Hence all problems whose difficulties reduce to one of these two forms can be constructed without needing the conic sections for anything but extracting the cube roots of some given quantities, that is, finding two mean proportionals between them and unity. The conics are needed only for the cube root, so the construction of solid problems reduces to cube duplication. The economy is the point: one solid operation suffices where Cardano's formula applies.

(AT 472–473) If one has z³ ∞ * + pz + q and the square of half the last term is not greater than the cube of a third of the known quantity of the penultimate, then, supposing the circle NQPV with radius NO equal to √(⅓p), the mean proportional between a third of p and unity, and the line NP inscribed in it equal to 3q/p, that is, standing to q as unity to a third of p, one need only divide each of the two arcs NQP and NVP into three equal parts, and NQ, the chord of a third of one, and NV, the chord of a third of the other, joined together, make the root sought. This is the casus irreducibilis, where all three roots are real but Cardano's formula passes through imaginary quantities. Descartes's trigonometric solution avoids them, so the case that troubled sixteenth-century algebra becomes a construction by trisection.

(AT 473) Finally, if one has z³ ∞ * pz − q, supposing again the circle NQPV with radius √(⅓p) and the inscribed NP equal to 3q/p, NQ, the chord of a third of the arc NQP, will be one of the roots sought, and NV, the chord of a third of the other arc, the other — at least if the square of half the last term is not greater than the cube of a third of the known quantity of the penultimate; for if it were greater, NP could not be inscribed in the circle, being longer than its diameter, so the two true roots would be only imaginary and the only real one would be the false root which, by Cardan's rule, is √C.(½q + √(¼qq − p³/27)) + √C.(½q − √(¼qq − p³/27)). The last form completes the case analysis, with the condition for real roots stated as the possibility of inscribing a chord, a geometrical test equivalent to the sign of the discriminant. When the chord cannot be inscribed, the false root is given by Cardano's formula, so the two methods divide the cases between them.

(AT 473–474; margin: "The way to express the value of all the roots of cubic equations, and then of all those that rise only to the square of square") It should be noted that expressing the value of roots by their relation to the sides of certain cubes of which only the content is known is in no way more intelligible or simpler than expressing them by their relation to the chords of certain arcs whose triple is given. So all cubic equations that cannot be expressed by Cardan's rules can be expressed as clearly or more clearly in the way proposed here. Descartes refuses to privilege radicals over trigonometric quantities: a cube root is a quantity known only through its cube, a chord of a third arc only through its triple, and neither is more intelligible. This anticipates the later acceptance of trigonometric and other functions as legitimate expressions of roots, and it reflects the treatise's constant subordination of notation to the intelligibility of the operation.

(AT 474–475) If one thinks one knows the root of z³ ∞ * − pz + q because one knows it is composed of two lines, one the side of a cube whose content is ½q added to the side of a square whose content is ¼qq + p³/27, the other the side of a cube whose content is the difference between ½q and the side of that square — which is all Cardan's rule teaches — there is no doubt one knows as well or more distinctly the root of z³ ∞ * + pz − q by considering it inscribed in a circle with radius √(⅓p) and knowing that it is the chord of an arc whose triple has as chord 3q/p. Indeed these terms are much less involved than the others, and they would be much shorter if one used some particular symbol to express these chords, as one uses √C. for the sides of cubes. The proposal of a special sign for chords of third arcs is a step toward function notation. The comparison of the two knowledges in terms of "distinctness" carries the Discourse's criterion of clear and distinct knowledge (AT 33) into the evaluation of algebraic expressions.

(AT 475) One can also, following this, express the roots of all equations rising to the square of square by the rules explained above, so that he knows nothing more to desire in this matter; for the nature of these roots does not allow them to be expressed in simpler terms, nor determined by any construction both more general and easier. The completeness claim for equations up to the fourth degree is correct in substance, since the quartic reduces to the cubic, and the cubic to cube roots and trisection. The assertion that no simpler expression is possible goes beyond what Descartes could prove, but it was vindicated for the general quintic by Abel and Galois in the opposite direction, by showing what cannot be done.

(AT 475–476; margin: "Why solid problems cannot be constructed without the conic sections, nor those that are more composite without some other more composite lines") He has not yet said on what reasons he relies in daring to affirm whether something is possible or not. But if one notes how, by his method, everything that falls under the geometers' consideration reduces to one kind of problem — finding the value of the roots of some equation — one will judge that it is not hard to make an enumeration of all the ways by which they can be found, sufficient to show that one has chosen the most general and the simplest. In particular, for solid problems, which he said cannot be constructed without some line more composite than the circle, this is easily found from their all reducing to two constructions: one requiring two points to be found at once between two given ones, as in finding two mean proportionals, the other requiring two points dividing a given arc into three equal parts. Since the curvature of the circle depends only on a simple relation of all its parts to the centre, it can only serve to determine one point between two extremes, as in finding one mean proportional or bisecting an arc; whereas the curvature of the conic sections, depending always on two different things, can serve to determine two different points. The impossibility argument is an argument by enumeration plus a heuristic about curvature: the circle has one centre and so fixes one point, a conic has two foci, or two parameters, and so fixes two. The argument is suggestive but not a proof. The correct proof that cube duplication and trisection cannot be done with ruler and compass came only with Wantzel (1837), using the irreducibility of cubics over the field generated by square roots. What Descartes did see correctly is that the question is about the degree of equations, which is where the proof would be found.

(AT 476) For the same reason it is impossible for any problem one degree more composite than the solid ones, presupposing the finding of four mean proportionals or the division of an angle into five equal parts, to be constructed by any of the conic sections. So he thinks he will do the best possible here if he gives a general rule for constructing them, using the curve described by the intersection of a parabola and a straight line in the way explained above; for he dares assert there is none simpler in nature that can serve the same effect, and it has been seen how it immediately follows the conic sections in the question so sought by the ancients, whose solution teaches in order all the curves that must be received in geometry. The trident, introduced in Book I (AT 381–382) as the first curve after the conics in the Pappus problem and traced by the machine of Book II (AT 394–395, 408–410), returns as the instrument for problems of the fifth and sixth degree. The three books are thus joined: the Pappus problem orders the curves, Book II legitimates and classifies them, and Book III assigns each its constructive use.

(AT 476–477; margin: "General way to construct all problems reduced to an equation of no more than six dimensions") It is already known how, when the unknown has five or six dimensions, the equation can be raised to six by increasing its roots, so that they all become true, and the known quantity of the third term is greater than the square of half that of the second; and how, if it rises only to the sursolid, it can be raised to the square of cube with no place empty. So that all these difficulties may be resolved by one rule, he wants all this done, reducing the equation always to the form y⁶ − py⁵ + qy⁴ − ry³ + syy − ty + v ∞ 0, in which q is greater than the square of half of p. The preparatory transformations of AT 450–452 now show their purpose: they bring every equation of degree five or six into one normal form with alternating signs and positive roots. A single construction for all such equations is the treatise's ideal of one method for a whole genre.

(AT 477–479) Having made the line BK indefinitely long both ways, and drawn from B the perpendicular AB with length ½p, one must describe in a separate plane a parabola CDF whose principal latus rectum is √(t/√v + q − ¼pp), called n for short. Then one must place the plane of this parabola on that containing AB and BK so that its axis DE falls exactly over the line BK, and, taking the part of the axis between E and D equal to 2√v/pn, apply at E a long ruler so that, also applied at A in the plane beneath, it stays joined to these two points while the parabola is raised or lowered along BK, on which its axis is applied. The intersection of the parabola and ruler at C will describe the curve ACN needed for constructing the problem. After it is described, take L on BK on the side toward which the parabola's vertex is turned, with BL equal to DE, that is 2√v/pn; then from L toward B take LH equal to t/2n√v, and from H draw at right angles, on the side of the curve ACN, the line HI whose length is r/2nn + √v/nn + pt/4nn√v, called m for short; then, joining L and I, describe the circle LPI with diameter IL and inscribe in it the line LP with length √((s + p√v)/nn); finally, from centre I through P describe the circle PCN. This circle will cut or touch the curve ACN at as many points as the equation has roots, so that the perpendiculars from these points to BK, such as CG, NR, QO and the like, will be the roots sought, without any exception or defect in this rule. For if s were so large in proportion to the others that LP were greater than the diameter IL and could not be inscribed, no root of the equation would be other than imaginary, nor would any if the circle IP were so small as to cut the curve ACN at no point. It can cut it at six different points, as the equation can have six roots, but when it cuts at fewer, this shows that some roots are equal or only imaginary. The sextic is solved by intersecting a circle with the trident, the analogue one genre higher of the circle-and-parabola construction of AT 465–467. The parameters are computed from the coefficients, so the rule is fully explicit, and the conditions for imaginary roots are again stated as failures of inscription or intersection. Double roots appear as fewer intersections, the same correspondence that grounded the method of normals (AT 418).

(AT 479–480) If tracing ACN by the motion of a parabola seems inconvenient, there are many other ways of describing it. For example, with the same quantities as before for AB and BL, and the same for BK as was set for the parabola's principal latus rectum, describe a semicircle KST with centre taken at will on BK so that it cuts AB somewhere, say at S; from its end T take toward K the line TV equal to BL; draw SV, then through A a line AC parallel to it and through S a line SC parallel to BK; the point C where these two parallels meet will be one of the points of the curve sought, and as many others as desired can be found likewise. The pointwise construction by ruler and compass is legitimate by the criterion of AT 411, since every point of the curve can be found. Offering an alternative to the moving parabola shows that the curve, not its mechanism, is what matters, and that one curve can be described in several ways, as AT 427 said of the ovals.

(AT 480–481) The demonstration of all this is easy. Applying the ruler AE with the parabola FD at the point C, as they can be applied together since C is on the curve described by their intersection, if CG is called y, GD will be yy/n, since the latus rectum n is to CG as CG to GD; subtracting DE, 2√v/pn, from GD gives yy/n − 2√v/pn for GE; then, since AB is to BE as CG to GE, AB being ½p, BE is py/2n − √v/ny. Likewise, supposing C found by the intersection of SC, parallel to BK, and AC, parallel to SV, SB, equal to CG, is y, and BK being equal to the latus rectum n, BT is yy/n, since KB is to BS as BS to BT; and TV being equal to BL, 2√v/pn, BV is yy/n − 2√v/pn, and as SB is to BV, so AB is to BE, which is therefore py/2n − √v/ny, as before; from which one sees that one and the same curve is described in the two ways. The two descriptions give the same relation between y and BE, which proves them equivalent. The proof is again by expressing one segment in two ways, the technique of AT 467–469.

(AT 481–482) Then, since BL and DE are equal, DL and BE are also, so adding LH, t/2n√v, to DL, py/2n − √v/ny, gives the whole DH, and subtracting GD, yy/n, gives GH, whose square, written in order, is a polynomial in y over nnyy. The construction's segments are expressed in y one by one. The calculation is set out so that the final comparison can be made term by term.

(AT 482) And wherever on this curve one imagines C, as toward N or Q, the square of the line between H and the foot of the perpendicular from C to BH can always be expressed in the same terms with the same signs. The uniformity over all points of the curve is what makes the construction a rule rather than a single solution. It mirrors the remark in Book I that all lines in the Pappus problem have expressions of the same form (AT 384).

(AT 482–483) Moreover, IH being m and LH t/2n√v, IL is √(mm + tt/4nnv), by the right angle IHL; and LP being √(s/nn + p√v/nn), IP or IC is √(mm + tt/4nnv − s/nn − p√v/nn), by the right angle IPL. Then, drawing CM perpendicular to IH, IM is the difference between IH and HM or CG, that is, between m and y, so its square is mm − 2my + yy, which subtracted from the square of IC leaves the square of CM, equal to the square of GH already found. The circle's radius is computed from the construction's data, and the square of CM is expressed in two ways, by the circle and by the curve. The two expressions will be equated in the next step.

(AT 483) Or, making this sum divided like the other by nnyy, and putting back the values of m and n, and multiplying both sums by nnyy, one has the equation y⁶ − py⁵ + qy⁴ − ry³ + syy − ty + v ∞ 0. Hence the lines CG, NR, QO and the like are the roots of this equation, which was to be demonstrated. The equation is recovered, so the construction is proved. The labour of the calculation is the price of the single general rule.

(AT 483–484) So, to find four mean proportionals between a and b, setting x for the first, the equation is x⁵ * * * * − a⁴b ∞ 0, or x⁶ * * * * − a⁴bx * ∞ 0; and making y − a ∞ x, one has y⁶ − 6ay⁵ + 15aay⁴ − 20a³y³ + 15a⁴yy − (6a⁵ + a⁴b)y + a⁶ + a⁵b ∞ 0. So one must take 3a for AB, √(6a³/√(aa + ab) + 6aa) for BK, the parabola's latus rectum n, and a/3n √(aa + ab) for DE or BL. The AT editors suggest a marginal title, "The finding of four mean proportionals," should be restored here. The problem that the mesolabe solved at the start of the book (AT 443) is now solved by the curve of lowest adequate genre, so the book closes the circle it opened, replacing the evident but inappropriate construction by the appropriate one. The preparatory substitution y − a ∞ x is the one prepared at AT 452.

(AT 484) Having described the curve ACN with these three measures, one must make LH, HI and LP equal to the expressions given; for the circle with centre I through the point P so found will cut the curve at two points C and N, and from the perpendiculars NR and CG drawn from them, if the smaller, NR, is subtracted from the larger, CG, the remainder will be x, the first of the four mean proportionals sought. Only two of the possible six intersections are real here, and the answer is their difference, which follows from the shift of AT 483. The example demonstrates the rule end to end, from equation to construction to answer.

(AT 484) In the same way it is easy to divide an angle into five equal parts, inscribe a figure of eleven or thirteen equal sides in a circle, and find infinitely many other examples of this rule. The quinsection of the angle and the regular hendecagon and tridecagon are classical problems beyond the reach of conics, and Descartes places them in the third genre without working them out. The regular heptagon and enneagon, which are solid, would have been cases for the parabola, and Gauss's later result on the constructible polygons (1796) gave the full answer for ruler and compass.

(AT 484–485) Yet in several of these examples the circle may cut the parabola of the second genre so obliquely that the point of intersection is hard to discern, so that the construction is not convenient in practice. This would be easy to remedy by composing other rules on the model of this one, as one can compose them in a thousand ways. Exactness in principle does not guarantee usability, the same concern that ruled the choice of lenses in the Optics (AT 185–186). The method generates alternatives, so the practical defect of one construction can be repaired by deriving another.

(AT 485) But his design is not to write a big book, and he tries rather to comprehend much in few words, as will perhaps be judged that he has done if one considers that, having reduced all problems of one genre to one construction, he has at once given the way to reduce them to infinitely many others, and so to solve each in infinitely many ways; and moreover that, having constructed all plane problems by cutting a straight line with a circle, all solid ones by cutting a parabola with a circle, and all those one degree more composite by cutting with a circle a line only one degree more composite than the parabola, one need only follow the same way to construct all those more composite to infinity. For in mathematical progressions, when one has the first two or three terms, it is not hard to find the others. And he hopes posterity will be grateful not only for what he has explained here, but also for what he has deliberately omitted, to leave them the pleasure of finding it. The Geometry ends as the Discourse did, with a hierarchy laid out and its continuation left to others (AT 72–73): circle and line for plane problems, circle and parabola for solid ones, circle and trident for the next genre, and an inductive "progression" for the rest. The claim that the first terms determine the series is a claim about the method's power to generalise, and it is also an admission that the general case has not been worked out. The closing hope for posterity's gratitude for the omissions returns to the treatise's pedagogy of invention, which its first readers found its most frustrating feature and its admirers its most characteristic.
